Simplicial volumes in Bruhat-Tits buildings of split classical type
Xu Gao

Abstract
In a Bruhat-Tits building of split classical type (that is, of type A n A_{n} , B n B_{n} , C n C_{n} , D n D_{n} , and any combination of them) over a local field, the simplicial volume counts the vertices within the given simplicial distance from a special vertex. This paper aims to study the asymptotic growth of the simplicial volume. A formula of the simplicial volume is deduced from the theory of concave functions. Then the dominant term in its asymptotic growth is found using the theory of q q -exponential polynomials developed in this paper.



§ 1  Introduction

Buildings
are important geometric/combinatorial objects and were first introduced by Jacques Tits (see
[
15
,
3
]
) in the 1950s-1960s to study semisimple groups (more generally, reductive groups) over arbitrary fields. Later, François Bruhat and Jacques Tits developed a specialized variant to study reductive groups over a non-Archimedean valued field (see
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
]
for the original sources).

A Bruhat-Tits building over a local field is, in particular, a polysimplicial complex. From the viewpoint of incidence geometry, we have a
simplicial distance
on it.
For any two vertices
x
and
y
on a Bruhat-Tits building, a
path
from
x
to
y
is a sequence of adjacent vertices
x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{l}
with
x_{0}=x
and
x_{l}=y
.
The number
l
is called the
length
of the path.
Then the
simplicial distance
between
x
and
y
is the minimum length of a path from
x
to
y
, and we denote it by
d(x,y)
.

Let
\operatorname{{B}}
be a Bruhat-Tits building and
x
a vertex in it.
The
simplicial ball with center
x
and radius
r
is the set of all vertices with simplicial distance at most
r
from
x
:

B(x,r):=\left\{y\text{ is a vertex in $\operatorname{{B}}$}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}d(x,y)\leqslant r\right\}.

The
simplicial sphere with center
x
and radius
r
is the set of all vertices with simplicial distance exactly
r
from
x
:

\partial(x,r):=\left\{y\text{ is a vertex in $\operatorname{{B}}$}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}d(x,y)=r\right\}.

In a Bruhat-Tits building, a vertex is either special or adjacent to a special one.
If two vertices
x
and
y
are adjacent, then we have

B(x,r-1)\subseteq B(y,r)\subseteq B(x,r+1).

In this sense, we may focus on special vertices.

In the rest of the paper,
o
will be a fixed special vertex.
The set
B(o,r)
will be denoted by
B(r)
for short, and its cardinality will be denoted by
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
.
Likewise, the set
\partial(o,r)
and its cardinality will be denoted by
\partial(r)
and
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
respectively.
The functions
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
are called
the
simplicial volume
and the
simplicial surface area
in
\operatorname{{B}}
respectively.

Before moving on, let me explain what does
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
count in the case where
\operatorname{{B}}
is of split type
A_{n}
.
Let
V
be a vector space of dimension
n+1
over the ground local field
K
.
Then vertices in
\operatorname{{B}}
can be interpreted as
homothetic
classes of
lattices
in
V
(see, e.g.
[
12
]
*2.22
[
4
]
*§10.2
[
6
]
*1.7).
Fix a lattice
L_{0}
so that its homothetic class
[L_{0}]
is taken to be the reference point
o
.
Then the quantity
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
counts, up to homotheties, the lattices
L
between
L_{0}
and
\varpi^{r}L_{0}
(see
[
14
]
*2.1.1), where
\varpi
is any uniformizer of
K
.

The purpose of this paper is to analyze the asymptotic growths of the simplicial volume and the simplicial surface area.
Note that

\partial(r)=B(r)\setminus B(r-1).

Therefore, for sufficiently large
r
, we have

C_{1}\cdot\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\leqslant\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)\leqslant C_{2}\cdot\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right),

where
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
are positive constants.
We use the asymptotic notation
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
to denote this fact.

One of the main theorems in this paper is the following.

1.1
.

Let
{B}
be an irreducible Bruhat-Tits building of split classical type over a local field
K
with residue cardinality
q
.
Then the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it have the following asymptotic dominant relation:

\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\asymp r^{\varepsilon(n)}q^{\pi(n)r},

where
\varepsilon(n)
and
\pi(n)
are given in the following table.

Split type of
{B}

\varepsilon(n)

\pi(n)

A_{n}
(
n
is odd)

0

(\frac{n+1}{2})^{2}

A_{n}
(
n
is even)

1

\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{2}+1)

B_{n}
(
n=3
)

0

5

B_{n}
(
n\geqslant 4
)

0

\frac{n^{2}}{2}

C_{n}
(
n\geqslant 2
)

0

\frac{n(n+1)}{2}

D_{n}
(
n=4
)

2

6

D_{n}
(
n\geqslant 5
)

1

\frac{n(n-1)}{2}

Table 1
:

This theorem talks about
irreducible
Bruhat-Tits buildings of
split classical types
only.
But we will see in
Section

4.1
that asymptotic results for general Bruhat-Tits buildings of split classical types can be deduced from the irreducible ones.

.

When the Bruhat-Tits building
{B}
is of split type
A_{n}
, this asymptotic dominant relation is given in
[
14
]
*2.1.2. We refer to
[
14
]
for an application of it.

In order to do asymptotic analysis, we need formulas for the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in terms of the root system
\Phi
and the ground local field
K
. This is achieved by the following theorem.

1.2
.

Let
\operatorname{{B}}
be a Bruhat-Tits building of split type
\Phi
over a local field
K
with residue cardinality
q
. Then the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it can be computed by the following formulas:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\right)}}\sum_{x\in B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\prod_{a(x)>0}q^{\lceil a(x)\rceil},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\right)}}\sum_{x\in\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\prod_{a(x)>0}q^{\lceil a(x)\rceil},

where

•

\lceil\>\cdot\>\rceil
is the ceiling function,

•

\Delta
is a basis of the root system
\Phi
,

•

\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}
is the Poincaré polynomial associated to the pair
(\Phi,I)
,

•

\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
is a Weyl chamber of
\Phi
,

•

and the index sets
B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
(resp.
\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
) consists of the vertices in
\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
having type
I
with simplicial distance at most
r
(resp. exactly
r
) from
o
.

In order to apply the formulas, we need to find explicit descriptions of the index sets
B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
and
\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
. The key step is the following characterization of the simplicial distance.

1.3
.

Let
\operatorname{{A}}
be an irreducible affine apartment of split classical type
\Phi
and
o
a fixed special vertex in it. Let
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
be a Weyl chamber of
\Phi
and
a_{0}
the highest root relative to
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
.
Then, for a vertex
x
in
\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
, we have:

d(x,o)\leqslant r\iff a_{0}(x-o)\leqslant r.

.

While the formulas in
Theorem

1.2
apply to general Bruhat-Tits building of split type,
Theorem

1.3
only holds when
\Phi
is of classical type.
This is the main reason why this paper focuses on classical type.

Once the explicit descriptions of the index sets are obtained, we can immediately see that each formula in
Theorem

1.2
can be expanded into a finite linear combination of multi-summations of the form

\sum_{c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}}q^{L(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})+e(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})},

where
L(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})
is a linear form of the variables
c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}
and
e(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})
is a parity function of
c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}
.
In order to handle such multi-summations, the notion of
(super)
q
-exponential polynomials
is introduced and studied.

Then we are able to prove
Theorem

1.1
and the following improvement.

1.4
.

Notations are as in
Theorem

1.1
.

(i)

Suppose
{B}
is of split type
A_{n}
,
C_{n}
,
B_{3}
, or
D_{4}
. Then the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it has the following asymptotic growth as
r\to\infty
:

\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)\sim\tilde{C}(n)\cdot r^{\varepsilon(n)}q^{\pi(n)r},

where
\tilde{C}(n)
is a positive number that is a rational function of
q
.
Similarly, the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
has the following asymptotic growth as
r\to\infty
:

\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\sim{C}(n)\cdot r^{\varepsilon(n)}q^{\pi(n)r},

where
{C}(n)
is a positive number that is a rational function of
q
.

(ii)

Suppose
{B}
is of split type
B_{n}
(
n\geqslant 4
) or
D_{n}
(
n\geqslant 5
). Then the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it has the following asymptotic growth as
r\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(2r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}_{0}(n)\cdot r^{\varepsilon(n)}q^{2\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(2r+1\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}_{1}(n)\cdot r^{\varepsilon(n)}q^{2\pi(n)r},

where
\tilde{C}_{0}(n)
and
\tilde{C}_{1}(n)
are positive numbers that are rational functions of
q
.
Similarly, the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in
\operatorname{{B}}
has the following asymptotic growth as
r\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(2r\right)

\displaystyle\sim{C}_{0}(n)\cdot r^{\varepsilon(n)}q^{2\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(2r+1\right)

\displaystyle\sim{C}_{1}(n)\cdot r^{\varepsilon(n)}q^{2\pi(n)r},

where
{C}_{0}(n)
and
{C}_{1}(n)
are positive numbers that are rational functions of
q
.

Indeed, we will prove stronger results (see
Theorems

6.1
,
7.1
,
7.2
,
8.1
, and
8.2
) and give explicit formulas for the involved constants.

Plan

This paper is organized as follows.

In
Section

2
, we will review the theory of Bruhat-Tits buildings and fix conventions.

In
Section

3
, we will prove the formulas of the simplicial volume and the simplicity surface area shown in
Theorem

1.2
.

In
Section

4
, we will deduce explicit descriptions of the index sets
B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
and
\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
from well-known results on root systems.

In
Section

5
, we will introduce the notion of
(super)
q
-exponential polynomials
and prepare for the second half of this paper.

In
Sections

6
,
7
,
8
, and
9
, we will study the asymptotic behaviors of the simplicial volume and simplicity surface area in Bruhat-Tits buildings of split type
A_{n}
,
B_{n}
,
C_{n}
, and
D_{n}
.

§ 2  Preliminaries and notations

This section aims to explain the terminology and notation used in this paper.

2.1
Buildings and apartments

Let us begin with general notions of (Euclidean) buildings and apartments. We refer to
[
13
]
and
[
3
]
*chap.V, §3 for details.

The starting point is a Euclidean affine space
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
Its associated vector space is denoted by
\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-6.0mu}{}{\mathbb{A}}}
.
A
reflection
on
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
is an affine isometry whose fixed-point set is a hyperplane.
Note that the reflections
r_{H}
on
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
and the hyperplanes
H
in
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
are one-one corresponding to each other.
Let
W
be a group of affine isometries generated by reflections and
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
the group of their vectorial parts.
Then
W
is called a
reflection group
if
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
is finite.

2.1.1
.

A
(Euclidean) apartment

\operatorname{{A}}
is a Euclidean affine space
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
equipped with a reflection group
W
(called its
Weyl group
) on it.

An apartment
\operatorname{{A}}
is
irreducible
(resp.
essential
,
trivial
, etc.), and its Weyl group
W
is said to be so if
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
acts irreducibly (resp. essentially, trivially, etc.) on
\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-6.0mu}{}{\mathbb{A}}}
.

The hyperplanes corresponding to the reflections in the Weyl group
W
are called the
walls
of the apartment
\operatorname{{A}}
.
They decompose the Euclidean space
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
into cells, called
facets
. We say a facet
F

covers
another facet
F^{\prime}
, or
F^{\prime}
is a
face
of
F
, if the closure of
F
contains
F^{\prime}
.
In this way, we can view an apartment
\operatorname{{A}}
as a
polysimplicial complex
, that is a cellular complex which is a product of simplicial complexes.

2.1.2
.

Let
\operatorname{{A}}
be an apartment.
A
(Euclidean) building of type
\operatorname{{A}}
is a polysimplicial complex
\operatorname{{B}}
equipped with a family
\operatorname{\mathcal{A}}
of subcomplexes, such that the following axioms are satisfied.

EB0.

Every
A\in\operatorname{\mathcal{A}}
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{{A}}
.

EB1.

For any two cells
F
and
F^{\prime}
, there is an
A\in\operatorname{\mathcal{A}}
containing them.

EB2.

If
A
and
A^{\prime}
are two members of
\operatorname{\mathcal{A}}
containing both
F
and
F^{\prime}
, then there is an isomorphism between
A
and
A^{\prime}
fixing
F
and
F^{\prime}
pointwise.

Members of
\operatorname{\mathcal{A}}
are called
apartments in
\operatorname{{B}}
. Cells in
\operatorname{{B}}
are called
facets
.
The Weyl group
W
of
\operatorname{{A}}
is also called the
Weyl group
of
\operatorname{{B}}
.

.

An apartment
\operatorname{{A}}
is a building, where the family
\operatorname{\mathcal{A}}
consists of exactly one member, namely
\operatorname{{A}}
itself.

A building
\operatorname{{B}}
of type
\operatorname{{A}}
is
irreducible
(resp.
essential
,
trivial
, etc.) if
\operatorname{{A}}
is.
From now on,
all buildings are assumed to be essential
.
Then the minimal facets have dimension
0
and are called
vertices
.
The set of vertices is denoted by
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
.

The kernel of
W\to\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
is called the
translation group
. It is either discrete or dense.
From now on,
all translation groups are assumed to be discrete
.
Then the maximal facets are the connected components of the complement of the union of all walls.
They are called
chambers
if the translation group is trivial and
alcoves
if not.

If
W\cong\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
, we say
W
is
linear
and the building is a
vectorial building
.
Otherwise, we say
W
is
affine
and the building is an
affine building
.
Note that the combinatorial information (the family of apartments and the poset of facets) in a vectorial building can be read from its unit sphere.
We use the term
spherical building
to refer to any cellular complex (equipped with a family of subcomplexes) encoding the same combinatorial information as a vectorial building.

Any apartment
\operatorname{{A}}=(\operatorname{\mathbb{A}},W)
admits a vectorial apartment
(\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-6.0mu}{}{\mathbb{A}}},\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}})
, denoted by
\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-7.0mu}{}{{A}}}
.
However, there is no similar construction for affine buildings since the underlying space of a building
\operatorname{{B}}
is not necessarily an affine space.
Instead, we can consider the subcomplex of facets covering a given point
x
.
This complex, equipped with the family of subcomplexes inheriting from
\operatorname{{B}}
, is not necessarily a Euclidean building but a spherical building.
This spherical building is called the
spherical building at
x
, denoted by
\operatorname{{B}}_{x}
.
A point
x\in\operatorname{{B}}
is called
special
if
\operatorname{{B}}_{x}
encodes the same combinatorial information as
\operatorname{\prescript{v\!}{}{{B}}}
.
This is the case if and only if the stabilizer
W_{x}
of
x
in the Weyl group
W
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
through the composition
W_{x}\hookrightarrow W\to\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
.
It is clear that a special point must be a vertex.

2.2
Affine roots

To explain what is
a building of split classical type
, we discuss various notions of
roots
in this subsection. We refer to
[
4
]
*§1 and
[
3
]
*chap.VI, §1-2 for details.

Let
\operatorname{{A}}=(\operatorname{\mathbb{A}},W)
be an apartment.
An
affine root
in it is a closed half-space
\alpha
of
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
bounded by a wall. This wall is denoted by
\partial\alpha
.
The set of all affine roots is called the
affine root system of
\operatorname{{A}}
and is denoted by
\Sigma
.
It is clear that the affine root system
\Sigma
determines the apartment
\operatorname{{A}}
: the Weyl group
W
is generated by the reflections
r_{\alpha}
with respect to the walls
\partial\alpha
of affine roots
\alpha\in\Sigma
.
Then we say
(\operatorname{\mathbb{A}},\Sigma)
is an apartment by an abuse of language.

Let
f
be an affine function on
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
We use the notation
\alpha_{f}
to denote the closed half-space
defined
by it:

\alpha_{f}:=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}f(x)\geqslant 0\right\}.

Up to a positive scale, the affine function
f
defining a given closed half-space is unique.
This allows us to use affine functions to talk about affine roots.

The affine root system of the vectorial apartment
\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-7.0mu}{}{{A}}}
of
\operatorname{{A}}
is called its
vectorial root system
and is denoted by
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Sigma}}
.
Members of
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Sigma}}
are called
vectorial roots
.
For an affine root
\alpha
in
\operatorname{{A}}
, there is a unique vectorial root
\prescript{v\!}{}{\alpha}\in\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Sigma}}
associated to it: if
f
is an affine function defining
\alpha
, then
\prescript{v\!}{}{\alpha}
is defined by the vectorial part
\prescript{v\mkern-6.0mu}{}{f}
of
f
.
This vectorial root is called the
vectorial part
of
\alpha
.

2.2.1
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
be a Euclidean vector space and
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}^{\ast}
its dual space identified with
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
through the inner product.
A finite spanning subset
\Phi\subseteq\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}^{\ast}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
is called a
root system
on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
if

RS1.

for any
a\in\Phi
, the reflection
r_{a}
with respect to
\operatorname{Ker}\left(a\right)
leaves
\Phi
stable;

RS2.

for any
a,b\in\Phi
,
r_{a}(b)-b\in\mathbb{Z}a
.

A root system
\Phi
is
reduced
if

RS3.

for any
a\in\Phi
,
\mathbb{R}a\cap\Phi=\left\{\pm a\right\}
.

Elements of
{\Phi}
are called
roots
in
\Phi
.
For a root
a\in\Phi
, the vector
a^{\vee}
orthogonal to
\operatorname{Ker}\left(a\right)
satisfying
a(a^{\vee})=2
is called its
coroot
.
The coroots form a root system
{\Phi^{\vee}}
on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}^{\ast}
.
A subset
\Psi\subseteq\Phi
is called a
root subsystem
if for any
a\in\Psi
,
r_{a}(\Psi)=\Psi
, and is said to be
closed
if for any
a,b\in\Psi
such that
a+b
is a root,
a+b\in\Psi
.

The following construction shows the relation between a vectorial root system and a root system.

2.2.2
.

Let
(\Phi,\operatorname{\mathbb{V}})
be a root system with its underlying Euclidean vector space.
The reflections
(r_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
generates a linear reflection group
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}\left(\Phi\right)
on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
, called the
Weyl group
of this root system.
Then
(\operatorname{\mathbb{V}},\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}\left(\Phi\right))
is a vectorial apartment whose vectorial root system
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Sigma}}
is
\left\{\prescript{v\!}{}{\alpha}_{a}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a\in\Phi\right\}
, where
\prescript{v\!}{}{\alpha}_{a}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a(\mathbf{v})\geqslant 0\right\}
.

Note that: 1, the map
\Phi\to\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Sigma}}\colon a\mapsto\prescript{v\!}{}{\alpha}_{a}
is injective if and only if
\Phi
is reduced; 2, non-isomorphic root systems may give isomorphic vectorial root systems.

Let
\operatorname{{A}}=(\operatorname{\mathbb{A}},\Sigma)
be an affine apartment and
\Phi
a root system on
\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-6.0mu}{}{\mathbb{A}}}
.
An
échelonnage
of
\Phi
by
\Sigma
is a correspondence
\operatorname{{E}}\subseteq\Phi\times\Sigma
such that
(a,\alpha)\in\operatorname{{E}}
implies
\prescript{v\!}{}{\alpha}_{a}=\prescript{v\!}{}{\alpha}
and that
\operatorname{{E}}
is stable under the obvious action of
W
.
Note that if such an échelonnage exists, then the Weyl group of
\Phi
is the same as
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
. So an échelonnage
\operatorname{{E}}
tells us how the affine root system
\Sigma
is related to a root system
\Phi
.

Now, suppose we have an échelonnage
\operatorname{{E}}
. Then, for any
(a,\alpha)\in\operatorname{{E}}
, there is a unique affine function
f
on
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
defining
\alpha
and having vectorial part
a
.
If we fix a reference point
o
in
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
, this function can be written as
f(x)=a(x-o)+k
for some
k\in\mathbb{R}
.
In this sense, we say
f
(and
\alpha
) has
expression
a+k
.
If the reference point
o
is implied, we simply use
a+k
to denote this affine function.
Let
\Gamma_{a}:=\left\{k\in\mathbb{R}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\alpha_{a+k}\in\Sigma\right\}
.
Then an échelonnage
\operatorname{{E}}
of
\Phi
by
\Sigma
is the same datum as an assignment
a\in\Phi\mapsto\Gamma_{a}
. Such a datum characterizes the extra information of a Bruhat-Tits building (ref.
Section

2.5
) respecting its valuations.
Hence, we make the following definition.

2.2.3
.

An
affine building of type
\operatorname{{E}}
is an affine building equipped with the échelonnage
\operatorname{{E}}
.

Let
x
be a point in
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
Let
\Sigma_{x}
be the set of affine roots
\alpha
such that
x\in\partial\alpha
.
Then, through the échelonnage
\operatorname{{E}}
, it corresponds to the following subset of
\Phi
:

\Phi_{x}:=\left\{a\in\Phi\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}(a,\alpha)\in\operatorname{{E}}\text{ for some }\alpha\in\Sigma_{x}\right\}.

This subset a closed root subsystem of
\Phi
and is called the
root subsystem of
\Phi
at
x
.
It is clear that
x
is special if and only if
\Phi_{x}=\Phi
.

2.2.4
.

Let
(\Phi,\operatorname{\mathbb{V}})
be a reduced root system with its underlying Euclidean vector space and
\Gamma
a discrete subgroup of
(\mathbb{R},+)
.
Let
\Sigma:=\left\{\alpha_{a+k}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a\in\Phi,k\in\Gamma\right\}
. Then it is an affine root system on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
, viewed as a Euclidean affine space.
We thus obtain an affine apartment
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi,\Gamma\right)
.
We simply denote it by
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi\right)
if the discrete subgroup
\Gamma
is implied.
Then there is an obvious échelonnage:

\operatorname{{E}}\left(\Phi\right):=\left\{(a,\alpha_{a+k})\in\Phi\times\Sigma\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}k\in\Gamma\right\}.

An affine building of type
\operatorname{{E}}\left(\Phi\right)
is also said to be
of split type
\Phi
.

2.3
Types and colors

The purpose of is subsection is to explain the usage of the terms
type
and
color
in this paper, their relation to the root system, and relevant conventions.

Let
\operatorname{{A}}=(\operatorname{\mathbb{A}},W)
be an apartment.
A
type function
(resp.
cotype function
) on
\operatorname{{A}}
is a strictly order-reversing (resp. order-preserving) map
\tau
from the complex of facets to a power set
(\operatorname{{P}}\left(\operatorname{\mathfrak{I}}\right),\subseteq)
mapping maximal facets to
\emptyset
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathfrak{I}}
) and is
W
-stable
: for any facet
F
and any
w\in W
,
\tau(F)=\tau(w.F)
.

The closure
\overline{C}
of a maximal facet
C
in
\operatorname{{A}}
is a fundamental domain of
W
in
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
(see e.g.
[
3
]
*chap.V, §3, no.3, thm.2).
Hence, any facet is transformed by
W
to a unique face of
C
.
Let
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}_{C}
be the set of
panels
, namely maximal proper faces of
C
. Then a type function is determined by a bijection
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}_{C}\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{I}}
.
Similarly, let
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{C}
be the set of
vertices
covered by
C
. Then a cotype function is determined by a bijection
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{C}\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{I}}
.

2.3.1
[
3
]
*chap.VI, §1.

Let
(\Phi,\operatorname{\mathbb{V}})
be a root system with its underlying Euclidean vector space.
A closed subset
\Phi^{+}
of
\Phi
is called a
system of positive roots
if
\Phi\setminus\Phi^{+}=-\Phi^{+}
.
The set
-\Phi^{+}
is called the system of
negative roots
and is denoted by
\Phi^{-}
.
A positive root is called a
simple root
if it cannot be written as the sum of two positive roots.
The set
\Delta
of simple roots forms a
basis
of
\Phi
in the sense that any root is a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of simple roots with either all non-negative or all non-positive coefficients.

Let
\Delta
be a basis of
\Phi
.
Then the following set is a vectorial chamber:

\prescript{v\!}{}{C}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a(\mathbf{v})>0\text{ for all }a\in\Delta\right\}.

It is called the
Weyl chamber
associated to
\Delta
.
Conversely, let
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
be a vectorial chamber.
Then, for any
\mathbf{v}\in\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
, consider the following subset of
\Phi
:

\Phi^{+}:=\left\{a\in\Phi\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a(\mathbf{v})>0\right\}.

It forms a system of positive roots and is independent of the choice of
\mathbf{v}
. Then one can obtain a basis
\Delta
by taking the simple roots.
There is a more geometric description: their null-sets
\left\{\operatorname{Ker}\left(a\right)\right\}_{a\in\Delta}
are precisely the walls enclosing
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
.

Now, we have a bijection
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}_{\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}\to\Delta
, mapping each panel
P
to the simple root
a\in\Delta
whose null-set
\operatorname{Ker}\left(a\right)
contains
P
.
This defines a type function, for which a
type
is a subset
I
of
\Delta
.
Let
\Phi_{I}
denote the root subsystem of
\Phi
generated by
I
.
Let
\Psi_{I}=\Phi_{I}\cup\Phi^{+}
.
Then subset
\Psi_{I}
has the property that
\Psi_{I}\cup(-\Psi_{I})=\Phi
and is closed.
Such a subset of
\Phi
is said to be
parabolic
.
Then we have a bijection from the parabolic subsets of
\Phi
containing
\Phi^{+}
to the types.

2.3.2
.

Given a basis
\Delta=\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\right\}
, a
type
is a subset of
\Delta
, and is identified with a subset of
\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\}
.
For a type
I
of
\Delta
,
we use
t_{I}
to denote the cardinality of
\Delta\setminus I
and
\ell_{i}(I)
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant t_{I}
) the
i
-th index in
\Delta\setminus I
.
We use the convention that
\ell_{0}=0
.
We will omit
I
if there is no ambiguity.

2.3.3
[
3
]
*chap.VI, §1-2.

Let
(\Phi,\operatorname{\mathbb{V}})
be a reduced root system with its underlying Euclidean vector space. Suppose
\Phi
is
irreducible
, namely it cannot be written as the union of two proper subsets such that they are orthogonal to each other.
Let
(\prescript{v\!}{}{C},\Phi^{+},\Delta)
be a triple of a Weyl chamber, a system of positive roots, and a basis of
\Phi
as in
2.3.1
.
Then there is a unique root
a_{0}
having the largest coefficients. This
a_{0}
is called the
highest root
relative to this triple.

Let
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi\right)
be the affine apartment of split type
\Phi
and fix a special vertex
o
in it as the reference point.
Suppose
\Delta=\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\right\}
.
Let
\alpha_{i}:=\alpha_{a_{i}+0}
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
) and
\alpha_{0}:=\alpha_{-a_{0}+}
, where
\alpha_{-a_{0}+}
is the intersection of all
\alpha_{-a_{0}+k}
with
k\in\Gamma
and
k>0
.
Then
\left\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\right\}
form a
basis

\widetilde{\Delta}
of the affine root system
\Sigma
in the sense that the intersection
\operatorname{{D}}
of its members is a fundamental domain of
W
in
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
The interior
C
of
\operatorname{{D}}
is an alcove, called the
fundamental alcove
associated to the basis
\widetilde{\Delta}
.
Conversely, let
C
be an alcove in
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi\right)
.
Then the affine roots containing
C
whose boundary avoids exactly one extreme point of
C
form a basis of
\Sigma
.

Now, we have a bijection
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{C}\to\widetilde{\Delta}
, mapping each vertex
v
to the affine root
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
whose boundary avoids
v
.
This defines a cotype function.

2.3.4
.

Given a basis
\Delta=\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\right\}
of an irreducible
\Phi
, the highest root relative to it is denoted by
a_{0}
and the coefficients are denoted by
h_{1},\cdots,h_{n}
, namely

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:highest_{r}oot}a_{0}=h_{1}a_{1}+\cdots+h_{n}a_{n}.

(2.3.1)

Given a basis
\widetilde{\Delta}=\left\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\right\}
of
\Sigma
, a
color
of a vertex
v
is the index
i
(
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
) such that
v
is mapped to
\alpha_{i}
through the cotype function in
2.3.3
.

2.4
Reductive groups

In this subsection, we will review the notion of reductive groups and recall how to exhibit a root system in a reductive group.
Then we introduce the essential of Tits buildings.
We refer to
[
11
]
for reductive groups over a field and to
[
9
]
for reductive group schemes over a general base.

In what follows,
{K}
is a field and
K^{\rm a}
is an algebraic closure of it.

2.4.1
.

A
group scheme
means a group object in the category of schemes. An
algebraic group
(defined over
K
) is then a group scheme of finite type over
K
.

We will use bold letters like
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
to denote algebraic groups defined over
K
.
For any
K
-algebra
R
, the group scheme obtained by base change
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\otimes_{K}R
is denoted by
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{R}
and the group of
R
-points is denoted by
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\left(R\right)
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\left(K\right)
is simply denoted by
G
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{R}\left(R\right)\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\left(R\right)
is simply denoted by
G_{R}
.

2.4.2
.

A
linear algebraic group
is a closed algebraic subgroup of the
general linear group

\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}\left(V\right)
for some finite-dimensional vector space
V
over
K
.
Linear algebraic groups are precisely the affine algebraic groups (see e.g.
[
11
]
*1.43 and 4.10).

2.4.3
.

An algebraic group is
solvable
if it admits a subnormal series with Abelian factors.
Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
be a smooth connected linear algebraic group.
Then there is a largest smooth connected solvable norm subgroup

\operatorname{{R}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\right)
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
(see e.g.
[
11
]
*6.44).
It is called the
radical
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
.

2.4.4
.

An algebraic group is
unipotent
if every nonzero linear representation of it has a nonzero fixed vector.
Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
be a smooth connected linear algebraic group.
Then there is a largest smooth connected unipotent norm subgroup

\operatorname{{R}}_{u}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\right)
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
(see e.g.
[
11
]
*6.46).
It is called the
unipotent radical
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
.

2.4.5
.

An algebraic group
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
is
reductive
(resp.
semisimple
) if its
geometric unipotent radical

\operatorname{{R}}_{u}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{K^{\rm a}}\right)
(resp.
geometric radical

\operatorname{{R}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{K^{\rm a}}\right)
) is trivial.

2.4.6
.

A
split reductive group
is a pair
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
of a reductive group and a split maximal torus in it.
If such a pair exists, we say
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
is
splittable
.

Now, we show how to exhibit a root system in a split reductive group.

2.4.7
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
be a smooth connected linear algebraic group and
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
a split torus in it.
Then
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
acts diagonalizably (via the adjoint representation) on the Lie algebra
\operatorname{\mathfrak{g}}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
. Therefore, we have a decomposition:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:root_{s}pace_{d}ecom}\operatorname{\mathfrak{g}}=\bigoplus_{a\in\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)}\operatorname{\mathfrak{g}}_{a},

(2.4.1)

where:

\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
is the
split character group
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
, namely
\operatorname{Hom}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}},\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}_{\rm m}\right)
, and
each
\operatorname{\mathfrak{g}}_{a}
is the subspace of
\operatorname{\mathfrak{g}}
on which
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
acts through a character
a\in\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
.
If
\operatorname{\mathfrak{g}}_{a}\neq 0
, then
a
is called a
root
.
The set of all roots is denoted by
\operatorname{\Phi}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
and is called the
root system
of the pair
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
.
For a root
a\in\operatorname{\Phi}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
, the set
(a)
of all positive real multiples of it is called its
radical ray
.
The set of all radical rays is denoted by
\operatorname{\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Phi}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}^{\vee}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
be the
split cocharacter group
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
, namely
\operatorname{Hom}\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}_{\rm m},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
.
Then the normalizer
\operatorname{\mathsf{N}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{N}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
acts on
\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}^{\vee}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
by conjugations.
Since the centralizer
\operatorname{\mathsf{Z}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Z}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
acts trivially on them, we obtain actions of
\operatorname{\mathsf{N}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{Z}}
on
\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}^{\vee}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
.
The quotient
\operatorname{\mathsf{N}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{Z}}
is denoted by
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
and is called the
Weyl group
of the pair
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
.

From now on, we will omit
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
from notations such as
\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
,
\operatorname{\Phi}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
,
\operatorname{\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Phi}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}^{\vee}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
,
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
, and any others defined later if the pair
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
is implied.

2.4.8
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
be a smooth connected linear algebraic group and
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
a split torus in it.
Then the
root subgroup

\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{(a)}
associated to a radical ray
(a)
is the largest connected closed subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
such that: 1, it is normalized by
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
; 2, any characters appearing in the adjoint representation of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
on the Lie algebra of
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{(a)}
belongs to
(a)
.
We refer to
[
11
]
*16.i for a construction of such groups.

Let
(a)\in\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Phi}}
such that
-(a)\in\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Phi}}
.
Then the
Levi subgroup

\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{(a)}
(reps.
derived Levi subgroup

\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}^{(a)}
) associated to
(a)
is the subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
generated by
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{(a)}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{-(a)}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
(resp. by
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{(a)}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{-(a)}
).
Then we have open immersions:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{(a)}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{-(a)}\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}^{(a)}

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{-(a)}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{(a)}\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{(a)}.

Moreover, there is a rational map
\beta_{(a)}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{(a)}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{-(a)}\dashrightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{-(a)}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{(a)}
compatible with them. We refer to
[
5
]
*1.1.10 for this fact.

If
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
be a split reductive group, then
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{(a)},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
is a split reductive group of semisimple rank one and
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}^{(a)}
is the derived group of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{(a)}
(see. e.g.
[
11
]
*21.c or
[
5
]
*§1.1).
Since
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}^{(a)}
is one-dimensional, there is a unique cocharacter
a^{\vee}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}^{\vee}
parameterizing it such that
a\circ a^{\vee}=2
.
This cocharacter is called the
coroot associated to
a
.
Note that the rational map
\beta_{(a)}
can be expressed using
a^{\vee}
(see e.g.
[
5
]
*3.2.7.(3) or
[
9
]
*XX, 2.1.(F)).

The set of coroots is called the
coroot system
of
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
and is denoted by
\operatorname{\Phi}^{\vee}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
.
Then the underlying space of
\Phi
is the subspace of
\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}
spanned by
\operatorname{\Phi}^{\vee}
and equipped with a
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
-invariant inner product.
It is called the
coroot space
of
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
and is denoted by
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
.
With the help of
\beta_{(a)}
, we can see that
\Phi
is a
reduced
root system on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
and that the Weyl group
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
acts on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
as a reflection group and is identified with the Weyl group of
\Phi
.
In this way, we obtain a vectorial apartment
\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-7.0mu}{}{{A}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)=(\operatorname{\mathbb{V}},\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}})
.

.

Since we can identify a radical ray
(a)\in\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\Phi}}
with a unique root
a\in\Phi
, we denote
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{(a)}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{(a)}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}^{(a)}
, and
\beta_{(a)}
by
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{a}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}_{a}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}^{a}
, and
\beta_{a}
respectively.

2.4.9
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
be a smooth connected linear algebraic group.
Then a
parabolic subgroup
of it is a smooth subgroup
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}
such that
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}
is a complete variety.
A subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
is
Borel
if it is smooth, connected, solvable, and parabolic.

2.4.10
[
11
]
*21.d and 21.i.

Let
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
be a split reductive group.
Then there are natural one-to-one correspondences between the following sets:

(a)

The set of Borel subgroups
\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
containing
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
.

(b)

The set of Weyl chambers
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
in the vectorial apartment
\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-7.0mu}{}{{A}}}
.

(c)

The set of systems of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
in the root system
\Phi
.

(d)

The set of bases
\Delta
of
\Phi
.

The Weyl group
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
acts simply transitively on each set.
Moreover, after choosing a quadruple
(\operatorname{\mathsf{B}},\prescript{v\!}{}{C},\Phi^{+},\Delta)
, we have the following isomorphic posets.

(a)

The poset of parabolic subgroups
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
containing
\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}
.

(b)

The poset of faces
\prescript{v\!}{}{F}
of the Weyl chamber
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
.

(c)

The poset of parabolic subsets
\Psi
of
\Phi
containing
\Phi^{+}
.

(d)

The poset of subsets
I
of
\Delta
.

2.4.11
.

If
(\operatorname{\mathsf{P}},\prescript{v\!}{}{F},\Psi,I)
is a quadruple as above, then we say that each of them
have type
I
, where
I
is identified as a subset of
\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\}
in
Convention

2.3.2
.

2.1
[
13
]
*§10
[
15
]
*§5.

Let
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
be a split reductive group. Then there is a unique (up to unique isomorphism)
G
-set

\operatorname{\prescript{v\!}{}{{B}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\right)

containing
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
and satisfying the following:

(ii)

\operatorname{\prescript{v\!}{}{{B}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\right)=\bigcup_{g\in G}g.\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
;

(ii)

N
stabilizes
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
and acts on it through
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
;

(ii)

for every
a\in\Phi
, the fixator of
\prescript{v\!}{}{\alpha}_{a}
is
T\cdot U_{a}
.

Then
\operatorname{\prescript{v\!}{}{{B}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\right)
is a building of type
\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-7.0mu}{}{{A}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
, called the
Tits building
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
.
Note that the apartment structure on
g.\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
is
\operatorname{\prescript{v\mkern-7.0mu}{}{{A}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}^{g}\right)
, where
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}^{g}
is the conjugate of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
by
g
.
Since split maximal tori of a splittable reductive group
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
are conjugate to each other (see e.g.
[
11
]
*17.105),
\operatorname{\prescript{v\!}{}{{B}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\right)
is independent of the choice of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
.
Moreover,
Proposition

2.4.10
together with the isogeny and existence theorem (see e.g.
[
11
]
*23.56) implies that the Tits building
\operatorname{\prescript{v\!}{}{{B}}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\right)
depends only on the root system
\Phi
and the ground field
K
.

2.5
Bruhat-Tits buildings

This subsection is about the Bruhat-Tits theory of constructing an affine building from a split reductive group over a valued field.
We refer to
[
4
,
5
]
for details.

In what follows,
K
is equipped with a discrete valuation
\operatorname{val}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)\colon K\to\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
.
Its valuation group
\operatorname{val}\left(K^{\times}\right)
is denoted by
\Gamma
.
We use
K^{\circ}
to denote the valuation ring,
\varpi
a fixed uniformizer, and
\kappa
the residue field.

2.5.1
.

Let
\Phi
be a root system and
G
a group.
A
root group datum
1
1

1

It is called a
root datum
in
[
4
]
*6.1.1.
of type
\Phi
in
G
is a system
(T,(U_{a},M_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
,
where
T
is a subgroup of
G
, each
U_{a}
is a non-trivial subgroup of
G
, and each
M_{a}
is a right congruence class modulo
T
, satisfying the axioms
(RD 1)
-
(RD 6)
in
[
4
]
*6.1.1.
This root group datum is said to be
generating
when
G
is generated by the subgroups
T
and
U_{a}
for
a\in\Phi
.

It turns out that,
M_{a}
is completely determined by
U_{a}
,
U_{-a}
, and
T
.
From now on, we say that
(T,(U_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
is a root group datum by an abuse of language.

2.5.2
.

Let
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
be a split reductive group.
Then there is a generating root group datum
(T,(U_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
of type
\Phi
in
G
, where each
U_{a}
is (the group of
K
-points of) the root subgroup
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{a}
.

2.5.3
.

A
valuation on the root group datum
(T,(U_{a},M_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
is a family

\varphi=(\varphi_{a})_{a\in\Phi}

of functions
\varphi_{a}\colon U_{a}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
satisfying the axioms
(V 0)
-
(V 5)
in
[
4
]
*6.2.1.
For each
a\in\Phi
, let
\Gamma_{a}
denote the set
\varphi_{a}(U_{a}\setminus\left\{1\right\})
and
(U_{a,k})_{k\in\mathbb{R}}
the filtration on
U_{a}
induced by
\varphi_{a}
, namely
U_{a,k}:=\varphi_{a}^{-1}([k,\infty])
.

For simplicity and the purpose of this paper, all root systems
\Phi
in what follows are assumed to be
reduced
. We refer to
[
4
,
5
]
for general statements. A valuation
\varphi
is said to be
special
if
0\in\Gamma_{a}
for all
a\in\Phi
, and
discrete
if each
\Gamma_{a}
is a discrete subset of
\mathbb{R}
.
If
\varphi
is both special and discrete, then each
\Gamma_{a}
is a discrete subgroup of
(\mathbb{R},+)
.

2.5.4
[
4
]
*6.2.3.

Let
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
be a split reductive group.
A
pinning
on it is a basis
\Delta
of
\Phi
together with a family of isomorphisms
(u_{a}\colon\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}_{\rm a}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{a})_{a\in\Delta}
.
Then the family
(u_{a})_{a\in\Delta}
extends uniquely to a coherent system of isomorphisms
(u_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
, called a

Chevalley system
.
We refer to
[
5
]
*3.2.2 or
[
9
]
*XXIII, 6.2 for more details.
Given such a system
(u_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
, the family
(\operatorname{val}\circ u_{a}^{-1})_{a\in\Phi}

is a special and discrete valuation on the root group datum
(T,(U_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
.

Let
(T,(U_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
be a generating root group datum and
\varphi=(\varphi_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
a valuation on it.
For any vector
\mathbf{v}
in the underlying space
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
of
\Phi
, the family
\psi=(\psi_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
given by
\psi_{a}\colon u\mapsto\varphi_{a}(u)+a(\mathbf{v})
is a valuation and is denoted by
\varphi+\mathbf{v}
.
The valuations
\varphi
and
\psi=\varphi+\mathbf{v}
are said to be
equipollent
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
be the equipollent class of
\varphi
.
Then
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
is an affine space under
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
.
We fix
\varphi
as the reference point.
Let

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle:=\left\{\alpha_{a+k}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a\in\Phi,k\in\Gamma_{a}\right\}

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{{E}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{(a,\alpha_{a+k})\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a\in\Phi,k\in\Gamma_{a}\right\}.

For
\alpha=\alpha_{a+k}\in\Sigma
, let
U_{\alpha}:=U_{a,k}
and
U_{\alpha+}:=\bigcup_{h>k}U_{a,h}
.

[
5
]
*4.2.9.

Let
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
be a split reductive group.
A valuation
\varphi=(\varphi_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
on a root group datum
(T,(U_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
is said to be
compatible with
\operatorname{val}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
if for all
u\in U_{a}
and
\boldsymbol{t}\in T
, we have
\varphi_{a}\left(\boldsymbol{t}u\boldsymbol{t}^{-1}\right)=\varphi_{a}(u)+\operatorname{val}\left(a(\boldsymbol{t})\right)
.
The valuation in
Example

2.5.4
is such a valuation.
The set of compatible valuations forms an equipollent class
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
Then
\Sigma
and
\operatorname{{E}}
are given as in
2.2.4
.
We use
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
to denote the affine apartment of type
\operatorname{{E}}
obtained in this way.

Let
N
be the subgroup of
G
generated by
T
and
M_{a}
for all
a\in\Phi
.
Then
N
normalizes
T
and induces an epimorphism
\prescript{v\!}{}{\nu}\colon N\to\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}\left(\Phi\right)
such that

\prescript{v\!}{}{\nu}(M_{a})=\left\{r_{a}\right\}
and that
\operatorname{Ker}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{\nu}\right)=T
.
Next,
N
acts on the valuations as follows.
Let
\varphi=(\varphi_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
be a valuation and
m\in N
.
Then
m.\varphi
is the valuation given by

(m.\varphi)_{a}\colon u\mapsto\varphi_{\prescript{v\!}{}{\nu}(m)^{-1}.a}(m^{-1}um)
.
Note that
m.(\varphi+\mathbf{v})=m.\varphi+\prescript{v\!}{}{\nu}(m).\mathbf{v}
.
Hence,
N
stabilizes each equipollent class
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
and induces a group homomorphism
\nu\colon N\to\operatorname{Aut}\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\right)
whose vectorial part is
\prescript{v\!}{}{\nu}
.

Let
H=\operatorname{Ker}\left(\nu\right)
and
\widehat{W}=\nu(N)
.
In general,
\widehat{W}
is larger than the expected affine Weyl group
W
making
(\operatorname{\mathbb{A}},\Sigma)
an affine apartment.
Indeed,
W
is generated by
\left\{r_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha\in\Sigma}
.

[
5
]
*4.2.7.

Let
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
be a split reductive group.
Then the group
N
is (the group of
K
-points of) the normalizer
\operatorname{\mathsf{N}}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
, the epimorphism
\prescript{v\!}{}{\nu}\colon N\to\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}\left(\Phi\right)
comes from the quotient
N\to N/T\cong\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}\left(\Phi\right)
as in
Definition

2.4.7
, and the translation subgroup
\nu(T)
of
\widehat{W}
has the following characterization: for any
\boldsymbol{t}\in T
, the translation vector
\mathbf{v}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
of
\nu(\boldsymbol{t})
is determined by

\chi(\mathbf{v}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=-\operatorname{val}\left(\chi(\boldsymbol{t})\right),\qquad\text{for all}\qquad\chi\in\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}^{\ast}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}.

On the other hand, the translation subgroup of
W
is
\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma
, where
\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}
is the
coroot lattice
, namely the lattice in
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
generated by the coroots.
In general, they are different.
But if
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
is semisimple and simply-connected, then
W
equals
\widehat{W}
.

From now on, assume a special and discrete valuation
\varphi
is fixed.

Let
\Omega
be a nonempty subset of
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
The subgroup of
G
generated by
U_{\alpha}
for all affine roots such that
\alpha\supseteq\Omega
is denoted by
U_{\Omega}
.
Then the image of
N\cap U_{\Omega}
under
\nu\colon N\to\widehat{W}
is generated by the reflections
r_{\alpha}
for affine roots
\alpha
such that
\Omega\subseteq\partial\alpha
.
Let
\Sigma_{\Omega}
be the set of such affine roots and
\Phi_{\Omega}
the corresponding root subsystem of
\Phi
under
\operatorname{{E}}
.
Then the vectorial part
\prescript{v\!}{}{\nu}(N\cap U_{\Omega})
equals the linear Weyl group of
\Phi_{\Omega}
.
Finally, let
\widehat{N}_{\Omega}
denote the fixator of
\Omega
in
N
and
\widehat{P}_{\Omega}:=\widehat{N}_{\Omega}\cdot U_{\Omega}
.

2.5.5
.

The
Bruhat-Tits building
of
G
(equipped with the generating root group datum
(T,(U_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
and the valuation
\varphi
on it) is the quotient
G
-set
\operatorname{{B}}\left(\varphi\right)
of
G\times\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
under the following equivalent relation:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:BT-building}(g,x)\sim(h,y)\iff\exists n\in N:y=\nu(n).x,\ g^{-1}hn\in\widehat{P}_{x}.

(2.5.1)

We will simply denote this set by
\operatorname{{B}}
if there is no ambiguity.

2.2
[
4
]
*§7.4.

The Bruhat-Tits building is independent of the choice of
\varphi\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
Identifying
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
with the subset
\left\{1\right\}\times\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
of
\operatorname{{B}}
, we have:

(ii)

\operatorname{{B}}=\bigcup_{g\in G}g.\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
;

(ii)

each
U_{\alpha}
fixes
\alpha\in\Sigma
pointwise;

(ii)

for each nonempty subset
\Omega\subseteq\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
, its fixator is
\widehat{P}_{\Omega}
, which acts transitively on apartments containing
\Omega
;

(ii)

the stabilizer (resp. fixator) of
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
is
N
(resp.
H
).

Then we see that
\operatorname{{B}}
is a building of type
(\operatorname{\mathbb{A}},W)
.
Like the Tits building, the action of
G
on
\operatorname{{B}}
is
strongly transitive
: it acts transitively on the pairs
(A,C)
where
A
is an apartment, and
C
is a chamber in
A
.
However, unlike the Tits building, the action of
G
on
\operatorname{{B}}
is not necessarily
type-preserving
: since
\widehat{W}
is usually larger than the affine Weyl group
W
, there cloud be some
g\in G
acts incompatible with the type function.

2.5.6
.

Let
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
be a split reductive group.
Then there is a unique Bruhat-Tits building associated to it through
Definition

2.5.5
.
Moreover, this building is of type
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)
and is independent of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
.
We denote it by
\operatorname{{B}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}\right)
.
Then, using the isogeny and existence theorem, we can see that it depends only on the root system
\Phi
and the ground field
K
.
To obtain a strongly transitive and type-preserving action on the building, we may take
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
to be semisimple and simply-connected.

2.6
Concave functions and smooth models

One important ingredient in Bruhat-Tits theory is the theory of various smooth models associated to concave functions.
In this subsection, we follow
[
4
,
5
,
16
]
to review this topic.

In what follows,
\Phi
is a
reduced
root system,
(T,(U_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
is a generating root group datum in
G
, and we fix a
special and discrete
valuation
\varphi
as the reference point of its equipollent class
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
We refer to
[
4
,
5
]
for statements in general settings.

Let’s first introduce the
ordered monoid of extended real numbers

\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
.
Informally,
\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
is the union of
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{R}+
, and
\left\{\infty\right\}
, where
\mathbb{R}+:=\left\{k+\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}k\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
(one can think
k+
as
k
plus an infinitesimal) and
\infty
is the positive infinity.
Then the commutative addition and the total order on
\mathbb{R}
are extended to
\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
intuitively. We refer to
[
4
]
*6.4.1 for details.

2.6.1
.

Whenever we have a filtration
(F_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{R}}
(for instance, the filtration
(U_{a,k})_{k\in\mathbb{R}}
of
U_{a}
in
Definition

2.5.3
), we can extend it to
(F_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}}
by defining

\displaystyle F_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{R},k\geqslant\lambda}F_{k},

\displaystyle F_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{R}}F_{k}.

We say
k\in\mathbb{R}
is a
jump
of the filtration if
F_{k+}\neq F_{k}
.
In our most usage, the jumps are precisely the elements of
\Gamma
.
For any
\lambda\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
, we use the notation
\lceil\lambda\rceil
to denote the smallest
k\in\Gamma
such that
\lambda\leqslant k
.

2.6.2
.

Let
\Phi
be a root system and
\widetilde{\Phi}=\Phi\cup\left\{0\right\}
.
Then a
concave function (on
\widetilde{\Phi}
)
is a function
f\colon\widetilde{\Phi}\to\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
such that for any finite family
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\widetilde{\Phi}
satisfying
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\in\widetilde{\Phi}
, we have

\sum_{i\in I}f(a_{i})\geqslant f(\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}).

A concave function
f
is said to be
on
\Phi
if
f(0)=0
and
f(\Phi)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.

In what follows, we fix a
good filtration

(H_{k})_{k\geqslant 0}
on
H
.
We refer to
[
4
]
*6.4.38 for its definition, under the name “
extension of the valuation
”.

Let
f
be a concave function on
\widetilde{\Phi}
.
We use
U_{f}
to denote the subgroup of
G
generated by
U_{a,f(a)}
for all
a\in\Phi
.
Let
P_{f}
denote the subgroup
H_{f(0)}\cdot U_{f}
, then we have the following multiplication map
[
4
]
*6.4.48:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Decomposition:Pf}\prod_{a\in\Phi^{-}}U_{a,f(a)}\times H_{f(0)}\times\prod_{a\in\Phi^{+}}U_{a,f(a)}\longrightarrow P_{f}.

(2.6.1)

It is injective in general and moreover bijective if
f(0)>0
.

2.6.3
[
4
]
*6.4.2
[
5
]
*4.6.26.

Let
\Omega
be a nonempty subset of
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
Define
f_{\Omega}\colon\Phi\to\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
as follows:

f_{\Omega}(a)=\inf\left\{k\in\mathbb{R}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\Omega\subseteq\alpha_{a+k}\right\}.

Then
f_{\Omega}
is a concave function on
\Phi
.
Then we have
U_{f_{\Omega}}=U_{\Omega}
and hence
P_{f_{\Omega}}\subseteq\widehat{P}_{\Omega}
.
When
F
is a facet in
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
, the group
P_{f_{F}}
is called a
parahoric subgroup
.
It is called an
Iwahori subgroup
if
F
is further an alcove.
Note that these terms are usually restricted to a specific choice of
H_{0}
: the
H^{\circ}
in
2.6.5
.

Let
f
be a concave function on
\widetilde{\Phi}
.
Define
f^{\prime}\colon\Phi\to\mathbb{R}
as follows:

f^{\prime}(a):=\inf\left\{k\in\Gamma_{a}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}k\geqslant f(a)\right\}.

It is called the
optimization
of
f
.
In general, it is not necessarily a concave function (see
[
5
]
*4.5.3).
However, under our assumptions, it is a concave function on
\Phi
.
When
f^{\prime}=f
, we say
f
is
optimal
. The set of roots
a\in\Phi
such that
f^{\prime}(a)+f^{\prime}(-a)=0
is a root subsystem and is denoted by
\Phi_{f}
, called the
root subsystem associated to
f
.

.

Note that for any
a\in\Phi
, we have

f_{\Omega}(a)+f_{\Omega}(-a)=-\inf_{x\in\Omega}a(x)-\inf_{x\in\Omega}(-a(x))=\sup_{x\in\Omega}a(x)-\inf_{x\in\Omega}a(x)\geqslant 0.

The equality holds if and only if
a(x)
is a constant on
\Omega
, namely
\Omega
is contained in a hyperplane having vectorial part
\operatorname{Ker}\left(a\right)
.
Therefore,
\Phi_{f_{\Omega}}=\Phi_{\Omega}
.

2.6.4
[
4
]
*6.4.23.

Let
f
be a concave function on
\Phi
.
Define
f^{\ast}\colon\widetilde{\Phi}\to\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
as follows:

f^{\ast}(a):=\begin{cases}f(a)&if $f(a)+f(-a)>0$,\\
f(a)+&if $f(a)+f(-a)=0$.\end{cases}

Then
f^{\ast}
is a concave function on
\widetilde{\Phi}
.
Let
\overline{G}_{f}
denote the quotient
P_{f}/P_{f^{\ast}}
and
\overline{U}_{f,a}
(resp.
\overline{T}_{f}
) the image of
U_{a,f(a)}
(resp.
H_{f(0)}
) in
\overline{G}_{f}
.
Then
(\overline{T}_{f},(\overline{U}_{f,a})_{a\in\Phi_{f}})
is a generating root group datum of type
\Phi_{f}
in
\overline{G}_{f}
.

.

Let
x
be a point in
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
.
Since
P_{f_{x}}
stabilizes
x
,
P_{f_{x}^{\ast}}
stabilizes the infinitesimal neighborhood of
x
.
Then we may think the root group datum
(\overline{T}_{f_{x}},(\overline{U}_{f_{x},a})_{a\in\Phi_{f_{x}}})
corresponds to the spherical building at
x
.

In what follows,
K
is a
Henselian field
in the sense that
K^{\circ}
is a Henselian ring.
We also assume that the residue field
\kappa
is perfect.
Let
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
be a split reductive group over
K
and take the generating root group datum
(T,(U_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
in
G
following
Example

2.5.2
.
Then we fix a special and discrete valuation
\varphi
following
Example

2.5.4
.

In the rest of this subsection, we should introduce the smooth models associated to concave functions.

2.6.5
[
16
]
*6.2
[
5
]
*§4.3.

For each root subgroup
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{a}
, there are connected smooth models
\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda}
(
\lambda\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}
) of it satisfying the following:

(i)

\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda}\left(K^{\circ}\right)=U_{a,\lambda}
;

(ii)

the special fiber
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda})_{\kappa}
is unipotent for all
\lambda
;

(iii)

the congruence subgroup

\Gamma(\varpi^{m},\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda}):=\operatorname{Ker}\left(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda}\left(K^{\circ}\right)\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{m}\right)\right)

equals
U_{a,\lambda+m\operatorname{val}\left(\varpi\right)}
for every positive integer
m
;

(iv)

the Lie algebras
\operatorname{\mathfrak{u}}_{a,\lambda}
of
\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda}
form a filtration on the Lie algebra
\operatorname{\mathfrak{u}}_{a}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{U}}_{a}
.

Moreover, the filtration
(U_{a,\lambda})_{\lambda\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}}
extends to an inductive system
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda})_{\lambda\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}}
.

.

In our situation, the scheme
\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda}
can be taken to be
\operatorname{\mathbb{W}}_{K^{\circ}}\left(U_{a,\lambda}\right)
.
Here, the
K^{\circ}
-group scheme
\operatorname{\mathbb{W}}_{K^{\circ}}\left(M\right)
associated to a
K^{\circ}
-module
M
is the functor
R\rightsquigarrow M\otimes_{K^{\circ}}R
. We refer to
[
9
]
*I, 4.6 for details of
\operatorname{\mathbb{W_{K^{\circ}}[\>\cdot\>]}}
.

Suppose we are given a good filtration
(H_{\lambda})_{\lambda\geqslant 0}
on
H\subseteq T
.
Then there is a smooth model
\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
such that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}\left(K^{\circ}\right)=H_{0}
.
We refer to
[
5
]
*§4.4 for details.
Here we follow
[
16
]
, considering the
Moy-Prasad filtration
.
We start with the subgroup

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline ef:MP}H^{\circ}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{t}\in T\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\operatorname{val}\left(\chi(\boldsymbol{t})\right)=0,\text{ for all }\chi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right\}.

Then the Moy-Prasad filtration
(H_{\lambda})_{\lambda\geqslant 0}
is defined as:

H_{\lambda}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{t}\in H^{\circ}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\operatorname{val}\left(\chi(\boldsymbol{t})-1\right)\geqslant\lambda,\text{ for all }\chi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right\}.

There is also a
Moy-Prasad filtration

(\operatorname{\mathfrak{t}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\geqslant 0}
of the Lie algebra
\operatorname{\mathfrak{t}}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
:

\operatorname{\mathfrak{t}}_{\lambda}:=\left\{t\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{t}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\operatorname{val}\left(\operatorname{\mathrm{d}}\chi(t)\right)\geqslant\lambda,\text{ for all }\chi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{X}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\right)\right\}.

2.6.6
[
16
]
*§4.

There are connected smooth models
\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{\lambda}
(
\lambda\geqslant 0
) of
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
satisfying the following:

(iv)

\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{\lambda}\left(K^{\circ}\right)=H_{\lambda}
;

(iv)

the special fiber
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{\lambda})_{\kappa}
is unipotent for all
\lambda>0
;

(iv)

the congruence subgroup

\Gamma(\varpi^{m},\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{\lambda}):=\operatorname{Ker}\left(\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{\lambda}\left(K^{\circ}\right)\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{\lambda}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{m}\right)\right)

equals
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}\left(K\right)_{\lambda+m\operatorname{val}\left(\varpi\right)}
for every positive integer
m
;

(iv)

the Lie algebra of
\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{\lambda}
equals
\operatorname{\mathfrak{t}}_{\lambda}
.

Moreover, the filtration
(H_{\lambda})_{\lambda\geqslant 0}
extends to an inductive system
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\geqslant 0}
.

At this stage, we have inductive systems of group schemes
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda\geqslant 0}
and
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,\lambda})_{\lambda\in\widetilde{\mathbb{R}}}
(
a\in\Phi
). Such a datum is a schematic version of a valuation on a root group datum.

The main theorem of the
schematic Bruhat-Tits theory
is

2.3
[
16
]
*8.3
[
5
]
*§4.6.

For a concave function
f
on
\widetilde{\Phi}
, there is a connected smooth model
\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
such that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}\left(K^{\circ}\right)=P_{f}
.
Moreover:

(ii)

The schematic closure of
T
in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}
is
\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{f(0)}
.

(ii)

For each
a\in\Phi
, the schematic closure of
U_{a}
in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}
is
\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)}
.

(ii)

The multiplication morphism (the products can be taken in any order)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\prod_{a\in\Phi_{f}^{-}}\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)}\cdot\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{f(0)}\cdot\prod_{a\in\Phi_{f}^{+}}\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}}

(2.6.2)

is an open immersion. If
f(0)>0
, it induces an isomorphism on special fibers.

Moreover, the assignment
f\rightsquigarrow\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}
is functorial.

.

Note that
(ii)
implies that
Eq.

2.6.2
gives a bijection on
K^{\circ}
-points (and more generally,
K^{\circ}/I
-points for any ideal
I
) using the Henselian property.

The smooth model
\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f_{F}}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
associated to a parahoric subgroup
G_{f_{F}}
is called a
parahoric group scheme
.
The parahoric group model
\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f_{x}}
associated to a special vertex
x
is essentially the
Chevalley group scheme
.
For the theory of Chevalley group schemes, we refer to
[
5
]
*§3.2 and 4.6.15
[
9
]
*XXV.

§ 3  Formula of the simplicial volume

In this section, we will deduce the formulas for the simplicial volume and simplicity surface area as shown in
Theorem

1.2
.
In what follows,
K
is assumed to be a
local field
.
Namely,
K
is complete with respect to its valuation
\operatorname{val}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
, and its residue field
\kappa
is finite. Let
q
be the cardinality of
\kappa
.

The strategy is to employ a
strongly transitive
and
type-preserving
automorphism group.
Let
\operatorname{{B}}
be a Bruhat-Tits building of split type
\Phi
.
Then
2.5.6
tells us that we can realize it as the Bruhat-Tits building of a simply-connected splittable semisimple group
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
having root system
\Phi
over the ground local field
K
. The group
G
of
K
-points of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
is such an automorphism group.

From now on, we fix a special vertex
o
in
\operatorname{{B}}
and choose a split maximal torus
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
such that the apartment
\operatorname{{A}}
associated to
(\operatorname{\mathsf{G}},\operatorname{\mathsf{T}})
contains
o
.
We will follow the notations and conventions in
Section

2.5
.
In particular,
o
is the reference point of the underlying Euclidean affine space
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
of
\operatorname{{A}}
.

For any vertex
x
in
\operatorname{{B}}
, it is clear that a type-preserving automorphism
\phi\in G
will map a path from
o
to
x
to a path from
\phi(o)
to
\phi(x)
.
Hence,
G
preserves the simplicial distance.
Therefore, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SimplicialVolumeStrategy}\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)\text{ (resp. $\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)$)}=\sum_{x}\left[P_{o}:P_{o,x}\right],

(3.0.1)

where

•

P_{o}
is the stabilizer of
o
in
G
,

•

P_{o,x}
is the stabilizer of
x
in
P_{o}
, and

•

the summation is taking over the intersection of
B(r)
(resp.
\partial(r)
) with a fundamental domain
\operatorname{{D}}
of the action of
P_{o}
.

The computation will be done as follows.
In
Section

3.1
, we break the index
\left[P_{o}:P_{o,x}\right]
into two factors.
In
Section

3.2
, we will see that the first factor can be given by Poincaré polynomials.
In
Section

3.3
, we will compute the second factor using the theory of concave functions.
In
Section

3.4
, we will describe a fundamental domain of the action of
P_{o}
and finally prove
Theorem

1.2
.

3.1
Parahoric reduction

The goal of this subsection is to break the index
\left[P_{o}:P_{o,x}\right]
into two factors, one of which is a power of
q
.
For this purpose, we need some facts about concave functions, recalling
Section

2.6
.

First note that
P_{o}
is a
parahoric subgroup
of
G
: it is indeed
P_{f_{o}}
using the notations in
Example

2.6.3
.
Then we have a generating root group datum
(\overline{T}_{o},(\overline{U}_{o,a})_{a\in\Phi})
of type
\Phi
in the quotient
P_{o}/P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}
, following
Lemma

2.6.4
.
Moreover, using
Theorem

2.3
, we can see that this datum arises from a split reductive group over
\kappa
.

3.1.1
.

Let
f
be a concave function on
\Phi
.
Denote the unipotent radical and the reductive quotient of
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f})_{\kappa}
by

\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{R}}}_{f}
and

\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{G}}}_{f}
respectively.
Let

\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{T}}}_{f}
(resp.

\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{U}}}_{f,a}
)
be the image of
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{0})_{\kappa}
(resp.
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)})_{\kappa}
) in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{G}}}_{f}
.
Then
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{G}}}_{f},\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{T}}}_{f})
is a split reductive group with root system
\Phi_{f}
and root subgroups
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{U}}}_{f,a})_{a\in\Phi_{f}}
.
Moreover, the generating root group datum
(\overline{T}_{f},(\overline{U}_{f,a})_{a\in\Phi_{f}})
associated to
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{G}}}_{f},\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{T}}}_{f})
is the same as in
Lemma

2.6.4
.

Proof.

Applying
Theorem

2.3
to
f
, we see that:

(iv)

(\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{0})_{\kappa}
is a split maximal torus in
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f})_{\kappa}
, the pair
\left((\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f})_{\kappa},(\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{0})_{\kappa}\right)
has root system
\Phi
, and for any
a\in\Phi
,
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)})_{\kappa}
is the root subgroup associated to it.

We also refer to
[
5
]
*4.6.4 for a direct proof.

By
[
5
]
*1.1.11, the multiplication morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\prod_{a\in\Phi_{f}^{-}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{R}}}_{f}\cap(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)})_{\kappa}\right)\cdot\operatorname{{R}}_{u}\left((\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{0})_{\kappa}\right)\cdot\prod_{a\in\Phi_{f}^{+}}\left(\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{R}}}_{f}\cap(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)})_{\kappa}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{R}}}_{f}}

(
\ast
)

is an isomorphism. Hence,
(iv)
implies that first statement of this lemma except that the root system is
\Phi_{f}
.

Next, applying
Theorem

2.3
to both
f
and
f^{\ast}
, and using the inductive systems in
Lemmas

2.6.6
and
2.6.5
, we see that the inclusion
P_{f^{\ast}}\subseteq P_{f}
extends to a homomorphism
\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f_{\ast}}\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}
so that

(iv)

through the homomorphism
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f_{\ast}})_{\kappa}\to(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f})_{\kappa}
, the unipotent group
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{0+})_{\kappa}
is mapped onto the unipotent radical
\operatorname{{R}}_{u}\left((\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{0})_{\kappa}\right)
of
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{0})_{\kappa}
, and for any
a\in\Phi
,
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f^{\ast}(a)})_{\kappa}
is mapped onto the intersection of the unipotent radical
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{R}}}_{f}
and the root subgroup
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)})_{\kappa}
.

We also refer to
[
5
]
*4.6.10 for another proof.

Now,
(iv)
tells us that, through the reduction
P_{f^{\ast}}\subseteq P_{f}\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}\left(\kappa\right)
,
T_{0+}
(resp.
U_{a,f^{\ast}(a)}
) is mapped to the group of
\kappa
-points of
\operatorname{{R}}_{u}\left((\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{0})_{\kappa}\right)
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{R}}}_{f}\cap(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)})_{\kappa}
). Note that,
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{R}}}_{f}\cap(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)})_{\kappa}
is the entire
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)})_{\kappa}
if and only if
f(a)=f^{\ast}(a)
.
Then, using the isomorphism
Eq.

∗

, the second statement of the lemma follows, and we see that the root system of
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{G}}}_{f},\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{T}}}_{f})
is
\Phi_{f}
.
∎

Now, back to our situation.
Let
\overline{P}_{o}
denote the quotient
P_{o}/P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}
and
\overline{P}_{o,x}
the image of
P_{o,x}
in it.
Then we claim that
\overline{P}_{o,x}
is the group of
\kappa
-points of a parabolic subgroup
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{P}}}_{o,x}
of
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{G}}}_{o}
, the reductive quotient of
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f_{o}})_{\kappa}
.
To see this, first note that
P_{o,x}
is the group
P_{f_{\left\{o,x\right\}}}
defined in
Example

2.6.3
.
Then we consider the following lemma.

3.1.2
.

Let
f,g
be two concave functions on
\Phi
with
g\geqslant f
. Suppose

\Psi_{f,g}:=\left\{a\in\Phi\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}f(a)=g(a)\right\}

is a parabolic subset of
\Phi
.
Then the image of
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g})_{\kappa}
in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{G}}}_{f}
is a parabolic subgroup
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{P}}}_{f,g}
containing
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{T}}}_{f}
, corresponding to the parabolic subset
\Psi_{f,g}
.

Proof.

Since
g\geqslant f
, we have
P_{g}\subseteq P_{f}
, which extends to a homomorphism
\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g}\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}
.
By the proof of
Lemma

3.1.1
, we have the follows.
First, since
g^{\ast}\geqslant f^{\ast}
, the image of
\operatorname{{R}}_{u}\left((\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g})_{\kappa}\right)
in
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f})_{\kappa}
is contained in
\operatorname{{R}}_{u}\left((\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f})_{\kappa}\right)
.
Then, for each
a\in\Phi
, the image of
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,g(a)})_{\kappa}
in
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f})_{\kappa}
is either the entire
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)})_{\kappa}
if
g(a)=f(a)
or is contained in
\operatorname{{R}}_{u}\left((\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f})_{\kappa}\right)
if
g(a)\geqslant f^{\ast}(a)
.
Therefore, the image of
(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g})_{\kappa}
in
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{G}}}_{f}
is generated by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{T}}}_{f}
and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{U}}}_{f,a}
for all
a\in\Psi
.
Then the statement follows.
∎

The parabolic subset of
\Phi
corresponding to
\operatorname{\mathfrak{\overline{P}}}_{o,x}
is

\Psi_{o,x}:=\left\{a\in\Phi\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a(x)\geqslant 0\right\}.

Now, we choose a Weyl chamber
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
such that
x\in\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
.
Let
\Phi^{+}
be the system of positive roots corresponding to
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
.
Then we have
\Psi_{o,x}=\Phi^{+}\cup\Phi_{o,x}
, where
\Phi_{o,x}
is the root subsystem associated to the concave function
f_{\left\{o,x\right\}}
:

\Phi_{o,x}:=\left\{a\in\Phi\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}f_{\left\{o,x\right\}}(a)\in\Gamma,f_{\left\{o,x\right\}}(a)+f_{\left\{o,x\right\}}(-a)=0\right\}=\left\{a\in\Phi\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a(x)=0\right\}.

The simple roots in
\Phi^{+}\cap\Phi_{o,x}
form a
type

I_{o,x}
(see
Conventions

2.3.2
and
2.4.11
).

3.1.3
.

Fix a choice of
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
. We say a point
x\in\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}

has type
I
if
I_{o,x}=I
.

At this stage, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:IndexDecom}\left[P_{o}:P_{o,x}\right]=\left[\overline{P}_{o}:\overline{P}_{o,x}\right]\cdot\left[P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}:P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}\cap P_{o,x}\right],

(3.1.1)

where
\overline{P}_{o}
is (the group of
\kappa
-points of) a splittable reductive group with root system
\Phi
,
\overline{P}_{o,x}
is (the group of
\kappa
-points of) a parabolic subgroup of the former having type
I_{o,x}
, and

P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}
is a
pro-unipotent group
in the sense that it is a projective limit of groups, each of them is the group of
\kappa
-points of an unipotent group over
\kappa
.

3.2
Poincaré polynomials of parabolic subgroups

This subsection treats the first factor
\left[\overline{P}_{o}:\overline{P}_{o,x}\right]
in
Eq.

3.1.1
. That is the index of (the group of
\kappa
-points of) a parabolic subgroup in a splittable reductive group over
\kappa
.

Let
(\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{T}}})
be a split reductive group over
\kappa
and
(\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{P}}},\prescript{v\!}{}{F},\Psi,I)
a quadruple as in
Proposition

2.4.10
.
Then
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}}}\left(\kappa\right)
acts strongly transitively and type-preserving on the Tits building
\operatorname{\prescript{v\!}{}{{B}}}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}}}
, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{P}}}\left(\kappa\right)
is the stabilizer of
\prescript{v\!}{}{F}
.
Hence, the quotient
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}}}\left(\kappa\right)/\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{P}}}\left(\kappa\right)
counts the facets in
\operatorname{\prescript{v\!}{}{{B}}}
having type
I
.
Note that
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}}}\left(\kappa\right)/\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{P}}}\left(\kappa\right)\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}/\overline{P}}}\left(\kappa\right)
according to
Lang’s theorem
(see, e.g.
[
11
]
*17.98).

Let
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
be the Weyl group of
(\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{T}}})
.
Then the
generalized Bruhat decomposition
(see, e.g.
[
11
]
*21.h and 21.i) says that

\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{P}}}=\bigsqcup_{\bar{w}\in\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}/\prescript{v}{}{W_{I}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\left(\bar{w}\right)\cong\bigsqcup_{w\in\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}^{I}}\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\left(w\right),

where each
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\left(\bar{w}\right)
(as well as
\operatorname{\mathsf{C}}\left(w\right)
) is an affine space of dimension
\ell(w)
(the
length
of
w
in
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
), called the
Schubert cell
of
w
,
\prescript{v}{}{W_{I}}
is the subgroup of
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
generated by reflections with respect to the simple roots in
I
, and
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}^{I}
is a system of representatives.

3.2.1
.

The
Poincaré polynomial
of the pair
(\Phi,I)
is the following:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Poincare:def}\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(z\right):=\sum_{w\in\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}^{I}}z^{\ell(w)}.

(3.2.1)

When
I=\emptyset
, it is denoted by
\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi}
and called the
Poincaré polynomial
of
\Phi
.

Then we have
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}/\overline{P}}}\left(\kappa\right)=\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)
.
Note that the image of
{\prod_{a\in\Phi\setminus\Psi}\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{U}}}_{a}}\to{\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{P}}}}
is the
big cell
.
Hence,
\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\right)=\left\lvert\Phi\setminus\Psi\right\rvert
.

At this stage, we already know that:

3.2.2
.

The index
\left[\overline{P}_{o}:\overline{P}_{o,x}\right]
is computed by an integral polynomial
\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}
of degree
\left\lvert\Phi\setminus\Psi\right\rvert
.

The rest of this subsection aims to deduce
\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}
from the information of
(\Phi,I)
.

3.2.3
.

\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}=\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi}/\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi_{I}}
, where
\Phi_{I}
is the root subsystem of
\Phi
generated by
I
.

Proof.

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{B}}}
be a Borel subgroup of
