\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{G}}}
contained in
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{P}}}
. Then we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline em:Poincare:Phi:ast}\left[\overline{G}:\overline{P}\right]=\left[\overline{G}:\overline{B}\right]\cdot\left[\overline{P}:\overline{B}\right].

(
\ast
)

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{L}}}
be the Levi subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{P}}}
. Then
(\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{L}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{T}}})
is a split reductive group with root system
\Phi_{I}
. Moreover,
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{B}}}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{L}}}
is a Borel subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{L}}}
. Then we have

\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{P}}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{B}}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{B}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{L}}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{B}}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{L}}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{B}}}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{\overline{L}}}.

Applying this to
Eq.

∗

, the statement follows.
∎

3.2.4
.

Suppose
\Phi
can be decomposed into root subsystems
\Phi_{1},\cdots,\Phi_{s}
. Then we have
\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi}(z)=\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi_{1}}\cdots\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi_{s}}
.

Proof.

This is because the decomposition of split reductive groups corresponds to the decomposition of Weyl groups and root systems.
∎

Hence, it suffices to know the Poincaré polynomials of irreducible root systems.
When
\Phi
is irreducible of type
X_{n}
, we will denote its Poincaré polynomial by
\operatorname{{P}}_{X_{n}}
.

3.2.5
.

Let
\Phi
be a reduced root system of rank
n
. Then there are positive integers
d_{1},\cdots,d_{n}
depending only on the Weyl group
W
of
\Phi
, such that

\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi}\left(z\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[d_{i}](z),

where
[d_{i}](z):=1+z+\cdots+z^{d_{i}-1}
.

Proof.

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
be a complex semisimple group,
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
a maximal torus in it, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}
a Borel subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}
containing
\operatorname{\mathsf{T}}
.
Let
\Phi
be the associated root system,
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
the Weyl group, and
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
be the complexification of the coroot space.
Then we have:

(iv)

The complex singular cohomology ring of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}
vanishes at odd degree and has a basis dual to the Schubert cells (see
[
1
]
).

(iv)

The
Borel’s theorem
(see
[
2
]
) says that, after dividing degree by two, the complex singular cohomology ring of
\operatorname{\mathsf{G}}/\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}
is isomorphic to the
coinvariant algebra

\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}]_{\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}}
, which is
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}]^{\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}}}\mathbb{C}
, where
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}]
is the ring of complex polynomial functions on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
and
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}]^{\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}}
is the subalgebra of invariant.

(iv)

The
Chevalley-Shephard-Todd theorem
(see
[
3
]
*chap.VI, §3 no.3 thm.3) says that
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}]^{\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}}
is a polynomial algebra generated by homogeneous polynomials on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
.
Let
d_{1},\cdots,d_{n}
be the degrees of them.

Recall that the
Hilbert-Poincaré series
of a graded commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
S_{\bullet}
is defined to be
\sum_{d}\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}\left(S_{d}\right)z^{d}
.
Now, considering the Hilbert-Poincaré series of above graded algebras, the statement follows.
∎

The numbers
d_{1},\cdots,d_{n}
are called the
degrees
of
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
(and of
\Phi
).
When
\Phi
is irreducible, they can be found in
[
3
]
*chap.VI, §4.

For irreducible root systems of type
A_{n}
,
B_{n}
,
C_{n}
, and
D_{n}
, the explicit formulas for their Poincaré polynomials
\operatorname{{P}}_{X_{n};I}
with various types
I
are listed in
Appendix

A
.

3.3
Concave functions

This subsection treats the second factor
\left[P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}:P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}\cap P_{o,x}\right]
in
Eq.

3.1.1
.
It has to be a power of
q
since
P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}
is a pro-unipotent group.

First, let
f_{o^{\ast}x}
be the following concave function:

f_{o^{\ast}x}\colon a\in\widetilde{\Phi}\longmapsto\max\left\{f_{o}^{\ast}(a),f_{\left\{o,x\right\}}(a)\right\}=\max\left\{0+,-a(x)\right\}.

Then, from the definition of
P_{f}
, we have
P_{f_{o^{\ast}x}}=P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}\cap P_{o,x}
. Note that both
f_{o^{\ast}x}
and
f_{o}^{\ast}
take the value
0+
at
0\in\widetilde{\Phi}
.

3.3.1
.

Let
f,g
be two concave functions on
\widetilde{\Phi}
such that
f(0)=g(0)>0
and
g\geqslant f
. Then we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:IndexDecomRoots}\left[P_{f}:P_{g}\right]=\prod_{a\in\Phi}\left\lvert\varphi_{a}^{-1}[f(a),g(a)]\right\rvert,

(3.3.1)

where
\varphi=(\varphi_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
is the valuation corresponding to the reference point
o
.

Proof.

There are two ways to show this. By
Lemmas

2.6.6
and
2.6.5
, we can extend the decomposition
Eq.

2.4.1
to obtain a Lie algebra version of
Eq.

3.3.1
. Hence, if the characteristic of
K
is
0
, the statement follows from the bijective exponential maps of unipotent groups.

In general case, we can consider the morphism
\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g}\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}
obtained by extending the inclusion
P_{g}\subseteq P_{f}
. Then the multiplicative morphism
Eq.

2.6.2
induces the following commutative diagram for all positive integer
i
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\prod_{a\in\Phi^{-}}\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,g(a)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\cdot\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{g(0)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\cdot\prod_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,g(a)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle{\prod_{a\in\Phi^{-}}\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\cdot\operatorname{\mathfrak{T}}_{f(0)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\cdot\prod_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)}}

By
(ii)
, since
f(0)>0
and
g(0)>0
, the horizontals are isomorphisms.
Since
f(0)=g(0)
, at the level of
K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Coker}\left(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{a\in\Phi}\operatorname{Coker}\left(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,g(a)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\right).

By
Lemma

2.6.5
, for each
a\in\Phi
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Coker}\left(\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,g(a)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{U}}_{a,f(a)}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Coker}\left(U_{a,g(a)}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\to U_{a,f(a)}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=U_{a,f(a)}/U_{a,g(a)}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}=\varphi_{a}^{-1}[f(a),g(a)]\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i},

which equals
\varphi_{a}^{-1}[f(a),g(a)]
if
i\cdot\operatorname{val}\left(\varpi\right)>g(a)-f(a)
(see
Example

2.5.4
).

Now, we pass to the limit of the following projective system of homomorphisms.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i+1}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g}\left(\kappa\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i+1}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}\left(\kappa\right)}}

Then we have

\displaystyle P_{f}/P_{g}

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{i}\operatorname{Coker}\left(\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{g}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{G}}_{f}\left(K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{i}\prod_{a\in\Phi}\varphi_{a}^{-1}[f(a),g(a)]\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K^{\circ}/\varpi^{i}=\prod_{a\in\Phi}\varphi_{a}^{-1}[f(a),g(a)].

Then
Eq.

3.3.1
follows.
∎

Applying
Eq.

3.3.1
to
f_{o^{\ast}x}
and
f_{o}^{\ast}
, we have

\displaystyle\left[P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}:P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}\cap P_{o,x}\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{a\in\Phi}\varphi_{a}^{-1}[0+,\max\left\{0+,-a(x)\right\}]

\displaystyle=\prod_{a\in\Phi}\varphi_{-a}^{-1}[0+,\max\left\{0+,a(x)\right\}].

Then by the definition of
\varphi
(see
Example

2.5.4
), we have

\displaystyle\left[P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}:P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}\cap P_{o,x}\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{a\in\Phi}\operatorname{exp}_{q}\left(\frac{\lceil\max\left\{0+,a(x)\right\}\rceil-\lceil 0+\rceil}{\operatorname{val}\left(\varpi\right)}\right)

(3.3.2)

\displaystyle=\prod_{a(x)>0}\operatorname{exp}_{q}\left(\frac{\lceil a(x)\rceil-\lceil 0+\rceil}{\operatorname{val}\left(\varpi\right)}\right),

where
\operatorname{exp}_{q}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
is the exponent function with base
q
.

3.4
Fundamental domain and the proof of
Theorem

1.2

The following lemma gives us a fundamental domain of
P_{o}
in
\operatorname{{B}}
.

3.4.1
.

The convex cone
\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
is a fundamental domain of
P_{o}
.

Proof.

Let
x
be any point in
\operatorname{{B}}
. We need to show that there exists some
g_{x}\in P_{o}
mapping
x
into
\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
.
First, let
g.\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
be an apartment containing both
o
and
x
. More precisely, suppose
o=[g,o+\mathbf{v}_{0}]
and
x=[g,o+\mathbf{v}]
.
Then, from the equivalence relation
Eq.

2.5.1
, there is an
n\in N
, such that
o+\mathbf{v}_{0}=\nu(n).o
and
gn\in P_{o}
.
Let
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
be the vector
\nu(n)^{-1}.(o+\mathbf{v})-o
.
Since
\overline{\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
is the fundamental domain of
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
in
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
, there is a
w\in\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
such that
w.\mathbf{v}_{1}\in\overline{\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
.
Now, let
n_{1}
be a preimage of
w
under
N_{o}\to W_{o}\cong\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
.
Then
n_{1}n^{-1}g^{-1}\in P_{o}
and it maps
x
into
\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
.

On the other hand, if there are two points
x,y\in\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
such that
y=g.x
for some
g\in P_{o}
. Then, by the
vectorial Bruhat decomposition

[
4
]
*7.3.4, we have

g=h_{1}nh_{2},

where
h_{1},h_{2}\in B_{o,\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
and
n\in N
. Therefore,
n\in N_{o}
, which implies
x=y
since
\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
is the fundamental domain of
W_{o}
.
∎

We will denote
\overline{o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}}
by
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
to emphasize that it is a fundamental domain.
Applying
Lemma

3.4.1
to
Eq.

3.0.1
and using
Eq.

3.1.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in B(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)}\left[\overline{P}_{o}:\overline{P}_{o,x}\right]\cdot\left[P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}:P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}\cap P_{o,x}\right],

(3.4.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\partial(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)}\left[\overline{P}_{o}:\overline{P}_{o,x}\right]\cdot\left[P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}:P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}\cap P_{o,x}\right].

(3.4.2)

By
Lemma

3.2.2
, the first factor
\left[\overline{P}_{o}:\overline{P}_{o,x}\right]
is computed by the Poincaré polynomial
\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;{I_{o,x}}}\left(q\right)
, which depends only on the
type
of
x
(see
Convention

3.1.3
).
Hence, we can decompose the index sets
B(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
and
\partial(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
according to the types:

\displaystyle B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in B(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\text{ $x$ has type $I$ }\right\},

(3.4.3)

\displaystyle\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in\partial(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\text{ $x$ has type $I$ }\right\}.

(3.4.4)

Then
Eqs.

3.4.1
and
3.4.2
become the following ones:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)\sum_{x\in B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\left[P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}:P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}\cap P_{o,x}\right],

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)\sum_{x\in\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\left[P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}:P_{f_{o}^{\ast}}\cap P_{o,x}\right].

Applying
Eq.

3.3.2
to the above, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)\sum_{x\in B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\prod_{a(x)>0}\operatorname{exp}_{q}\left(\frac{\lceil a(x)\rceil-\lceil 0+\rceil}{\operatorname{val}\left(\varpi\right)}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)\sum_{x\in\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\prod_{a(x)>0}\operatorname{exp}_{q}\left(\frac{\lceil a(x)\rceil-\lceil 0+\rceil}{\operatorname{val}\left(\varpi\right)}\right).

Note that, the ceiling function
\lceil\>\cdot\>\rceil
used here follows
Convention

2.6.1
.
If we use the usual ceiling function instead and note that
\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;{I_{o,x}}}\right)=\left\lvert\Phi\setminus\Psi_{I_{o,x}}\right\rvert
equals the number of roots
a\in\Phi
such that
a(x)>0
, then we obtain the following formulas:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\right)}}\sum_{x\in B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\prod_{a(x)>0}q^{\lceil a(x)\rceil},

(3.4.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\right)}}\sum_{x\in\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\prod_{a(x)>0}q^{\lceil a(x)\rceil}.

(3.4.6)

This proves
Theorem

1.2
.

.

If the valuation
\operatorname{val}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
is normalized, namely
\operatorname{val}\left(\varpi\right)=1
and hence
\Gamma
equals the additive group of integers
\mathbb{Z}
, then the two versions of ceiling functions
\lceil\>\cdot\>\rceil
coincide and the formulas
Eqs.

3.4.5
and
3.4.6
can be understood in either way.

3.4.2
.

From now on, we assume the valuation
\operatorname{val}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
is normalized.

3.5
Variants of the simplicial volume

Let
\tau\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\to\operatorname{\mathfrak{I}}
be a function factoring through the type function
x\mapsto I_{x,o}
.
Then we can define the
\tau
-variants of the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
as follows.
For any
\dagger\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{I}}
, the quantities
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
count the following sets respectively:

\displaystyle B_{\dagger}(r)

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in B(r)\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\tau(x)=\dagger\right\},

\displaystyle\partial_{\dagger}(r)

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in\partial(r)\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\tau(x)=\dagger\right\}.

Following
Eqs.

3.4.3
and
3.4.4
, we can introduce the following subsets:

\displaystyle B_{\dagger}(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in B_{\dagger}(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\text{ $x$ has type $I$ }\right\},

\displaystyle\partial_{\dagger}(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in\partial_{\dagger}(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\text{ $x$ has type $I$ }\right\}.

Then the same argument for
Theorem

1.2
works and gives us the following formulas:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\right)}}\sum_{x\in B_{\dagger}(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\prod_{a(x)>0}q^{\lceil a(x)\rceil},

(3.5.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\right)}}\sum_{x\in\partial_{\dagger}(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)}\prod_{a(x)>0}q^{\lceil a(x)\rceil}.

(3.5.2)

Such variants may be interesting when we need to focus on certain types of vertices (for instance, when not all vertices are special).
In this paper, we will consider
\dagger=
“being special”, although other variants are also worth considering.

§ 4  Vertices in an apartment

The purpose of this section is to deduce explicit descriptions of the index sets
B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
and
\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
in
Theorem

1.2
.
In
Section

4.1
, this problem will be reduced to irreducible cases.
Then, in
Section

4.2
, a framework will be established, which allow us to give explicit descriptions of the index sets when the Bruhat-Tits building
\operatorname{{B}}
is of split classical type.
Finally, in
Sections

4.3
,
4.4
,
4.5
, and
4.6
, explicit descriptions of the index sets are obtained by a study of the vertices in the affine apartments of split type
A_{n}
,
C_{n}
,
B_{n}
, and
D_{n}
respectively.

4.1
Reduce to irreducible ones

4.1.1
.

Suppose
\operatorname{{B}}
is decomposed into irreducible ones:

\operatorname{{B}}=\operatorname{{B}}_{1}\times\cdots\times\operatorname{{B}}_{m}.

Let
\operatorname{SSA}_{i}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
) be the simplicial surface area in
\operatorname{{B}}_{i}
.
Then, we have

\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)=\sum_{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}=r}\operatorname{SSA}_{1}\left(r_{1}\right)\cdots\operatorname{SSA}_{m}\left(r_{m}\right).

Proof.

Let
\partial_{i}(r)
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
) be the simplicial sphere in
\operatorname{{B}}_{i}
, then we need to show:

\partial(r)=\bigsqcup_{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}=r}\partial^{1}(r_{1})\times\cdots\times\partial^{m}(r_{m}).

This follows from the following lemma.
∎

4.1.2
.

Suppose
\operatorname{{B}}=\operatorname{{B}}_{1}\times\operatorname{{B}}_{2}
is a decomposition of Bruhat-Tits buildings.
Let
d_{i}(\>\cdot\>,\>\cdot\>)
(
i=1,2
) be the simplicial distance on
\operatorname{{B}}_{i}
and
\operatorname{pr}_{i}
the canonical projection from
\operatorname{{B}}
to
\operatorname{{B}}_{i}
. Then we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SimplicialDistanceDecom}d(x,y)=d_{1}(\operatorname{pr}_{1}\left(x\right),\operatorname{pr}_{1}\left(y\right))+d_{2}(\operatorname{pr}_{2}\left(x\right),\operatorname{pr}_{2}\left(y\right)).

(4.1.1)

Proof.

First, the left-hand side is no larger than the right-hand side by triangle inequality. To show the equality, we only need to show that if
x,y
are adjacent, then
\operatorname{pr}_{i}\left(x\right)=\operatorname{pr}_{i}\left(y\right)
holds either for
i=1
or
2
. Indeed,
x,y
are adjacent means that the segment
[x,y]
contains no vertex inside it and the set

\Psi=\left\{a\in\Phi\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}[x,y]\subseteq\partial\alpha\text{ for some }\alpha\in\Sigma\right\}

has rank one less than
\Phi
. Then, we must have
\Psi\cap\Phi_{i}=\Phi_{i}
for either
i=1
or
2
. Suppose
\Psi\cap\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{1}
.
For any
(a,\alpha)\in\operatorname{{E}}
,
\operatorname{pr}_{1}(\partial\alpha)
is either a wall in
\operatorname{{B}}_{1}
(if
a\in\Phi_{1}
) or the entire building (if
a\notin\Phi_{1}
).
Therefore,
\operatorname{pr}_{1}([x,y])
is a vertex and hence
\operatorname{pr}_{1}\left(x\right)=\operatorname{pr}_{1}\left(y\right)
.
∎

In particular, in order to compute the simplicial surface area in general, it suffices to do that for irreducible ones.
Since
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)=\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{SV}\left(r-1\right)
, the same holds for the simplicial volume.

4.2
Generality on vertices and the index sets

Proposition

4.1.1
suggests that, in order to compute the simplicial volume and simplicial surface area in general Bruhat-Tits buildings, we only need to do so in irreducible ones.
Now, suppose
\operatorname{{B}}
is an irreducible Bruhat-Tits building of split type
X_{n}
. Then we can deduce explicit descriptions of the index sets
B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
and
\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
as follows.

(i)
.

We first fix a realization of the affine apartment
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi\right)
. For this purpose, we start with the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Then the underlying Euclidean space
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
of
\Phi
is a certain subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
We use
(\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{m})
to denote the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
(\chi_{1},\cdots,\chi_{m})
the dual basis in
(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{\ast}
.
By an abuse of notation, we do not distinguish
\chi_{i}
from its restriction to
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
.
The standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
is denoted by
\lparen\>\cdot\>,\>\cdot\>\rparen
.
Then the underlying Euclidean affine space of
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi\right)
can be written as
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}=o+\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
.
We keep the convention that any linear function on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
is also viewed as an affine function on
\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
by taking
o
as the reference point.

The root system
\Phi
can be written in terms of linear functions on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
, and we thus obtain a concrete description of the
coroot lattice

\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}
in
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
.
This gives the translation group of the apartment
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi\right)
.
On the other hand, the action of the Weyl group
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
has a concrete geometric interpretation on
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
.
Then the affine Weyl group
W
is obtained as the semi-product of them.

(ii)
.

Next, we choose a Weyl chamber
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
and describe the associated system of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
, the system of simple roots
\Delta=\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\right\}
, and the highest root
a_{0}
. In addition, we express the sum of positive roots
2\rho
by simple roots.

(iii)
.

The
fundamental coweights

\omega_{1},\cdots,\omega_{n}
relative to
\Delta
are the vectors in
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
such that
a_{i}(\omega_{j})=\delta_{ij}
for all
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n
.
They form a basis of the
coweight lattice

\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
in
\mathbb{V}
.
Recall that special vertices in
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi\right)
are points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
such that
a(x)\in\Gamma
for all
a\in\Phi
.
Hence, the set of special vertices are precisely
o+\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Gamma
.

The above can be found in
[
3
]
*chap.VI, §4, no. 5-9.

(iv)
.

The simple roots
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
, together with the highest root
a_{0}
, give rise to a basis
\widetilde{\Delta}=\left\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\right\}
of
\Sigma
as in
2.3.3
.
Since we have assumed that
\operatorname{val}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
is normalized, we have
\alpha_{0}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}-a_{0}(x)+1\geqslant 0\right\}
.
Hence, the fundamental alcove
C
associated to
\widetilde{\Delta}
can be written as

C:=\left\{o+\mathbf{v}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\mathbf{v}\in\prescript{v\!}{}{C},a_{0}(\mathbf{v})<1\right\}.

Let
v_{0}=o,v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}
be its extreme points, where each
v_{i}
is opposite to the wall
\partial\alpha_{i}
.
Then we have (recall
Convention

2.3.4
for
h_{i}
)

a_{j}(v_{i})=h_{i}^{-1}\delta_{ij},\qquad\text{for all}\qquad 1\leqslant j\leqslant n

Therefore,
v_{i}=o+h_{i}^{-1}\omega_{i}
.

(v)
.

We follow
Convention

2.3.4
.
Then a vertex
has color
i
if it is conjugated to
v_{i}
by the affine Weyl group
W
.
Let
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
be the sets of vertices in
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi\right)
having color
i
.
Recall that
W
is the semi-product of
W_{o}\cong\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}
.
Then we have

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}=W_{o}.v_{i}+\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}.

Note that for any
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
, we have

r_{\alpha_{j}}(v_{i})=v_{i}+a_{j}(v_{i})a_{j}^{\vee}=v_{i}+h_{i}^{-1}\delta_{ij}a_{j}^{\vee}.

Since
W_{o}
is generated by
\left\{r_{\alpha_{j}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant j\leqslant n\right\}
, we see that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:VerticesBetweenCoroots}v_{i}+\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\subseteq v_{i}+h_{i}^{-1}\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}.

(4.2.1)

In particular, if
v_{i}
is a special vertex, then
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}=v_{i}+\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}
.
In general,
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
is obtained by computing
W_{o}.v_{i}
.
The set
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
of vertices in
\operatorname{{A}}\left(\Phi\right)
is then the disjoint union of
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
for
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
.
Let
h
be the maximum of
h_{1},\cdots,h_{n}
.
Then we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:VerticesBetweenCoweights}o+\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq o+\tfrac{1}{h}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}.

(4.2.2)

(vi)
.

The next step is to characterize the simplicial distance in terms of roots.
The goal is to prove
Theorem

1.3
.

We begin with some technical lemmas.

4.2.1
.

Suppose
\left\{a_{[i]}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i\leqslant n\right\}
is a linearly independent set of roots. Let
x,y
be two vertices such that
a_{[i]}(x)=a_{[i]}(y)=k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
expect
i=i_{0}
and that
|a_{[i_{0}]}(x)-a_{[i_{0}]}(y)|=h^{-1}
. Then
x
and
y
are adjacent.

Proof.

We need to show the segment
[x,y]
is an edge. Since it already lies in the intersection of the walls
\partial\alpha_{a_{[i]}-k_{i}}
(
i\neq i_{0}
), it remains to show that there is no vertex inside this segment. Suppose
x_{t}:=tx+(1-t)y
(
0<t<1
) is a vertex inside the segment
[x,y]
. By
Eq.

4.2.2
, we have
ha_{[i_{0}]}(x_{t})
and
ha_{[i_{0}]}(y)\in\mathbb{Z}
. But

ha_{[i_{0}]}(x_{t})=ha_{[i_{0}]}(y)+th(a_{[i_{0}]}(x)-a_{[i_{0}]}(y))=ha_{[i_{0}]}(y)\pm t.

Therefore,
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

This lemma will be used later. For now, we need another more specific lemma.

4.2.2
.

Suppose
h_{i_{0}}\leqslant 2
. Let
x,y
be two vertices such that
a_{i}(x)=a_{i}(y)=k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
except
i=i_{0}
and that either
a_{i_{0}}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
or
a_{i_{0}}(y)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then there is a path of length
m=\left\lvert a_{i_{0}}(x)-a_{i_{0}}(y)\right\rvert h_{i_{0}}
between them.

Proof.

We may assume that
a_{i_{0}}(y)\in\mathbb{Z}
and that
a_{i_{0}}(x)-a_{i_{0}}(y)>0
.
Consider the sequence
x_{j}=\tfrac{j}{m}x+(1-\tfrac{j}{m})y
(
0\leqslant j\leqslant m
). Then we have
a_{i}(x_{j})=k_{i}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
except
i=i_{0}
. Moreover, since
a_{i_{0}}(x_{j})=a_{i_{0}}(y)+\tfrac{j}{h_{i_{0}}}
, we have
a_{0}(x_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Now, we need to show that the segment
[x_{j-1},x_{j}]
is an edge for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant m
.
Since this segment already lies in the intersection of the walls
\partial\alpha_{a_{i}-k_{i}}
(
i\neq i_{0}
), it remains to show that there is no vertex inside it.
Suppose
x_{t}:=tx_{j-1}+(1-t)x_{j}
(
0<t<1
) is a vertex inside the segment
[x_{j-1},x_{j}]
. Then there must be another root
a=c_{1}a_{1}+\cdots+c_{n}a_{n}
linearly independent of
a_{i}
(
i\neq i_{0}
) such that
a(x_{t})\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
c_{i_{0}}a_{i_{0}}(x_{t})
has to be a nonzero integer. Now, we have

\displaystyle c_{i_{0}}a_{i_{0}}\left(x_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{i_{0}}a_{i_{0}}\left(tx_{j-1}+(1-t)x_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{i_{0}}a_{i_{0}}\left(y+\frac{t(j-1)+(1-t)j}{m}(x-y)\right)

\displaystyle=c_{i_{0}}a_{i_{0}}(y)+\frac{c_{i_{0}}}{h_{i_{0}}}(j-t).

Therefore, we have
\tfrac{c_{i_{0}}}{h_{i_{0}}}(j-t)\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By the basic property of the highest root, we must have
1\leqslant c_{i_{0}}\leqslant h_{i_{0}}
.
If
c_{i_{0}}=h_{i_{0}}
, then
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, a contradiction.
If
c_{i_{0}}<h_{i_{0}}
, since we have assumed that
h_{i_{0}}\leqslant 2
, we must have
c_{i_{0}}=1
. Then
\tfrac{1}{h_{i_{0}}}(j-t)\in\mathbb{Z}
implies that
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, a contradiction.
∎

Now, we can prove the following weaker result:

4.2.3
.

Suppose
h\leqslant 2
. Then, for any
special
vertex
x\in\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
, we have

d(x,o)\leqslant r\iff a_{0}(x)\leqslant r.

Proof.

First, since any edge intersects with a wall by a vertex, we must have

d(o,x)\geqslant a_{0}(x).

This shows the only if part.

Next, we consider the sequence
x_{i}=x_{i-1}+a_{i}(x)\omega_{i}
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
) with
x_{0}=o
. Then all
x_{i}
are special vertices and for the successive vertices
x_{i-1}
and
x_{i}
have the property that
a_{j}(x_{i-1})=a_{j}(x_{i})
for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
except
j=i
.
Hence, by
Lemma

4.2.2
, there is a path of length
h_{i}a_{i}(x)
between them. In this way, we obtain a path from
o
to
x
of length

h_{1}a_{1}(x)+\cdots+h_{n}a_{n}(x)=a_{0}(x).

This proves the lemma.
∎

The argument for the only if part works for
Theorem

1.3
. As for the if part, we can construct a suitable path from
x
to a special vertex
x_{0}\in\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
, verifying that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SpecialX0inLemma}d(x_{0},x)\leqslant\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil-a_{0}(x_{0}).

(4.2.3)

Indeed, if such a path exists, by
Lemma

4.2.3
, we have

d(o,x)\leqslant d(o,x_{0})+d(x_{0},x)\leqslant a_{0}(x_{0})+\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil-a_{0}(x_{0})=\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil\leqslant r.

This proves
Theorem

1.3
.

(vii)
.

Now, we can describe the index set
B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
and
\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
as follows.
First, by
Theorem

1.3
, we see that

\displaystyle B(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\cap\alpha_{-a_{0}+r},

\displaystyle\partial(r)\cap\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\cap(\alpha_{-a_{0}+r}\setminus\alpha_{-a_{0}+r-1}).

For a type
I
, let
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}_{I}
denote the face of
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
having type
I
:

\prescript{v\!}{}{C}_{I}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\forall a\in I,a(\mathbf{v})=0;\forall a\in\Delta\setminus I,a(\mathbf{v})>0\right\}.

Recall that for any point
x
, its type is
I_{o,x}=\left\{a\in\Delta\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}a(x)=0\right\}
.
Hence, a point
x\in\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
has type
I
if and only if
x\in o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}_{I}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle=(o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}_{I})\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\cap\alpha_{-a_{0}+r},

(4.2.4)

\displaystyle\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle=(o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}_{I})\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\cap(\alpha_{-a_{0}+r}\setminus\alpha_{-a_{0}+r-1}).

(4.2.5)

The
\dagger
-variants are obtained similarly.

By
Convention

2.3.2
, a point
x
has type
I
if and only if it is of the form

x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}.

Then the condition
x\in o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}_{I}
can be interpreted as “
c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}>0
”.
Next,
x
is a
special
vertex if and only if
c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\dagger=
“being special”. Then, by
Eqs.

4.2.4
and
4.2.5
, we have the following explicit descriptions:

\displaystyle B_{\dagger}(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle=\left\{x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
h_{\ell_{1}}c_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell_{t}}c_{t}\leqslant r\end{array}\right\},

\displaystyle\partial_{\dagger}(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle=\left\{x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
h_{\ell_{1}}c_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell_{t}}c_{t}=r\end{array}\right\},

where
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
denotes the set of positive integers.

4.3
Vertices in the apartment
\operatorname{{A}}\left(A_{n}\right)

(i)
.

The underlying Euclidean vector space
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
is the following:

\mathbb{V}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(\mathbf{v})+\cdots+\chi_{n+1}(\mathbf{v})=0\right\}.

Its dual space is
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}=(\mathbb{R}\chi_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{R}\chi_{n+1})/\mathbb{R}(\chi_{1}+\cdots+\chi_{n+1})
.
After identifying each
\chi_{i}
with its restriction to
\mathbb{V}
, the root system can be written as follows:

\Phi:=\left\{\chi_{i}-\chi_{j}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n+1\right\}.

Then the coroot lattice
\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}
is the restriction of the standard lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
to
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
, and the Weyl group
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
acts on
\mathbb{V}
as permutations of coordinates.

(ii)
.

We can choose the following Weyl chamber
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
:

\prescript{v\!}{}{C}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{V}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{i}(\mathbf{v})>\chi_{j}(\mathbf{v})\text{ for all }1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n+1\right\}.

Then the system of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
associated to
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
is the following:

\Phi^{+}:=\left\{\chi_{i}-\chi_{j}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n+1\right\}.

Among them, the simple roots are the following:

\displaystyle a_{i}

\displaystyle:=\chi_{i}-\chi_{i+1}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n)}

Using the basis
\Delta=\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\right\}
, the positive roots can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\chi_{i}-\chi_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{j-1}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n+1)}

(4.3.1)

Among them, the highest root
a_{0}
relative to
\Delta
is

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:HighestRoot_{A}n}a_{0}:=\chi_{1}-\chi_{n+1}=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}.

(4.3.2)

Moreover, the sum of positive roots is

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:2rho_{A}n}2\rho=\sum_{i=1}^{n}i(n+1-i)a_{i}.

(4.3.3)

(iii)
.

The fundamental coweights relative to
\Delta
are the following:

\displaystyle\omega_{i}

\displaystyle:=(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i})-\tfrac{i}{n+1}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n+1}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n)}

Then the special vertices are
o+\mathbb{Z}\omega_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\omega_{n}
.

(iv)
.

Associated to
\Delta
, the fundamental alcove
C
can be written as follows:

C:=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x)>\cdots>\chi_{n+1}(x),\chi_{1}(x)-\chi_{n+1}(x)<1\right\}.

The extreme points of
C
other than
v_{0}=o
are the following:

\displaystyle v_{i}

\displaystyle:=o+\omega_{i}=o+(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i})-\tfrac{i}{n+1}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n+1}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n)}

Note that all of them are special vertices.

(v)
.

Since each
v_{i}
is a special vertex, we have

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}=v_{i}+\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{j}(x)+\tfrac{i}{n+1}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ for all }1\leqslant j\leqslant n+1\right\}.

In particular, all vertices are special and hence
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}=o+\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
.

(vi)
.

Since all vertices are special, the
A_{n}
case of
Theorem

1.3
follows from
Lemma

4.2.3
.

(vii)
.

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
Since all vertices are special, by
(vii)
and
(vii)
, we have the following explicit descriptions:

\displaystyle B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle=\left\{x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}\leqslant r\end{array}\right\},

\displaystyle\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle=\left\{x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r\end{array}\right\}.

4.4
Vertices in the apartment
\operatorname{{A}}\left(C_{n}\right)
(
n\geqslant 2
)

(i)
.

The underlying Euclidean vector space
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
is the entire
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and its dual space
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is thus spanned by the coordinate functions
\chi_{1},\cdots,\chi_{n}
.
Then the root system can be written as follows:

\Phi:=\left\{\pm\chi_{i}\pm\chi_{j}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\right\}\cup\left\{\pm 2\chi_{i}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i\leqslant n\right\}.

Then its coroot lattice
\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}
is precisely the standard lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and the Weyl group
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
acts on
\mathbb{V}
as permutations and sign changes of coordinates.

(ii)
.

We can choose the following Weyl chamber
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
:

\prescript{v\!}{}{C}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{V}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{i}(\mathbf{v})>\chi_{j}(\mathbf{v})>0\text{ for all }1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\right\}.

Then the system of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
associated to
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
is the following:

\Phi^{+}:=\left\{\chi_{i}\pm\chi_{j}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\right\}\cup\left\{2\chi_{i}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i\leqslant n\right\}.

Among them, the simple roots are the following:

\displaystyle a_{i}

\displaystyle:=\chi_{i}-\chi_{i+1},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle:=2\chi_{n}.

Using the basis
\Delta=\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\right\}
, the positive roots can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\chi_{i}-\chi_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{j-1},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle\chi_{i}+\chi_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{j-1}+2a_{j}+\cdots+2a_{n-1}+a_{n},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n)}

(4.4.1)

\displaystyle 2\chi_{i}

\displaystyle=2a_{i}+\cdots+2a_{n-1}+a_{n}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n)}

Among them, the highest root
a_{0}
relative to
\Delta
is

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:HighestRoot_{C}n}a_{0}:=2\chi_{1}=2a_{1}+\cdots+2a_{n-1}+a_{n}.

(4.4.2)

Moreover, the sum of positive roots is

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:2rho_{C}n}2\rho=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}i(2n+1-i)a_{i}+\binom{n+1}{2}a_{n}.

(4.4.3)

(iii)
.

The fundamental coweights relative to
\Delta
are the following:

\displaystyle\omega_{i}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle\omega_{n}

\displaystyle:=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n}).

Hence, the coweight lattice
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
is
\mathbb{Z}^{n}+\mathbb{Z}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n})
.

(iv)
.

Associated to
\Delta
, the fundamental alcove
C
can be written as follows:

C:=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\tfrac{1}{2}>\chi_{1}(x)>\cdots>\chi_{n}(x)>0\right\}.

The extreme points of
C
other than
v_{0}=o
are the following:

\displaystyle v_{i}

\displaystyle:=o+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{i}=o+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle v_{n}

\displaystyle:=o+\omega_{n}=o+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n}).

Note that
v_{n}
is a special vertex, while
v_{i}
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
) are not special.

(v)
.

For each
i
, by
(i)
,
W_{o}.v_{i}
consists of the points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
whose coordinates are either
0
or
\pm\tfrac{1}{2}
and exactly
i
of them are nonzero. Then we have

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\text{ and exactly $i$ of them are non-integers }\right\}.

Hence, we have
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}=o+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
In particular, we have
a_{i}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
and
a_{n}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Conversely, if
a_{i}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
and
a_{n}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
, then we can see that
x-o\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
Let
\omega_{i}^{\prime}
denote
h_{i}^{-1}\omega_{i}
.
Then we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:VerticesCn}\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}=o+\mathbb{Z}\omega_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}\omega_{n}^{\prime}.

(4.4.4)

(vi)
.

Not every vertex is special. We thus need the following notion:

4.4.1
.

Let
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
be a point.
Then an index
j\in\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\}
is called a
jump
if
a_{j}(x)\notin\mathbb{Z}
.
The set of jumps of
x
is denoted by
J_{x}
.

Let
x
be a vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
with jumps
j_{1},\cdots,j_{s}
, ordered from smallest to largest.
Note that we must have
j_{s}<n
.
Let
x_{i}=x-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{j_{1}}+\cdots+\omega_{j_{i}})
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant s
.
Then the following lemma tells us that
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
are adjacent vertices.

4.4.2
.

Let
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
be a vertex and
j_{1}
its smallest jump. Then
y=x-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j_{1}}
is a vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
adjacent to
x
.

Proof.

First note that, by
(v)
, we have
a_{j}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
for all
j\in J_{x}
. Hence,
J_{y}=J_{x}\setminus\left\{j_{1}\right\}
.
We define the roots
a_{[i]}
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
) as follows.
If
i\in J_{y}
, let

a_{[i]}=2a_{i}+\cdots+2a_{n-1}+a_{n}.

Otherwise, let
a_{[i]}=a_{i}
.
Then
a_{[1]},\cdots,a_{[n]}
are linearly independent positive roots, and
a_{[1]}(y),\cdots,a_{[n]}(y)
are non-negative integers.
Hence,
y
is a vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
.
Since
x-y=\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j_{1}}
, we have
a_{[i]}(x)=a_{[i]}(y)
for all
i
except
i=j_{1}
and
a_{[j_{1}]}(x)-a_{[j_{1}]}(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}
.
Then
Lemma

4.2.1
applies to the roots
a_{[i]}
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
) and the vertices
x
and
y
.
∎

Then the sequence
(x,x_{1},\cdots,x_{s})
forms a path from
x
to
x_{s}
of length
s
in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
. Since
x_{s}
has no jumps, it is a special vertex. Moreover, we have

a_{0}(x)-a_{0}(x_{s})=\tfrac{1}{2}a_{0}(\omega_{j_{1}}+\cdots+\omega_{j_{s}})=s.

Then this
x_{s}
is the expected
x_{0}
verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
.
Thus,
Theorem

1.3
is proved.

(vii)
.

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
By introducing
\omega_{i}^{\prime}=h_{i}^{-1}\omega_{i}
, we can write a point
x
having type
I
as follows:

x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}^{\prime}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}^{\prime}.

By
Eq.

4.4.4
, such an
x
is a vertex if and only if
c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, by
Eqs.

4.2.4
and
4.2.5
, we have the following explicit description:

\displaystyle B(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle=\left\{x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}^{\prime}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}^{\prime}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}\leqslant r\end{array}\right\},

\displaystyle\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)

\displaystyle=\left\{x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}^{\prime}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}^{\prime}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r\end{array}\right\}.

4.5
Vertices in the apartment
\operatorname{{A}}\left(B_{n}\right)
(
n\geqslant 3
)

(i)
.

The underlying Euclidean vector space
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
is the entire
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and its dual space
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is thus spanned by the coordinate functions
\chi_{1},\cdots,\chi_{n}
.
Then the root system can be written as follows:

\Phi:=\left\{\pm\chi_{i}\pm\chi_{j}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\right\}\cup\left\{\pm\chi_{i}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i\leqslant n\right\}.

Then its coroot lattice is the following sublattice of the standard lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\lparen\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}\rparen\in 2\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

The Weyl group
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
acts on
\mathbb{V}
as permutations and sign changes of coordinates.

(ii)
.

We can choose the following Weyl chamber
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
:

\prescript{v\!}{}{C}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{V}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{i}(\mathbf{v})>\chi_{j}(\mathbf{v})>0\text{ for all }1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\right\}.

Then the system of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
associated to
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
is the following:

\Phi^{+}:=\left\{\chi_{i}\pm\chi_{j}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\right\}\cup\left\{\chi_{i}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i\leqslant n\right\}.

Among them, the simple roots are the following:

\displaystyle a_{i}

\displaystyle:=\chi_{i}-\chi_{i+1},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle:=\chi_{n}.

Using the basis
\Delta=\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\right\}
, the positive roots can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\chi_{i}-\chi_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{j-1},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle\chi_{i}+\chi_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{j-1}+2a_{j}+\cdots+2a_{n},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n)}

(4.5.1)

\displaystyle\chi_{i}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{n}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n)}

Among them, the highest root
a_{0}
relative to
\Delta
is

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:HighestRoot_{B}n}a_{0}:=\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=a_{1}+2a_{2}+\cdots+2a_{n}.

(4.5.2)

Moreover, the sum of positive roots is

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:2rho_{B}n}2\rho=\sum_{i=1}^{n}i(2n-i)a_{i}.

(4.5.3)

(iii)
.

The fundamental coweights relative to
\Delta
are the following:

\displaystyle\omega_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n)}

Hence, the coweight lattice
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
is precisely the standard lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

(iv)
.

Associated to
\Delta
, the fundamental alcove
C
can be written as follows:

C=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}\chi_{1}(x)>\cdots>\chi_{n}(x)>0,\\
\chi_{1}(x)+\chi_{2}(x)<1\end{array}\right\}.

The extreme points of
C
other than
v_{0}=o
are the following:

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle=o+\omega_{1}=o+\mathbf{e}_{1},

\displaystyle v_{i}

\displaystyle=o+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{i}=o+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(2\leqslant i\leqslant n)}

Note that
v_{1}
is a special vertex, while
v_{i}
(
2\leqslant i\leqslant n
) are not special.

(v)
.

First, apply the affine Weyl group
W
to
v_{0}
, we have

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{0}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n}(x)\in\mathbb{Z},\chi_{1}(x)+\cdots+\chi_{n}(x)\text{ is even }\right\}.

Applying
W_{o}
to
v_{1}
, we see that
W_{o}.v_{1}
consists of the points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
having one coordinate being
1
or
-1
and all others are
0
. Then we have:

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{1}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n}(x)\in\mathbb{Z},\chi_{1}(x)+\cdots+\chi_{n}(x)\text{ is odd }\right\}

For each
i>1
,
W_{o}.v_{i}
consists of the points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
whose coordinates are either
0
or
\pm\tfrac{1}{2}
and exactly
i
of them are nonzero. Then we have:

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\text{ and exactly $i$ of them are non-integers }\right\}.

Hence, the vertices are all the point
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
such that
\chi_{j}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
for all
j
and the number of non-integer coordinates is not
1
. In particular, we have

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq o+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}=o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}.

However, the equality doesn’t hold. For instance, the point
o+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1}
is clearly not a vertex. Another example is
o+\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{i-1}+\omega_{i})
where all
\chi_{j}(x)
are integers except
j=i
.

To better describe the vertices, we need the notion introduced in
Definition

4.4.1
.
Then the complement of
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
in
o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
can be described as follows.

4.5.1
.

A point
x\in o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
belongs to the complement if and only if either
J_{x}=\left\{j_{1},j_{2}\right\}
and
j_{2}-j_{1}=1
, or
J_{x}=\left\{1\right\}
.

We will use
\Xi
to denote the set of points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
having the property in the lemma.

Proof.

First, points in
\Xi
cannot be vertices.
If
J_{x}=\left\{1\right\}
, then
x
is not a vertex since all
\chi_{j}(x)
are integers except
j=1
.
If
J_{x}=\left\{j_{1},j_{2}\right\}
and
j_{2}-j_{1}=1
, then
x
is not a vertex since all
\chi_{j}(x)
are integers except
j=j_{2}
.

Conversely, suppose
x\in o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
and
x\notin\Xi
, then there are four cases:

1.

\left\lvert J_{x}\right\rvert\geqslant 3
. Then at least two coordinates of
x
are non-integers.

2.

J_{x}=\left\{j_{1},j_{2}\right\}
and
j_{2}-j_{1}>1
.
Then
\chi_{j_{1}+1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{j_{2}}(x)
are non-integers.

3.

J_{x}=\left\{j_{1}\right\}
and
j_{1}>1
.
Then
\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{j_{1}}(x)
are non-integers.

4.

x
has no jumps. Then it is a special vertex.

In any of above cases,
x
is a vertex by our characterization.
∎

We illustrate the structure of
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
by the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Xi\cap o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigcup\limits_{i=2,\cdots,n}\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}}}

where
\dagger
denotes “being special”.

(vi)
.

Let
x\in\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
be a vertex having jumps
j_{1},\cdots,j_{s}
, ordered from smallest to largest. To construct a path between
x
and a special vertex
x_{0}
in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
, we need the following lemmas.

4.5.2
.

Suppose either
j_{s}-j_{s-1}>1
or
s>3
.
Then
y=x-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j_{1}}
is a vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
adjacent to
x
.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
, we have
J_{y}=J_{x}\setminus\left\{j_{1}\right\}
.
We define the roots
a_{[j]}
(
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
) as follows. First, let

a_{[j_{s}]}=\begin{cases}a_{j_{s}-1}+2a_{j_{s}}+\cdots+2a_{n}&if $j_{s}-j_{s-1}>1$,\\
a_{j_{s-2}}+\cdots+a_{j_{s}-1}+2a_{j_{s}}+\cdots+2a_{n}&otherwise.\end{cases}

For
2\leqslant i\leqslant s-1
, let

a_{[j_{i}]}=a_{j_{i}}+\cdots+a_{j_{i+1}}.

Finally, if
j\notin J_{y}
, let
a_{[j]}=a_{j}
.
Then
a_{[1]},\cdots,a_{[n]}
are linearly independent positive roots, and
a_{[1]}(y),\cdots,a_{[n]}(y)
are non-negative integers.
Hence,
y
is a vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
.
Since
x-y=\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j_{1}}
, we have
a_{[j]}(x)=a_{[j]}(y)
for all
j
except
j=j_{1}
and
a_{[j_{1}]}(x)-a_{[j_{1}]}(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}
.
Then
Lemma

4.2.1
applies to the roots
a_{[j]}
(
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
) and the vertices
x
and
y
.
∎

Let
y=x-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j_{1}}
be as in
Lemma

4.5.2
. Then we must have
a_{0}(y)\in\mathbb{Z}
and

\displaystyle\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil-a_{0}(y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}a_{0}(x)-a_{0}(y)&if $j_{1}>1$,\\
a_{0}(x)+\tfrac{1}{2}-a_{0}(y)&if $j_{1}=1$;\end{cases}

\displaystyle a_{0}(x)-a_{0}(y)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}a_{0}(\omega_{j_{1}})=\begin{cases}1&if $j_{1}>1$,\\
\tfrac{1}{2}&if $j_{1}=1$.\end{cases}

Hence,
\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil-a_{0}(y)=1
.
By repeating using
Lemma

4.5.2
, we can reduce our problem to the case where
s=3
, or further
s=1
if we start with
j_{s}-j_{s-1}>1
.

Now, we may assume either
s\leqslant 3
with
j_{s}-j_{s-1}=1
or
s=1
.

4.5.3
.

Suppose
s=3
and
j_{1}>1
. Then
x_{0}=x-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{j_{1}}-\omega_{j_{2}}+\omega_{j_{3}})
is a special vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
adjacent to
x
and verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
.

Proof.

First note that
a_{1}(x_{0}),\cdots,a_{n}(x_{0})
are non-negative integers. Hence,
x_{0}
is a special vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
.
Since
j_{1}>1
, we have
a_{0}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
and

a_{0}(x)-a_{0}(x_{0})=\tfrac{1}{2}a_{0}(\omega_{j_{1}}-\omega_{j_{2}}+\omega_{j_{3}})=1.

Hence, it remains to show that
x_{0}
is adjacent to
x
.

To do this, we define the roots
a_{[j]}
(
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
) as follows.
If
j\neq j_{2},j_{3}
, let
a_{[j]}=a_{j}
. Otherwise, let

\displaystyle a_{[j_{2}]}

\displaystyle=a_{j_{1}}+\cdots+a_{j_{2}},

\displaystyle a_{[j_{3}]}

\displaystyle=a_{j_{2}}+\cdots+a_{j_{3}}.

Then
a_{[1]},\cdots,a_{[n]}
are linearly independent.
Since
x-x_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{j_{1}}-\omega_{j_{2}}+\omega_{j_{3}})
, we have
a_{[j]}(x)=a_{[j]}(x_{0})
for all
j
except
j=j_{1}
and
a_{[j_{1}]}(x)-a_{[j_{1}]}(x_{0})=\tfrac{1}{2}
. Then
Lemma

4.2.1
applies to the roots
a_{[j]}
(
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
) and the vertices
x
and
x_{0}
.
∎

4.5.4
.

Suppose
s=3
,
j_{3}-j_{2}=1
and
j_{1}=1
, then
y=x-\tfrac{1}{2}(-\omega_{j_{1}}+\omega_{j_{2}})
is a vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
adjacent to
x
.

Proof.

We define the roots
a_{[j]}
(
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
) as follows.
If
j\neq j_{2},j_{3}
, let
a_{[j]}=a_{j}
. Otherwise, let

\displaystyle a_{[j_{2}]}

\displaystyle=a_{j_{1}}+\cdots+a_{j_{2}},

\displaystyle a_{[j_{3}]}

\displaystyle=a_{j_{1}}+\cdots+a_{j_{2}}+2a_{j_{3}}+\cdots+2a_{n}.

Then
a_{[1]},\cdots,a_{[n]}
are linearly independent positive roots, and
a_{[1]}(y),\cdots,a_{[n]}(y)
are non-negative integers.
Hence,
y
is a vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
.
Since
x-y=\tfrac{1}{2}(-\omega_{j_{1}}+\omega_{j_{2}})
, we have
a_{[j]}(x)=a_{[j]}(y)
for all
j
except
j=j_{1}
and
a_{[j_{1}]}(x)-a_{[j_{1}]}(y)=-\tfrac{1}{2}
.
Then
Lemma

4.2.1
applies to the roots
a_{[j]}
(
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
) and the vertices
x
and
y
.
∎

Let
y=x-\tfrac{1}{2}(-\omega_{j_{1}}+\omega_{j_{2}})
be as in
Lemma

4.5.4
. Then the only jump of
y
is
j_{3}>1
.
Hence,
a_{0}(y)\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil-a_{0}(y)=1
.
Therefore,
Lemma

4.5.4
reduces our problem to the case where
s=1
.

Note that, by
Lemma

4.5.1
,
s=2
and
j_{s}-j_{s-1}=1
contradict to each other. Therefore, we may assume
s=1
now. By
Lemma

4.5.1
again, we must have
j_{1}>1
.
Let
x_{0}=x-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j_{1}}
.
Then it is a special vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
since
a_{1}(x_{0}),\cdots,a_{n}(x_{0})
are non-negative integers.
Applying
Lemma

4.2.1
to the simple roots
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
and the vertices
x
and
x_{0}
, we see that they are adjacent. Moreover, we have
a_{0}(x)-a_{0}(x_{0})=1
verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
.
This finishes the proof of
Theorem

1.3
.

(vii)
.

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
For any set
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}
of points, we introduce the following subsets:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)

\displaystyle:=(o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}_{I})\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{X}},

(4.5.4)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I,r\right)

\displaystyle:=(o+\prescript{v\!}{}{C}_{I})\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\cap(\alpha_{-a_{0}+r}\setminus\alpha_{-a_{0}+r-1}).

Then we have
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I,r\right)=\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
by
Eqs.

4.2.4
and
4.2.5
.

By introducing
\omega_{i}^{\prime}=h_{i}^{-1}\omega_{i}
, we can write a point
x
having type
I
as follows:

x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}^{\prime}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}^{\prime}.

Then
a_{0}(x)\leqslant r
if and only if
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}\leqslant r
.

Consider the set
o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
and recall that
h_{1}=1
while
h_{2}=\cdots=h_{n}=2
.
Let
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
be the set
o+\mathbb{Z}\omega_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\omega_{n}^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1}
. Then we have

o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}.

This gives a superset of the
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
.

By
Lemma

4.5.1
, the complement of
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
in
o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
is the restriction of
\Xi
. Inspired by this and
Definition

4.4.1
, we can consider the following sets for each
J\subseteq\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\}
:

\displaystyle\Xi_{J}

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}J_{x}=J\right\}

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}

\displaystyle:=\Xi_{J}\cap o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}.

Then we have

o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}\setminus\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1,2\right\}}\cup\cdots\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-1,n\right\}}.

Note that, for any
J
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:XJfromX0Bn}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}-\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}.

(4.5.5)

Moreover, it is clear that
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}
is precisely
o+\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
, the set of special vertices.

Next, we consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)
for above sets.
First, if
\ell_{1}>1
, then
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)
. Otherwise,
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)\neq\emptyset
.
For any
J
, it is clear that
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)\neq\emptyset
if and only if
I\cap J=\emptyset
. If this is the case, we have the following refinement of
Eq.

4.5.5
:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:XIJfromXI0Bn}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I\right)-\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}.

(4.5.6)

Finally, we consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I,r\right)
. First, it is clear that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:IndexBsrciBn:pu0}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I,r\right)=\left\{x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}^{\prime}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}^{\prime}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}>0,\\
c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r\end{array}\right\}.

(4.5.7)

Also note that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:IndexBsrciBn:pu1}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I,r\right)=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I,r\right)-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1}.

(4.5.8)

Then we need to work out
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right)
. For
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I,r\right)
, an explicit description is given in
(vii)
and
(vii)
. For general
J
, we have the following refinement of
Eq.

4.5.6
:

4.5.5
.

Suppose
I\cap J=\emptyset
.
Then we have

\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right)=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I,r+\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)\right)-\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j},

where
\delta(J)
is defined as follows:

\delta(J)=\begin{cases}1&if $1\in J$,\\
0&otherwise.\end{cases}

Proof.

By
Eq.

4.5.6
, it suffices to show that for any
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I,r\right)
,

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:tag:lem:XIJfromXI0Bn}\left\lceil a_{0}\left(x-\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}\right)\right\rceil=a_{0}(x)-\left\lvert J\right\rvert+\delta(J).

(
\ast
)

Note that
a_{0}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
and that

a_{0}\left(\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}\right)=\begin{cases}\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\tfrac{1}{2}&if $1\in J$,\\
\left\lvert J\right\rvert&otherwise.\end{cases}

Then
Eq.

∗

follows.
∎

We illustrate above discussions by the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I,r\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigcup\limits_{J=\left\{2,3\right\},\cdots,\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\Xi_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I,r\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigcup\limits_{J=\left\{2,3\right\},\cdots,\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I,r\right)=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I,r\right)}}

Figure 4.5.1:
Vertices of type
I
in
\operatorname{{A}}\left(B_{n}\right)
(
\ell_{1}>1
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I,r\right)\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I,r\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigcup\limits_{J=\left\{1\right\},\left\{1,2\right\},\cdots,\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\Xi_{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I,r\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigcup\limits_{J=\left\{1\right\},\left\{1,2\right\},\cdots,\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I,r\right)=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I,r\right)}}

Figure 4.5.2:
Vertices of type
I
in
\operatorname{{A}}\left(B_{n}\right)
(
\ell_{1}=1
)

4.6
Vertices in the apartment
\operatorname{{A}}\left(D_{n}\right)
(
n\geqslant 4
)

(i)
.

The underlying Euclidean vector space
\operatorname{\mathbb{V}}
is the entire
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and its dual space
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is thus spanned by the coordinate functions
\chi_{1},\cdots,\chi_{n}
.
Then the root system can be written as follows:

\Phi=\left\{\pm\chi_{i}\pm\chi_{j}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\right\}.

Then its coroot lattice is the following sublattice of the standard lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\operatorname{\mathcal{Q}}^{\vee}:=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\lparen\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}\rparen\in 2\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

The Weyl group
\operatorname{\prescript{v}{}{\mathit{W}}}
acts on
\mathbb{V}
as permutations and even number of sign changes of coordinates.

(ii)
.

We can choose the following Weyl chamber
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
:

\prescript{v\!}{}{C}=\left\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{V}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{i}(\mathbf{v})>\left\lvert\chi_{j}(\mathbf{v})\right\rvert\text{ for all }1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\right\}.

Then the system of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
associated to
\prescript{v\!}{}{C}
is the following:

\Phi^{+}:=\left\{\chi_{i}\pm\chi_{j}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\right\}.

Among them, the simple roots are the following:

\displaystyle a_{i}

\displaystyle:=\chi_{i}-\chi_{i+1},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle:=\chi_{n-1}+\chi_{n}.

Using the basis
\Delta=\left\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\right\}
, the positive roots can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\chi_{i}-\chi_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{j-1},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle\chi_{i}+\chi_{n}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{n-2}+a_{n},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle\chi_{i}+\chi_{n-1}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{n-2}+a_{n-1}+a_{n},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n-2)}

(4.6.1)

\displaystyle\chi_{i}+\chi_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{i}+\cdots+a_{j-1}

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n-2)}

\displaystyle\quad+2a_{j}+\cdots+2a_{n-2}+a_{n-1}+a_{n}.

Among them, the highest root
a_{0}
relative to
\Delta
is

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:HighestRoot_{D}n}a_{0}:=\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}=a_{1}+2a_{2}+\cdots+2a_{n-2}+a_{n-1}+a_{n}.

(4.6.2)

Moreover, the sum of positive roots is

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:2rho_{D}n}2\rho=\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}i(2n-1-i)a_{i}+\binom{n}{2}(a_{n-1}+a_{n}).

(4.6.3)

(iii)
.

The fundamental coweights relative to
\Delta
are the following:

\displaystyle\omega_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i},

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant n-2)}

\displaystyle\omega_{n-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n-1}-\mathbf{e}_{n}),

\displaystyle\omega_{n}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n}).

Hence, the coweight lattice
\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
is
\mathbb{Z}^{n}+\mathbb{Z}\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n})
.

(iv)
.

Associated to
\Delta
, the fundamental alcove
C
can be written as follows:

C=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}\chi_{1}(x)>\cdots>\chi_{n-1}(x)>\left\lvert\chi_{n}(x)\right\rvert,\\
\chi_{1}(x)+\chi_{2}(x)<1\end{array}\right\}.

The extreme points of
C
other than
v_{0}=o
are the following:

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle=o+\omega_{1}=o+\mathbf{e}_{1},

\displaystyle v_{i}

\displaystyle=o+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{i}=o+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(2\leqslant i\leqslant n-2)}

\displaystyle v_{n-1}

\displaystyle=o+\omega_{n-1}=o+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n-1}-\mathbf{e}_{n}),

\displaystyle v_{n}

\displaystyle=o+\omega_{n}=o+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{n}).

Note that
v_{1},v_{n-1},v_{n}
are special vertices, while
v_{i}
(
2\leqslant i\leqslant n-2
) are not special.

(v)
.

First, apply the affine Weyl group
W
to
v_{0}
, we have

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{0}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n}(x)\in\mathbb{Z},\chi_{1}(x)+\cdots+\chi_{n}(x)\text{ is even }\right\}.

Applying
W_{o}
to
v_{1}
, we see that
W_{o}.v_{1}
consists of the points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
having one coordinate being
1
or
-1
and all others are
0
. Then we have:

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{1}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n}(x)\in\mathbb{Z},\chi_{1}(x)+\cdots+\chi_{n}(x)\text{ is odd }\right\}

For each
1<i<n-1
,
W_{o}.v_{i}
consists of the points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
whose coordinates are either
0
or
\pm\tfrac{1}{2}
and exactly
i
of them are nonzero. Then we have:

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\text{ and exactly $i$ of them are non-integers }\right\}.

Then
W_{o}.v_{n-1}
consists of the points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
whose coordinates are
\pm\tfrac{1}{2}
and odd numbers of them are negative. Then we have:

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{n-1}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\setminus\mathbb{Z},\chi_{1}(x)+\cdots+\chi_{n}(x)-\tfrac{n}{2}\text{ is odd }\right\}

Then
W_{o}.v_{n}
consists of the points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
whose coordinates are
\pm\tfrac{1}{2}
and even numbers of them are negative. Then we have:

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{n}=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\setminus\mathbb{Z},\chi_{1}(x)+\cdots+\chi_{n}(x)-\tfrac{n}{2}\text{ is even }\right\}

Hence, the vertices are all the point
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
such that
\chi_{j}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
for all
j
and the number of non-integer coordinates is neither
1
nor
n-1
. In particular, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:verticesInZn}\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\subsetneq o+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\subsetneq o+\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}.

(4.6.4)

Let
\omega_{i}^{\prime}=h_{i}^{-1}\omega_{i}
. Consider the following sets:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}

\displaystyle:=o+\mathbb{Z}\omega_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+\mathbb{Z}\omega_{n}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n}),

(4.6.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}.

4.6.1
.

We have
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}=o+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}\subseteq o+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
Conversely, we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{e}_{1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1},

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{e}_{2}

\displaystyle=\omega_{2}^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1},

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{{}={}}][c]{\vdots}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{e}_{i}

\displaystyle=\omega_{i}^{\prime}-\omega_{i-1}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{{}={}}][c]{\vdots}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{e}_{n-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n})-\omega_{n-2}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{e}_{n}

\displaystyle=\omega_{n}-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n}).

Then the statement follows.
∎

To better describe the vertices, we need the notion introduced in
Definition

4.4.1
.
Then the complement of
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
in
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
can be described as follows.

4.6.2
.

A point
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
is not a vertex if and only if either
J_{x}=\left\{j_{1},j_{2}\right\}
and
j_{2}-j_{1}=1
, or
J_{x}=\left\{1\right\}
.

We will use
\Xi^{(0)}
to denote the set of points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
having the property in the lemma.

Proof.

First, points in
\Xi^{(0)}
cannot be vertices.
If
J_{x}=\left\{1\right\}
, then
x
is not a vertex since either all
\chi_{j}(x)
are integers except
j=1
(when
a_{n-1}(x)+a_{n}(x)
is even), or all
\chi_{j}(x)
are non-integers except
j=1
(when
a_{n-1}(x)+a_{n}(x)
is odd).
Next, suppose
J_{x}=\left\{j_{1},j_{2}\right\}
and
j_{2}-j_{1}=1
.
If
j_{2}<n-1
, then
x
is not a vertex since either all
\chi_{j}(x)
are integers except
j=j_{2}
(when
a_{n-1}(x)+a_{n}(x)
is even), or all
\chi_{j}(x)
are non-integers except
j=j_{2}
(when
a_{n-1}(x)+a_{n}(x)
is odd).
If
j_{2}=n-1
, then
x
is not a vertex since
\chi_{n}(x)\notin\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
.
If
j_{2}=n
, then
J_{x}=\left\{n-1,n\right\}
and we leave this situation in
Lemma

4.6.3
.

Conversely, suppose
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
and
x\notin\Xi^{(0)}
.
Then there are four cases:

1.

\left\lvert J_{x}\right\rvert\geqslant 3
. Then among the coordinates of
x
, at least two of them are integers and two of them are non-integers.

2.

J_{x}=\left\{j_{1},j_{2}\right\}
and
1<j_{2}-j_{1}<n-2
.
Then
\chi_{j_{1}+1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{j_{2}}(x)
are either all the integer coordinates of
x
, or all the non-integer coordinates of
x
.

3.

J_{x}=\left\{j_{1}\right\}
and
1<j_{1}<n-1
.
Then
\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{j_{1}}(x)
are either all the integer coordinates of
x
, or all the non-integer coordinates of
x
.

4.

x
has no jumps. Then it is a special vertex.

In any of above cases,
x
is a vertex by our characterization.
∎

4.6.3
.

A point
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
is not a vertex if and only if
J_{x}\subseteq\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}
.

Note that
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
implies that
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq J_{x}
.
We will use
\Xi^{(1)}
to denote the set of points
x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}
having the property that
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq J_{x}\subseteq\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}
.

Proof.

First, points in
\Xi^{(1)}
cannot be vertices.
Indeed, if
x\in\Xi^{(1)}
, then
a_{j}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
j<n-2
.
Hence,
\chi_{1}(x),\cdots,\chi_{n-2}(x)
are either all integers or all non-integers.
Hence, by
Eq.

4.6.4
, for
x
to be a vertex, we must have that
2\chi_{n-1}(x)
and
2\chi_{n}(x)
are integers in the same parity.
But
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq J_{x}
implies that they are not.

Conversely, suppose
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
and
x\notin\Xi^{(1)}
.
Then
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq J_{x}
implies that exactly one of
\chi_{n-1}(x)
and
\chi_{n}(x)
is an integer.
If
j<n-2
is an index in
J_{x}
, then exactly one of
\chi_{j}(x)
and
\chi_{j+1}(x)
is an integer.
Hence, among the coordinates of
x
, at least two of them are integers and two of them are non-integers.
∎

We illustrate the structure of
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
by the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Xi^{(0)}\cup\Xi^{(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}\cap\Xi^{(0)})\cup(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}\cap\Xi^{(1)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigcup\limits_{i=2,\cdots,n-2}\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{i}}}

where
\dagger
denotes “being special”.

(vi)
.

Let
x\in\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
be a vertex.
We divide into two cases:
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
or
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
.

First, let us assume
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
and suppose
x
has jumps
j_{1},\cdots,j_{s}
, ordered from smallest to largest.
To construct a path between
x
and a special vertex
x_{0}
in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
, we need the following lemmas.

4.6.4
.

Suppose either
j_{s}-j_{s-1}>1
or
s>3
. Then
y=x-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j_{1}}
is a vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
adjacent to
x
.

Proof.

It is clear that
y\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
.
Then the proof is similar to
Lemma

4.5.2
except that the root
a_{[j_{s}]}
is defined as follows:

a_{[j_{s}]}=\begin{cases}a_{j_{s}-1}+2a_{j_{s}}+\cdots+2a_{n-2}+a_{n-1}+a_{n}&if $j_{s}-j_{s-1}>1$,\\
a_{j_{s-2}}+\cdots+a_{j_{s}-1}+2a_{j_{s}}+\cdots+2a_{n-2}+a_{n-1}+a_{n}&otherwise,\\
\end{cases}

Hence, we omit the proof here.
∎

Note that
a_{0}(y)\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil-a_{0}(x)=1
.
Hence, by repeating using
Lemma

4.6.4
, we can reduce our problem to the case where
s=3
, or further
s=1
if we start with
j_{s}-j_{s-1}>1
.

Now, we may assume either
s\leqslant 3
with
j_{s}-j_{s-1}=1
or
s=1
.

4.6.5
.

Suppose
s=3
and
j_{1}>1
.
Then
x_{0}=x-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{j_{1}}-\omega_{j_{2}}+\omega_{j_{3}})
is a special vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
adjacent to
x
and verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as
Lemma

4.5.3
.
∎

4.6.6
.

Suppose
s=3
,
j_{3}-j_{2}=1
and
j_{1}=1
, then
y=x-\tfrac{1}{2}(-\omega_{j_{1}}+\omega_{j_{2}})
is a vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
adjacent to
x
.

Proof.

It is clear that
y\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
.
Then the proof is similar to
Lemma

4.5.4
except that the root
a_{[j_{s}]}
is defined as follows:

a_{[j_{s}]}=a_{j_{1}}+\cdots+a_{j_{2}}+2a_{j_{3}}+\cdots+2a_{n-2}+a_{n-1}+a_{n}.

Hence, we omit the proof here.
∎

Note that the only jump of
y
is
j_{3}>1
.
Hence,
a_{0}(y)\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil-a_{0}(y)=1
.
Therefore,
Lemma

4.6.6
reduces our problem to the case where
s=1
.

Note that, by
Lemma

4.6.2
,
s=2
and
j_{s}-j_{s-1}=1
contradict to each other.
Therefore, we may assume
s=1
now.
By
Lemma

4.6.2
again, we must have
j_{1}>1
.
Let
x_{0}=x-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j_{1}}
.
Then it is in a special vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
since
a_{1}(x_{0}),\cdots,a_{n}(x_{0})
are non-negative integers.
Applying
Lemma

4.2.1
to the simple roots
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}
and the vertices
x
and
x_{0}
, we see that they are adjacent. Moreover, we have
a_{0}(x)-a_{0}(x_{0})=1
verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
.
This finishes the proof of
Theorem

1.3
when
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
.

Next, let us assume
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
. Then we must have
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq J_{x}
.
Suppose
x
has jumps
j_{1},\cdots,j_{s},n-1,n
, ordered from smallest to largest.
To construct a path between
x
and a special vertex
x_{0}
in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
, we need the following lemmas.

4.6.7
.

Suppose either
j_{s}-j_{s-1}>1
or
s>2
. Then
y=x-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j_{1}}
is a vertex in
