\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
adjacent to
x
.

Proof.

It is clear that
y\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
.
When
j_{s}-j_{s-1}>1
or
s>3
, the proof is similar to
Lemma

4.6.4
except that we have to define
a_{[n-1]}
and
a_{[n]}
as follows:

\displaystyle a_{[n-1]}

\displaystyle=a_{j_{s}}+\cdots+a_{n-2}+a_{n-1},

\displaystyle a_{[n]}

\displaystyle=a_{j_{s}}+\cdots+a_{n-2}+a_{n}.

When
s=3
and
j_{3}-j_{2}=1
, the proof still works if we define
a_{[j_{s}]}
as follows:

a_{[j_{3}]}=a_{j_{2}}+\cdots+a_{n}.\qed

Note that
a_{0}(y)\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil-a_{0}(x)=1
.
Hence, by repeating using
Lemma

4.6.7
, we can reduce our problem to the case where
s=2
, or further
s=1
if we start with
j_{s}-j_{s-1}>1
.

Now, we may assume either
s\leqslant 2
with
j_{s}-j_{s-1}=1
or
s=1
.

4.6.8
.

Suppose
s=2
and
j_{1}>1
.
Then
x_{0}=x-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{j_{1}}-\omega_{j_{2}}+\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n})
is a special vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
adjacent to
x
and verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
.

Proof.

First note that
a_{0}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
and

a_{0}(x)-a_{0}(x_{0})=\tfrac{1}{2}a_{0}(\omega_{j_{1}}-\omega_{j_{2}}+\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n})=1.

Then the proof is similar to
Lemma

4.5.3
except that there is no
j_{3}
and that we need to define
a_{[n-1]}
and
a_{[n]}
as follows:

\displaystyle a_{[n-1]}

\displaystyle=a_{j_{2}}+\cdots+a_{n-2}+a_{n-1},

\displaystyle a_{[n]}

\displaystyle=a_{j_{2}}+\cdots+a_{n-2}+a_{n}.

Then the statement follows.
∎

4.6.9
.

Suppose
s=2
and
j_{1}=1
, then
x_{0}=x-\tfrac{1}{2}(-\omega_{j_{1}}+\omega_{j_{2}}-\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n})
is a special vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
adjacent to
x
and verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
.

Proof.

First note that
a_{0}(x)\in\tfrac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
and

a_{0}(x)-a_{0}(x_{0})=\tfrac{1}{2}a_{0}(-\omega_{j_{1}}+\omega_{j_{2}}-\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n})=\tfrac{1}{2}.

Then the proof is similar to
Lemma

4.6.8
except that
a_{[n]}
is defined as follows:

a_{[n]}=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}.

Then the statement follows.
∎

4.6.10
.

Suppose
s=1
.
Then
x_{0}=x-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{j_{1}}-\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n})
is a special vertex in
\operatorname{{D}}\left(\prescript{v\!}{}{C}\right)
adjacent to
x
and verifying
Eq.

4.2.3
.

Proof.

First note that

a_{0}(x)-a_{0}(x_{0})=\tfrac{1}{2}a_{0}(\omega_{j_{1}}-\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n})=\tfrac{1}{2}a_{0}(\omega_{j_{1}}).

Hence,
\lceil a_{0}(x)\rceil-a_{0}(x_{0})=1
.
Then the proof is similar to
Lemma

4.6.8
or
Lemma

4.6.9
except that there is no
j_{2}
and that the root
a_{[n]}
is defined as follows:

a_{[n]}=a_{n-2}+a_{n-1}+a_{n}.

Here we need
j_{1}<n-2
, which is guaranteed by
Lemma

4.6.3
.
∎

This finishes the proof of
Theorem

1.3
.

(vii)
.

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
Using the notations introduced in
Eq.

4.5.4
, we have
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I,r\right)=\partial(r,\prescript{v\!}{}{C},I)
.
We also have introduced the sets
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}
, and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
in
Eq.

4.6.5
.
Inspired
Lemmas

4.6.1
,
4.6.2
, and
4.6.3
and
Definition

4.4.1
, we consider the following sets for each
J\subseteq\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\}
:

\displaystyle\Xi_{J}

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in\operatorname{\mathbb{A}}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}J_{x}=J\right\}

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}

\displaystyle:=\Xi_{J}\cap o+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Note that
o+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle o+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\setminus\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1,2\right\}}\cup\cdots\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-3,n-2\right\}}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}}.

Note that, for any
J
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:XJfromX0Dn}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}-\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}.

(4.6.6)

Moreover, it is clear that
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}
is precisely
o+\operatorname{\mathcal{P}}^{\vee}
, the set of special vertices.

Next, we consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)
for above sets.
First, if
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I\neq\emptyset
then
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(0)}\left(I\right)
.
Otherwise,
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)\cap\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{(1)}\left(I\right)\neq\emptyset
.
In each case, we have
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)\cap(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(I\right))=\emptyset
if and only if
\ell_{1}>1
.
For any
J
, it is clear that
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)\neq\emptyset
if and only if
I\cap J=\emptyset
. If this is the case, we have the following refinement of
Eq.

4.6.6
:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:XIJfromXI0Dn}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I\right)-\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}.

(4.6.7)

Finally, we consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I,r\right)
. First, it is clear that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:IndexBsrciDn:pu00}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I,r\right)=\left\{x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}^{\prime}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}^{\prime}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}>0,\\
c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r\end{array}\right\}.

(4.6.8)

Then the followings follow from
Eq.

4.6.5
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I,r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I,r+1\right)-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n}),

(4.6.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I,r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I,r\right)-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1},

(4.6.10)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(I,r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I,r+1\right)-\tfrac{1}{2}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{n-1}+\omega_{n}).

(4.6.11)

Then we need to work out
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right)
. For
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I,r\right)
, an explicit description is given in
(vii)
and
(vii)
.
For general
J
, we have the following refinement of
Eq.

4.6.7
:

4.6.11
.

Suppose
I\cap J=\emptyset
.
Then we have

\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right)=\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I,r+\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)\right)-\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j},

where
\delta(J)
is defined as follows:

\delta(J)=\begin{cases}2&if $\left\{1,n-1,n\right\}\subseteq J$,\\
0&if $\left\{1,n-1,n\right\}\cap J=\emptyset$,\\
1&otherwise.\end{cases}

Proof.

By
Eq.

4.5.6
, it suffices to show that for any
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}\left(I,r\right)
,

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:tag:lem:XIJfromXI0Dn}\left\lceil a_{0}\left(x-\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}\right)\right\rceil=a_{0}(x)-\left\lvert J\right\rvert+\delta(J).

(
\ast
)

Note that
a_{0}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
and that

a_{0}\left(\sum_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}\right)=\begin{cases}\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\tfrac{3}{2}&if $\left\{1,n-1,n\right\}\subseteq J$,\\
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-1&if $1\notin J$ but $\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq J$,\\
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\tfrac{1}{2}&if $1\in J$ but $\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap J=\emptyset$,\\
\left\lvert J\right\rvert&if $\left\{1,n-1,n\right\}\cap J=\emptyset$.\end{cases}

Then
Eq.

∗

follows.
∎

§ 5  Asymptotic analysis

This section aims to provide tools to analyze the asymptotic behavior of the simplicial volume and the simplicial surface area.

We have already seen that
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
in
Section

1
, the introduction.
Hence, in order to prove
Theorem

1.1
, it suffices to prove the simplicial surface area part.
Likewise, the simplicial volume part of
Theorem

1.4
can be deduced from the simplicial surface area part, either by
Lemma

5.1.9
or direct computation.
Therefore, it suffices to consider the asymptotic analysis of the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
only.

In the formulas
Eqs.

3.4.5
and
3.4.6
, there are only finitely many
I\subseteq\Delta
and each
\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\left(q\right)
is an integral polynomial.
Hence, the asymptotic study of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be reduced to summations of the following form:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SX}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right):=\sum_{x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I,r\right)}\prod_{a\in\Phi^{+}}q^{\lceil a(x)\rceil},

(5.0.1)

where
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}
is a set of points, and the notations
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I,r\right)
follow
Eq.

4.5.4
.

The growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
varies for different types
I
.
For the purpose of asymptotic analysis, only the dominant ones are relevant.
To better analyze their growth, we introduce the following auxiliary functions:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SXasymp}\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right):=\sum_{x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I,r\right)}q^{2\rho(x)},

(5.0.2)

where
2\rho
is the sum of positive roots.
Note that

2\rho(x)=\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}a(x)\leqslant\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil\leqslant\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}(a(x)+1)=2\rho(x)+\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{\Phi;I}\right).

Hence, we have
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.
But the later one is easier to study.

This section is structured as follows.
In
Section

5.1
, we will introduce
q
-numbers
and
q
-functions
and discuss the
discrete calculus
on them.
We will then only focus on the
q
-functions defined by
(super)
q
-exponential polynomials
.
To study them algebraically, we will review gradings and filtrations in
Section

5.2
.
Then in
Sections

5.3
and
5.4
, we will introduce
(super)
q
-exponential polynomials
and study the asymptotic properties of the
q
-functions defined by them.
Finally, with those notions in hand, we will study the asymptotic growth of multi-summations in
Sections

5.5
and
5.6
.

5.1
Discrete calculus of
q
-functions

It is often more convenient to treat
q
as a formal variable when we apply algebraic operations to
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.
But to carry out the asymptotic analysis, we need to view
q
as a real number.
Inspired by this, we have the following definition.

5.1.1
.

Let
q
be a formal variable and
h
a positive integer.
Then a
q
-number (of level
h
)
is a rational function of
q^{1/h}
over
\mathbb{Q}
having no poles on the half real line
\mathbb{R}_{>1}:=\left\{r\in\mathbb{R}\nonscript\>\middle|\allowbreak\nonscript\>\mathopen{}r>1\right\}
.
Let
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;h\right)
denote the ring of
q
-numbers of level
h
.
Then a
q
-function (of level
h
)
is a function defined for sufficiently large integers and valued in
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;h\right)
.

5.1.2
.

Let
h
be a positive integer larger than
1
.
Then
(q^{1/h}-1)^{-1}
is a
q
-number of level
h
, while
(q-h)^{-1}
is not a
q
-number.

.

A rational function of
q^{1/h}
is in particular an algebraic function of
q
and hence we can talk about its poles.
On the half real line
\mathbb{R}_{>1}
, the function
q^{1/h}
has a unique real-valued branch.
This allows us to treat
q
-numbers as real-valued continuous functions on
\mathbb{R}_{>1}
.

Each
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;h\right)
is a principal ideal domain.
When the level
h
varies, they form an inductive system.
Let
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
denote the inductive limit.
We will view it as the ring of all
q
-numbers.
On this principal ideal domain, we will consider the
pointwise topology
inheriting from the algebra
\operatorname{{C}}\left(\mathbb{R}_{>1}\right)
of real-valued continuous functions on
\mathbb{R}_{>1}
.
In particular, if
f
is a
q
-function, then the limit of
f(z)
as
z\to\infty
is defined pointwise:

\lim_{z\to\infty}f(z)=\left(\lim_{z\to\infty}f(z)(q)\right)_{q>1}.

Then we can view each
q
-function
f
as a family of discrete functions
(f_{q})_{q>1}
indexed by the half real line
\mathbb{R}_{>1}
, where
f_{q}(z):=f(z)(q)
.

5.1.3
.

Let
f
and
g
be two
q
-functions.
We say that they are
asymptotically equal
and that
f

has asymptotic growth

g
, denoted by
f(z)\sim g(z)
, if

\lim_{z\to\infty}\frac{f(z)}{g(z)}=1.

We also need asymptotic dominant relations of
q
-functions.
Like the topology, these notions are defined
pointwise
.

5.1.4
.

A
q
-number
C
is said to be
positive
(resp.
non-negative
) if for all
q>1
,
C(q)
is a positive (resp. non-negative) real number.
A
q
-function
f
is said to be
eventually positive
(resp.
eventually non-negative
) if for all
q>1
,
f_{q}
is an eventually positive (resp. eventually non-negative) function, namely:
f_{q}(z)>0
(resp.
f_{q}(z)\geqslant 0
) for sufficiently large
z
.

5.1.5
.

Let
f
and
g
be two
q
-functions.
We say that
f

dominates

g
, denoted by
f(z)\gg g(z)
, if there exists a positive
q
-number
C
such that
\left\lvert f\right\rvert-C\cdot\left\lvert g\right\rvert
is an eventually non-negative
q
-function.
We will denote
f(z)\asymp g(z)
if both
f(z)\gg g(z)
and
g(z)\gg f(z)
.

Then we can consider the
discrete calculus
on
q
-numbers.

5.1.6
.

Let
f
be a
q
-function.
Its
difference

\operatorname{\Delta}{f}
is the following
q
-function:

\operatorname{\Delta}{f}(z):=f(z+1)-f(z).

The
difference operator

\operatorname{\Delta}
is
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
-linear and satisfies the
Leibniz rule
:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:LeibnizRule}\operatorname{\Delta}\left(fg\right)=f\cdot\operatorname{\Delta}{g}+g\cdot\operatorname{\Delta}{f}+\operatorname{\Delta}{f}\cdot\operatorname{\Delta}{g}.

(5.1.1)

5.1.7
.

A
q
-function
f
is said to be
eventually strictly increasing
if for all
q>1
,
f_{q}
is an eventually strictly increasing function.

Clearly,
f
is eventually strictly increasing if and only if
\operatorname{\Delta}{f}
is eventually positive.

5.1.8
.

A
q
-function
f
is said to be
unbounded
if for all
q
-number
C
, the
q
-function
\left\lvert f\right\rvert-C
is eventually positive.

5.1.9
.

Let
f
and
g
be two eventually strictly increasing unbounded
q
-functions.
Then we have
f(z)\sim g(z)
if and only if
\operatorname{\Delta}{f}(z)\sim\operatorname{\Delta}{g}(z)
.

Proof.

Apply
Stolz-Cesàro theorem
(see e.g.
[
8
]
*theorem 2.7.2) to
f_{q}
and
g_{q}
for all
q>1
.
Then the statement follows.
∎

We also need the discrete version of integrals.

5.1.10
.

Let
f
be a
q
-function.
Then an
anti-difference
of
f
is a
q
-function
g
such that
\operatorname{\Delta}{g}=f
.
Since
\operatorname{Ker}\left(\operatorname{\Delta}\right)
consists of constant
q
-functions, we see that the anti-difference is not unique but unique up to a constant
q
-function.
By an abuse of notation, we will use
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
to denote an anti-difference of
f
.
Let
a
be an integer in the domain of
f
.
Then the
anti-difference of
f
with anchor
a
, denoted by
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}
, is defined as follows:

\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}(z):=(\operatorname{\Sigma}{f})(z)-(\operatorname{\Sigma}{f})(a).

Note that
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}
is well-defined although
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
is not.

Note that, if
a,b
are two integers, then we have the following summation formula:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:FTC}\sum_{z=a}^{b-1}f(z)=(\operatorname{\Sigma}{f})(b)-(\operatorname{\Sigma}{f})(a)=(\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f})(b).

(5.1.2)

We will consider the following notions of
q
-numbers and
q
-functions.

5.1.11
.

A
q
-number is said to be
primary
if it is of level one.
Then a
q
-function is said to be
primary
if its values are primary
q
-numbers.

5.2
Weakly graded algebras

Before moving on, let’s review gradings and then the filtrations induced by them.
In the study of (super)
q
-exponential polynomials, it is the filtration induced by a grading, rather than the grading itself, will play an essential role.

Throughout this subsection,
R
is a commutative ring and
\Gamma
is an additive monoid.
In the applications later,
\Gamma
will be
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, or products of them.

5.2.1
.

A
\Gamma
-grading
on an
R
-module
M
is a decomposition into a direct sum

M=\bigoplus_{g\in\Gamma}M_{g},

where each
M_{g}
is an
R
-submodule, called the
homogeneous component of grade
g
.
Elements of
M_{g}
are said to be
homogeneous of grade
g
.
A general element
m
of
M
is decomposed into homogeneous elements
m_{g}
(
g\in\Gamma
), each
m_{g}
is called its
homogeneous component of grade
g
.
An
R
-module equipped with a
\Gamma
-grading
is called a
\Gamma
-graded module over
R
.

5.2.2
.

Let
M
be a
\Gamma
-graded module over
R
and
h\in\Gamma
.
Then an operator
T
on
M
is said to
respect the grading
if
T(M_{g})\subseteq M_{g}
for all
g\in\Gamma
and
shift the grading homogeneously by
h
if
T(M_{g})\subseteq M_{g+h}
for all
g\in\Gamma
.

5.2.3
.

Suppose
\Gamma
is a group and
h\in\Gamma
.
Let
M
be a
\Gamma
-graded projective module over
R
and
T
a surjective
R
-linear operator
T
on
M
shifting the grading homogeneously by
h
.
Then there is a section of
T
shifts the grading homogeneously by
-h
.

Proof.

The conditions on
T
imply that its restriction to each
M_{g}
is surjective onto
M_{g+h}
.
Since
M_{g+h}
is projective,
T|_{M_{g}}
admits a section
S_{g}\colon M_{g+h}\to M_{g}
.
Then the desired section of
T
is the direct sum of the sections
S_{g}
.
∎

.

The
Grothendieck group

\operatorname{\mathcal{G}}\left(\Gamma\right)
of
\Gamma
is the universal Abelian group under
\Gamma
.
If
M
is a
\Gamma
-graded module, then we will treat it as a
\operatorname{\mathcal{G}}\left(\Gamma\right)
-graded module by defining
M_{g}=\left\{0\right\}
if
g\in\operatorname{\mathcal{G}}\left(\Gamma\right)\setminus\Gamma
.
With this convention,
Lemma

5.2.3
holds even without assuming that
\Gamma
is a group.

5.2.4
.

Let
M
be a
\Gamma
-graded projective module over
R
and
T
a surjective
R
-linear operator
T
on
M
respecting the grading.
Then there is a section of
T
respects the grading.

5.2.5
.

A
\Gamma
-graded algebra over
R
is an
R
-algebra
A
equipped with a
\Gamma
-grading such that

A_{g}A_{h}\subseteq A_{g+h},\qquad\text{for all}\qquad g,h\in\Gamma.

Note that
A_{0}
is a subalgebra and each
A_{g}
(
g\in\Gamma
) is an
A_{0}
-bimodule.
The subalgebra
A_{0}
is called the
subalgebra of grade
0
.

If
A
is a
\Gamma
-graded algebra over
R
, then a
\Gamma
-graded (left)
A
-module
is a
\Gamma
-graded module
M
over
R
equipped with a (left)
A
-action such that

A_{g}M_{h}\subseteq M_{g+h},\qquad\text{for all}\qquad g,h\in\Gamma.

5.2.6
.

Let
A
be an
R
-algebra
A
equipped with a
\Gamma
-grading.
Suppose that
A_{0}
is a subalgebra of
A
and that each homogeneous component
A_{g}
(
g\in\Gamma
) is a free
A_{0}
-module of rank one.
Then the grading can be written as follows:

A=\bigoplus_{g\in\Gamma}A_{0}e_{g},

where each
e_{g}\in A_{g}
is a generator of the
A_{0}
-module
A_{g}
and
e_{0}=1\in A_{0}
.
The family
(e_{g})_{g\in\Gamma}
is called a
homogeneous basis of
A
over
A_{0}
. Note that a homogeneous basis of
A
determines the
\Gamma
-grading on it.
Let
a\in A
.
Then its homogeneous component of grade
g
is of the form
a_{g}=c_{g}e_{g}
with
c_{g}\in A_{0}
.
The element
c_{g}
is called the
coefficient
of
a_{g}
and is said to be a
coefficient of
a
and
attached to
e_{g}
in
a
.

.

The assumption in
Definition

5.2.6
does not require
A
to be a
\Gamma
-graded algebra.

In the rest of this subsection,
\Gamma
is a totally ordered additive monoid (for instance,
\Gamma
is a submonoid of
(\mathbb{R},+)
).

5.2.7
.

A
\Gamma
-filtration
on an
R
-module
M
is a family of
R
-submodules
(M_{\leqslant g})_{g\in\Gamma}
of
M
such that
M_{\leqslant g}\subseteq M_{\leqslant h}
whenever
g\leqslant h
and that
\bigcup_{g\in\Gamma}M_{\leqslant g}=M
.
We will use
M_{\approx g}
to denote the set of elements in
M_{\leqslant g}
but not in any
M_{\leqslant h}
with
h<g
.

A
\Gamma
-grading on an
R
-module
M
induces a
\Gamma
-filtration as follows:

\displaystyle M_{\leqslant g}

\displaystyle:=\bigoplus_{h\leqslant g}M_{h}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(g\in\Gamma)}

Then we say an element
m\in M
is
of grade
g
if
m\in M_{\approx g}
.
If an element is of grade
g
, then its homogeneous component of grade
g
will be called its
leading term
.

Note that a nonzero homogeneous element of grade
g
is of grade
g
.
For a general nonzero element
m\in M
, its grade is the largest
g
such that the homogeneous component of grade
g
of
m
is nonzero.

5.2.8
.

If
M
is
\mathbb{N}
-graded, we will say that
0\in M
has grade
-1
.
If
M
is
\mathbb{Q}
-graded, we will say that
0\in M
has grade
-\infty
.

5.2.9
.

Let
M
be a
\Gamma
-graded module over
R
and
h\in\Gamma
.
Then an operator
T
on
M
is said to
respect the filtration strictly
if
T(M_{\approx g})\subseteq M_{\approx g}
for all
g\in\Gamma
and
shift the grading by
h
if
T(M_{\approx g})\subseteq M_{\approx g+h}
for all
g\in\Gamma
.

5.2.10
.

Suppose
\Gamma
is a totally ordered group and
h\in\Gamma
.
Let
M
be a
\Gamma
-graded module over
R
and
T
a surjective
R
-linear operator
T
on
M
shifting the grading by
h
.
Then any section of
T
shifts the grading by
-h
.

Proof.

Since
M_{\approx g}\cap M_{\approx g^{\prime}}=\emptyset
whenever
g\neq g^{\prime}
, the conditions on
T
imply that the preimage of
M_{\approx g+h}
under
T
is precisely
M_{\approx g}
.
Hence, if
S
is a section of
T
, we have
S(M_{\approx g+h})\subseteq M_{\approx g}
as expected.
∎

.

The total order on
\Gamma
can be canonically extended to its Grothendieck group
\operatorname{\mathcal{G}}\left(\Gamma\right)
.
Then
Lemma

5.2.10
holds without assuming that
\Gamma
is a group.

5.2.11
.

Let
M
be a
\Gamma
-graded module over
R
and
T
a surjective
R
-linear operator
T
on
M
respecting the filtration strictly.
Then any section of
T
respects the filtration strictly.

5.2.12
.

A
weakly
\Gamma
-graded algebra over
R
is an
R
-algebra
A
equipped with a
\Gamma
-grading such that

A_{\approx g}A_{\approx h}\subseteq A_{\approx g+h}\qquad\text{for all}\qquad g,h\in\Gamma.

If
A
is a weakly
\Gamma
-graded algebra over
R
, then
a
weakly
\Gamma
-graded (left)
A
-module
is a
\Gamma
-graded module
M
over
R
equipped with a (left)
A
-action such that

A_{\approx g}M_{\approx h}\subseteq M_{\approx g+h}\qquad\text{for all}\qquad g,h\in\Gamma.

.

If
A
is a weakly
\Gamma
-graded algebra over
R
.
Then a free (left) module over
A
is naturally a weakly
\Gamma
-graded (left)
A
-module.

5.2.13
.

The ring of polynomials
R[z]
over
R
is naturally a
\mathbb{N}
-graded algebra, where the monomials
(z^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
forms a homogeneous basis of it.
However, if the characteristic of
R
is
0
, then the induced
\mathbb{N}
-filtration on
R[z]
, namely the
degree
filtration, can also be induced from the following alternative grading:

R[z]=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R\binom{z}{n},

where

\binom{z}{n}:=\frac{1}{n!}z(z-1)\cdots(z-n+1).

In this paper, this grading will be called the
degree
.
Note that this convention is different from the usual one.
In particular, this grading does not make
R[z]
into a
\mathbb{N}
-graded algebra over
R
, only a weakly
\mathbb{N}
-graded algebra.

5.3
q
-exponential polynomials

In this and the next section, we will introduce
(super)
q
-exponential polynomials
and study their interaction with anti-difference operators.

5.3.1
.

A
q
-polynomial
is a polynomial with
q
-number coefficients.
Following the usual notation, we will denote the ring of
q
-polynomials by
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
.

5.3.2
.

A
q
-exponential polynomial
is a finite formal sum

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:ExponentialPolynomial}f(z)=\sum_{\nu}f_{\nu}(z)q^{\nu z},

(5.3.1)

where
\nu\in\mathbb{Q}
and each
f_{\nu}(z)
is a
q
-polynomial.
The ring of
q
-exponential polynomials will be denoted by
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
.

5.3.3
.

Following
Example

5.2.13
, we will consider the following grading on
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
and call it the
degree
:

\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)\binom{z}{n}.

That is to say, an element of grade
n
in the sense of
Definition

5.2.7
will be said to be of
degree

n
.
However, note that this grading only makes
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
a weakly
\mathbb{N}
-graded algebra over
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
.
Let
f
be a
q
-polynomial.
We will use
\operatorname{deg}\left(f\right)
to denote its
degree
.
The
leading coefficient

\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right)
of
f
is defined to be the coefficient of its leading term, namely the
q
-number attached to
\binom{z}{\operatorname{deg}\left(f\right)}
in
f
.

5.3.4
.

The following grading on
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
will be called the
order
:

\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}=\bigoplus_{\nu\in\mathbb{Q}}\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\nu z}.

That is to say, an element of grade
\nu
in the sense of
Definition

5.2.7
will be said to be of
order

\nu
.
Note that this grading makes
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
a
\mathbb{Q}
-graded algebra over
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
.
We will use
\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)
to denote the
order
of a
q
-exponential polynomial
f
.

Each homogeneous component
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\nu z}
is
a free module of rank one over the weakly
\mathbb{N}
-graded algebra
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
and thus naturally a weakly
\mathbb{N}
-graded module.
The
degree

\operatorname{deg}\left(f\right)
and the
leading coefficient

\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right)
of a
q
-exponential polynomial
f
are defined to be the degree and the leading coefficient of its leading term in the order grading.

5.3.5
.

Let
f
be a
q
-exponential polynomial as in
Eq.

5.3.1
.
Then its order is the largest
\nu\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
f_{\nu}\neq 0
, its degree and leading coefficient is the degree and the leading coefficient of the
q
-polynomial
f_{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)}
.

.

Every
q
-polynomial will be viewed as a
q
-exponential polynomial which is homogeneous of order
0
.

It is clear that a
q
-exponential polynomial
f
defines a
q
-function.
We will use the same notion to denote this
q
-function.
Then it has the following asymptotic growth:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:AsymptoticOfEP}f(z)\sim\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right)\binom{z}{\operatorname{deg}\left(f\right)}q^{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)z}.

(5.3.2)

We thus introduce the following convention.

5.3.6
.

Let
\operatorname{S}
be a
q
-function and
f
a
q
-exponential polynomial.
If
\operatorname{S}
can be defined by a
q
-exponential polynomial whose leading term is the same as
f(z)
, then we will say that
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)

has asymptotic growth

f(z)
and write

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim f(z)

by an abuse of language. Note that this implies that
\operatorname{S}
is asymptotically equal to the
q
-function defined by
f
.

Now, we turn to the discrete calculus.

5.3.7
.

The
difference operator

\operatorname{\Delta}
on
q
-polynomials is the
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
-linear operator vanishing on constant
q
-polynomials and satisfying the following:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\Delta}{\binom{z}{n}}

\displaystyle=\binom{z}{n-1}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(n\geqslant 1)}

(5.3.3)

This operator extends to
q
-exponential polynomials as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\Delta}\left(\binom{z}{n}q^{\nu z}\right)

\displaystyle:=\left((q^{\nu}-1)\binom{z}{n}+q^{\nu}\binom{z}{n-1}\right)q^{\nu z}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(n\geqslant 1,\nu\neq 0)}

(5.3.4)

It is straightforward to verify that the difference operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
satisfies the
Leibniz rule

Eq.

5.1.1
.
For a
q
-exponential polynomial
f
, the
q
-function defined by
\operatorname{\Delta}{f}
is precisely the
difference
of the
q
-function defined by
f
.

The following lemma follows from the definition.

5.3.8
.

The linear operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
respects the order grading on
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
.
On each homogeneous component
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\nu z}
(
\nu\neq 0
), the operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
respects the degree filtration strictly.
On the subalgebra
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
, the operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
shifts the degree homogeneously by
-1
.

Next, we will introduce the anti-difference operators.

5.3.9
.

The linear operators
\operatorname{\Delta}
admits a section
\operatorname{\Sigma}
such that

(iv)

it respects the order;

(iv)

on each homogeneous component of order
\nu\neq 0
, it respects the degree filtration strictly;

(iv)

on the subalgebra of order
0
, it shifts the degree homogeneously by
1
.

Moreover, if
f
is a
q
-exponential polynomial, then we have the following formula:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:asumEP}\operatorname{lead}\left(\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}\right)=\begin{cases}\left(q^{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)}-1\right)^{-1}\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right)&if $\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)\neq 0$,\\
\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right)&if $\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)=0$.\end{cases}

(5.3.5)

Proof.

First note that the restriction of the linear operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
to each homogeneous component
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\nu z}
(
\nu\neq 0
) is bijective, while its restriction to the subalgebra
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
is surjective.
In particular, the linear operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
itself is surjective.
Since
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
is a free module over
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
,
the statements on orders and degrees follows from
Lemma

5.3.8
by applying
Corollaries

5.2.4
,
5.2.11
, and
5.2.3
to the operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
.
As for the leading coefficients,
\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)=0
case follows from
Eq.

5.3.3
.
If
\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)>0
, by
Eq.

5.3.4
, we have

\operatorname{lead}\left(\operatorname{\Delta}{f}\right)=\left(q^{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)}-1\right)\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right).

Replacing
f
by
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
,
Eq.

5.3.5
follows.
∎

5.3.10
.

The linear operator
\operatorname{\Sigma}
in
Lemma

5.3.9
is called the
free anti-difference operator
.
Let
a
be an integer.
The
anti-difference operator with anchor
a
, denoted by
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}
, is the linear operator
\operatorname{\Sigma}-\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ\operatorname{\Sigma}
, where
\operatorname{ev}_{a}
evaluates a
q
-exponential polynomial
f(z)
at
z=a
.

For a
q
-exponential polynomial
f
, the
q
-function defined by
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}
is the
anti-difference with anchor
a
of the
q
-function defined by
f
.

We end this subsection with discussions of primarity.

5.3.11
.

Let
f
be a
q
-exponential polynomial as in
Eq.

5.3.1
.
Then
f
is said to be
primary
if its coefficients are primary
q
-numbers and
f_{\nu}=0
for all
\nu\notin\mathbb{Z}
.

Clearly, primary
q
-exponential polynomials define primary
q
-functions.

5.3.12
.

Let
f
be a
q
-exponential polynomial.
If
\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)\geqslant 0
, then the
q
-functions defined by
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
are asymptotically equal to the
q
-function defined by
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
.
If
f
is a primary, then so are
\operatorname{\Delta}{f}
,
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
, and
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}
(
a\in\mathbb{Z}
).

Proof.

We have
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}-\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
.
Note that elements of
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
have order
0
and degree
0
, while
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
has nonzero order or degree by
Lemma

5.3.9
.
Hence, we have
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}\sim\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
.
The last statement is evident.
∎

5.4
Super
q
-exponential polynomials

5.4.1
.

A
parity function
is a function which is defined on integers and factors through the projection
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
A parity function valued in
q
-numbers is called a
parity
q
-function
.

5.4.2
.

A
super
q
-polynomial
is a polynomial with coefficients in parity
q
-functions.
A
super
q
-exponential polynomial
is a finite formal sum

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:superExponentialPolynomial}f(z)=\sum_{\nu}f_{\nu}(z)q^{\nu z},

(5.4.1)

where
\nu\in\mathbb{Q}
and each
f_{\nu}(z)
is a super
q
-polynomial.

To better understand the structure of the ring of super
q
-exponential polynomials, we recall the following notions.

5.4.3
.

A
superalgebra
over a commutative ring
R
is a
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-graded algebra
A
over
R
.
The
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-grading
A=A_{0}\oplus A_{1}
is called the
parity
.
In particular, the subalgebra
A_{0}
is called the
even part
and the
A_{0}
-module
A_{1}
is called the
odd part
.
For any element
a\in A
, its homogeneous component of parity
0
(resp.
1
) is called its
even part
(resp.
odd part
).
If
A
is a superalgebra, then an
A
-supermodule
is a
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-graded
A
-module.

5.4.4
.

We will use
(-1)^{z}
to denote the parity function mapping even numbers to
1
and odd numbers to
-1
.

Then the following lemma is easy to verify.

5.4.5
.

Let
e(z)
be a parity function.
Then we have

e(z)=\frac{1}{2}(e(0)+e(1))+\frac{1}{2}(e(0)-e(1))(-1)^{z}.

By this lemma, we have the following.

5.4.6
.

The ring of parity
q
-functions with formal variable
z
is precisely the
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
-algebra generated by
(-1)^{z}
and is a superalgebra decomposed into even and odd parts as follows:

\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z}]=\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)\oplus\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)(-1)^{z}.

Then the ring of super
q
-polynomials can be denoted by
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]
.

5.4.7
.

The ring of super
q
-polynomials is a superalgebra decomposed into even and odd parts as follows:

\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]=\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]\oplus\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z](-1)^{z}.

5.4.8
.

The
degree
grading on
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]
is defined similarly to that on
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
in
Definition

5.3.3
and makes it a weakly
\mathbb{N}
-graded algebra over
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z}]
.
Since this weakly graded algebra is also a superalgebra, the degree grading induces a grading on its even part and one on its odd parts.
They are called the
even degree
and the
odd degree
.
Note that the even degree is precisely the degree grading on
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
defined in
Definition

5.3.3
, and the odd degree is precisely the natural grading on a free module of rank one over
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]
.

Let
f
be a super
q
-polynomial.
We will use
\operatorname{deg}\left(f\right)
to denote its
degree
.
Its
even degree

\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f\right)
(resp.
odd degree

\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f\right)
) is the even degree (resp. odd degree) of its even part (resp. odd part).
The
leading coefficient

\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right)
of
f
is defined to be the coefficient of its leading term, namely the parity
q
-function attached to
\binom{z}{\operatorname{deg}\left(f\right)}
in
f
.
Its
even leading coefficient

\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f\right)
(resp.
odd leading coefficient

\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f\right)
) is the leading coefficient of its even part (resp. odd part).

5.4.9
.

Let
f
be a super
q
-polynomial as follows:

f(z)=f_{0}(z)+f_{1}(z)(-1)^{z},

where
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are
q
-polynomials.
Then the even degree of
f
is
\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{0}\right)
, the odd degree of
f
is
f
is
\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{1}\right)
, and the degree of
f
is the larger one of them.
If
\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{0}\right)>\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{1}\right)
, then the leading coefficient of
f
is precisely its even leading coefficient, which is
\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{0}\right)
.
If
\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{0}\right)<\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{1}\right)
, then the leading coefficient of
f
is precisely its odd leading coefficient multiplied by
(-1)^{z}
, which is
\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{1}\right)(-1)^{z}
.
If
\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{0}\right)=\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{1}\right)
, then the leading coefficient of
f
is the parity
q
-function
\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{0}\right)+\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{1}\right)(-1)^{z}
.

Similarly to
Definition

5.3.2
, the ring of super
q
-exponential polynomials will be denoted by
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
.

5.4.10
.

The
order
grading on
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
is defined similarly to that on
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
in
Definition

5.3.4
and makes it a
\mathbb{Q}
-graded algebra over the superalgebra
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]
. We will use
\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)
to denote the
order
of a super
q
-exponential polynomial
f
.

Each homogeneous component
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]q^{\nu z}
is a free supermodule of rank one over the weakly
\mathbb{N}
-graded superalgebra
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]
and thus naturally a weakly
\mathbb{N}
-graded supermodule.
The
degree

\operatorname{deg}\left(f\right)
, the
even degree

\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f\right)
, the
odd degree

\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f\right)
, the
leading coefficient

\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right)
, the
even leading coefficient

\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f\right)
, and the
odd leading coefficient

\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f\right)
of a super
q
-exponential polynomial
f
are defined to be the degree, the even degree, the odd degree, the leading coefficient, the even leading coefficient, and the odd leading coefficient of its leading term in the order grading.

5.4.11
.

Let
f
be a
q
-exponential polynomial as in
Eq.

5.4.1
.
Then the order of
f
is the largest
\nu\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
f_{\nu}\neq 0
and its leading term is the product of the super
q
-polynomial
f_{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)}
and
q^{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)z}
.

.

Every super
q
-polynomial will be viewed as a super
q
-exponential polynomial which is homogeneous of order
0
.

It is clear that a super
q
-exponential polynomial
f
defines a
q
-function.
We will use the same notion to denote this
q
-function.
Then we have the following asymptotic equalities:

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle\sim\left(\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f\right)\binom{z}{\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f\right)}+\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f\right)\binom{z}{\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f\right)}(-1)^{z}\right)q^{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)z}

(5.4.2)

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right)\binom{z}{\operatorname{deg}\left(f\right)}q^{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)z}.

Note that the leading coefficient
\operatorname{lead}\left(f\right)
is a parity
q
-function rather than a
q
-number.
In particular, the asymptotic behaviors of
f(z)
along even integers and odd integers are different if
\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f\right)=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f\right)
.

5.4.12
.

Let
\operatorname{S}
be a
q
-function and
f
a super
q
-exponential polynomial.
If
\operatorname{S}
can be defined by a super
q
-exponential polynomial whose leading term is the same as
f(z)
, then we will say that
\operatorname{S}

has asymptotic growth

f
and write

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim f(z)

by an abuse of language. Note that this implies that
\operatorname{S}
is asymptotically equal to the
q
-function defined by
f
.

Now, we turn to the discrete calculus.

5.4.13
.

The
difference operator

\operatorname{\Delta}
on super
q
-exponential polynomials is the extension of the difference operator defined in
Definition

5.3.7
satisfying the Leibniz rule
Eq.

5.1.1
and acts on parity
q
-functions as in
Definition

5.1.6
.

.

By
Lemma

5.4.5
, the action of
\operatorname{\Delta}
on parity
q
-functions is determined by its action on
(-1)^{z}
.
Note that
\operatorname{\Delta}{(-1)^{z}}=-2(-1)^{z}
.
Hence, the action of
\operatorname{\Delta}
on the superalgebra of super
q
-exponential polynomials respects the parity.

For a super
q
-exponential polynomial
f
, the
q
-function defined by
\operatorname{\Delta}{f}
is precisely the
difference
of the
q
-function defined by
f
.

The following lemma follows from the definition.

5.4.14
.

The linear operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
respects the parity and the order grading on
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
.
On each homogeneous component
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]q^{\nu z}
(
\nu\neq 0
), the operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
respects the degree filtration, the even degree filtration on its even part, and the odd degree filtration on its odd parts strictly.
On the subalgebra
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]
, the operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
shifts the even degree homogeneously by
-1
and respects the odd degree filtration strictly.

Next, we will introduce the anti-difference operators.

5.4.15
.

The linear operators
\operatorname{\Delta}
admits a section
\operatorname{\Sigma}
such that

(iv)

it respects the parity and the order;

(iv)

on each homogeneous component of order
\nu\neq 0
, it respects the degree filtration, the even degree filtration on its even part, and odd degree filtration on its odd part strictly;

(iv)

on the subalgebra of order
0
, it shifts the even degree homogeneously by
1
and respects the odd degree filtration strictly.

Moreover, if
f
is a
q
-exponential polynomial, then we have the following formulas:

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(q^{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)}-1\right)^{-1}\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f\right)&if $\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)\neq 0$,\\
\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f\right)&if $\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)=0$,\end{cases}

(5.4.3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(q^{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)}+1\right)^{-1}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f\right).

Proof.

First note that the restriction of the linear operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
to each homogeneous component
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]q^{\nu z}
(
\nu\neq 0
) is bijective, while its restriction to the subalgebra
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]
is surjective.
In particular, the linear operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
itself is surjective. Since
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)[(-1)^{z},z]q^{\mathbb{Q}z}
is a free module over
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
, the statements on orders and degrees follows from
Lemma

5.4.14
by applying
Corollaries

5.2.4
,
5.2.11
, and
5.2.3
to the operator
\operatorname{\Delta}
.
The statements on even leading coefficients follows from
Lemma

5.3.9
.
As for the odd leading coefficients,
first note that for each
\nu\in\mathbb{Q}
, we have

\operatorname{\Delta}\left((-1)^{z}\binom{z}{n}q^{\nu z}\right)=-\left(q^{\nu}+1\right)(-1)^{z}\binom{z}{n}q^{\nu z}-q^{\nu}(-1)^{z}\binom{z}{n-1}q^{\nu z}.

Therefore, we have

\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(\operatorname{\Delta}{f}\right)=-\left(q^{\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)}+1\right)\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f\right).

Replacing
f
by
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
,
Eq.

5.3.5
follows.
∎

5.4.16
.

The linear operator
\operatorname{\Sigma}
in
Lemma

5.4.15
is called the
free anti-difference operator
.
Let
a
be an integer.
The
anti-difference operator with anchor
a
, denoted by
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}
, is the linear operator
\operatorname{\Sigma}-\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ\operatorname{\Sigma}
, where
\operatorname{ev}_{a}
evaluates a super
q
-exponential polynomial
f(z)
at
z=a
.

For a super
q
-exponential polynomial
f
, the
q
-function defined by
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}
is the
anti-difference with anchor
a
of the
q
-function defined by
f
.

We end this subsection with discussions of primarity.

5.4.17
.

Let
f
be a super
q
-exponential polynomial as in
Eq.

5.4.1
.
Then
f
is said to be
primary
if its coefficients are primary
q
-numbers and
f_{\nu}=0
for all
\nu\notin\mathbb{Z}
.

Clearly, primary super
q
-exponential polynomials define primary
q
-functions.

5.4.18
.

Let
f
be a
q
-exponential polynomial.
If
\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)>0
or
\operatorname{ord}\left(f\right)=0
with either
\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f\right)\geqslant 0
or
\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f\right)>0
, then the
q
-functions defined by
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
are asymptotically equal to the
q
-function defined by
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
.
If
f
is primary, then so are
\operatorname{\Delta}{f}
,
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
, and
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}
(
a\in\mathbb{Z}
).

Proof.

We have
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}-\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
.
Elements of
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
have order
0
and even degree
0
, while the assumption on
f
implies that
\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
has nonzero order or degree by
Lemma

5.4.15
.
Hence, we have
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{a}{f}\sim\operatorname{\Sigma}{f}
.
The last statement is evident.
∎

5.5
Asymptotic growth of multi-summations

To analyze the growth of the
q
-functions
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
, we need to write them as
q
-exponential polynomials.
This can be done by considering multi-summations of homogeneous (super)
q
-exponential polynomials.
In this subsection, we give some general results.

The strategy is: we will inductively construct a sequence of (super)
q
-exponential polynomials where the final one defines the desired
q
-function, and then we will compare the orders, the degrees, and the leading coefficients of them.

To better describe the results, let’s introduce some conventions.

5.5.1
.

Let
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
be an index set.
The set of functions from
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
to another set
X
will be denoted by
X^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}
.
Such a function
\mathbf{c}
will be identified with a sequence
(c_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}
indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
, where
c_{i}=\mathbf{c}(i)
.
The constant sequence mapping all
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
to
1
will be denoted by
\mathbf{1}
.
If
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\mathbf{c}
are two sequences of real numbers indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
, then
\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{c}
denotes their dot product, namely
\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\mu_{i}c_{i}
.

5.5.2
.

Let
\operatorname{S}
be the
q
-function defined by the following multi-summation

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\colon\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}=z}q^{\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{c}},

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is a sequence of non-negative rational numbers.
Define the following notations:

•

\mu_{\max}
is the maximum of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
;

•

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}
is the set of indices
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
such that
\mu_{i}=\mu_{\max}
.

Then
\operatorname{S}
can be defined by a
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left(q^{\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\rvert-1}q^{\mu_{\max}z}.

Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{\mu}
takes integral values, then the
q
-exponential polynomial is primary.

.

Note that the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}
is eventually positive since the leading coefficient of the
q
-exponential polynomial defining it is positive.

Proof.

First note that the condition on the sequence
\mathbf{c}
of variables is stable under reindexing.
Hence, we may assume
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{1,\cdots,i_{0}\right\}
by reindexing the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
if necessary.
We change the variables from
\mathbf{c}
to
\mathbf{b}
as follows:

\displaystyle b_{i}

\displaystyle:=c_{1}+\cdots+c_{i}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Then we can write
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)
as follows:

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=q^{\nu_{t}z}\sum_{b_{t-1}=t-1}^{z-1}q^{\nu_{t-1}b_{t-1}}\cdots\sum_{b_{1}=1}^{b_{2}-1}q^{\nu_{1}b_{1}},

where
\nu_{i}=\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1
and
\nu_{t}=\mu_{t}
.

To analyze the growth of
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)
, we define
f_{1},\cdots,f_{t}
inductively as follows:

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=q^{\nu_{1}z},

\displaystyle f_{i}(z)

\displaystyle=q^{\nu_{i}z}\operatorname{\Sigma}_{i-1}{f_{i-1}}(z).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<i\leqslant t)}

Then each
f_{i}
is a
q
-exponential polynomial, and we can analyze them by induction.
In particular,
f_{t}
defines the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}
by
Eq.

5.1.2
.
Moreover, if
\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{t}
are integers, then every
f_{i}
is primary by
Lemma

5.3.12
.

For
1<i\leqslant i_{0}
, repeatedly applying
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{ord}\left(f_{i}\right)=\nu_{i}+\operatorname{ord}\left(f_{i-1}\right)=\nu_{i}=\begin{cases}0&if $i<i_{0}$,\\
\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i_{0}+1}&if $i=i_{0}$.\end{cases}

By
(iv)
, we have the following recurrence relations:

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{i-1}\right)+1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{i-1}\right).

In particular, we have
\operatorname{ord}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i_{0}+1}
,
\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=i_{0}-1
, and
\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=1
.

For
i_{0}<i\leqslant t
, repeatedly applying
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{ord}\left(f_{i}\right)=\nu_{i}+\operatorname{ord}\left(f_{i-1}\right)=\nu_{i}+\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}=\begin{cases}\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i+1}&if $i<t$,\\
\mu_{i_{0}}&if $i=t$.\end{cases}

In particular, they are positive.
Then by
(iv)
, we have the following recurrence relations:

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{i-1}\right).

In particular, we have
\operatorname{ord}\left(f_{t}\right)=\mu_{i_{0}}
,
\operatorname{deg}\left(f_{t}\right)=i_{0}-1
, and
\operatorname{lead}\left(f_{t}\right)
equals the product of
\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}
for
i_{0}+1\leqslant i\leqslant t
.

Therefore, we have the following:

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{t}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{z}{i_{0}-1}q^{\mu_{i_{0}}z}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

In the rest of this subsection, we will consider multi-summations involving parity functions.
We first extend
Definition

5.4.1
to the following definition.

5.5.3
.

A
multivariable parity function
(indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
) is a function defined on
\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}
factoring through the projection
\mathbb{Z}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}
.

5.5.4
.

By an abuse of notation, we will use the same notation to denote a multivariable parity function indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
and a function on defined on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}
.
In other words, we will treat any sequence in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
as a sequence in
\mathbb{Z}
by viewing
0\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
and
1\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
as their standard representatives
0\in\mathbb{Z}
and
1\in\mathbb{Z}
.

5.5.5
.

Let
\operatorname{S}
be the
q
-function defined by the following multi-summation

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\colon\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}=z}q^{\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{c}+e(\mathbf{c})},

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is a sequence of non-negative rational numbers and
e
is a multivariable parity function.
Define the following notations:

•

\mu_{\max}
is the maximum of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
;

•

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}
is the set of indices
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
such that
\mu_{i}=\mu_{\max}
.

Then
\operatorname{S}
can be defined by a super
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\left(C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}+C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}(-1)^{z}\right)\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\rvert-1}q^{\mu_{\max}z},

where the constants
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}

\displaystyle:=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}}q^{e(\mathbf{s})+(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}},

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}

\displaystyle:=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}q^{e(\mathbf{s})+(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}},

where
\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}
denotes the sequence
(\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
the following the constant:

C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\frac{1}{2^{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\rvert}}\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left(q^{2(\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}.

Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
e
take integral values, then the super
q
-exponential polynomial is primary.

.

Note that the even leading coefficient
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}
is positive and the odd leading coefficient
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}
satisfies
\left\lvert C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}\right\rvert<C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}
.
Hence, the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}
is eventually positive.
Note that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}
could be
0
, in which case the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)
along even integers and odd integers coincide.

To prove
Lemma

5.5.5
, we begin with some special cases.

5.5.6
.

Let
\operatorname{S}
be the
q
-function defined by the following multi-summation

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\colon\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}=z}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{c}},

where
\mathbf{s}
is a sequence of integers.
Define the following notations:

•

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}
) is the set of indices
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
such that
s_{i}
is even (resp. odd).

Then
\operatorname{S}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-polynomial so that

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}\right\rvert}\binom{z}{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}\right\rvert-1}+\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}\right\rvert}\binom{z}{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}\right\rvert-1}(-1)^{z}.

Proof.

First, if either
\mathbf{s}
contains no even numbers or no odd numbers, then the statement follows from
Lemma

5.5.2
.
We may assume that the sequence
\mathbf{s}
contains at least one even number and one odd number.
Since the condition on the sequence
\mathbf{c}
of variables is stable under reindexing, we may assume
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\circ}=\left\{2,\cdots,i_{0}+1\right\}
by reindexing the sequence
\mathbf{s}
if necessary.
We change the variables from
\mathbf{c}
to
\mathbf{b}
as follows:

\displaystyle b_{i}

\displaystyle:=c_{1}+\cdots+c_{i}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Then we can write
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)
as follows:

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=(-1)^{r_{t}z}\sum_{b_{t-1}=t-1}^{z-1}(-1)^{r_{t-1}b_{t-1}}\cdots\sum_{b_{1}=1}^{b_{2}-1}(-1)^{r_{1}b_{1}},

where
r_{i}=s_{i}-s_{i+1}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1
and
r_{t}=s_{t}
.
Then our assumption implies that
r_{i}
is even whenever
i\notin\left\{1,i_{0}+1,t\right\}
.

To analyze the growth of
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)
, we can define
f_{1},\cdots,f_{t}
inductively as follows:

\displaystyle f_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r_{1}z},

\displaystyle f_{i}(z)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r_{i}z}\operatorname{\Sigma}_{i-1}{f_{i-1}}(z).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<i\leqslant t)}

Note that
f_{1}
fails the condition of
Lemma

5.4.18
.
But we can compute
\operatorname{\Sigma}_{1}f_{1}
directly:

\operatorname{\Sigma}_{1}f_{1}(z)=-\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}(-1)^{z}.

Then each
f_{i}
is a primary super
q
-polynomial by
Lemma

5.4.18
.
Moreover,
f_{t}
defines the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}
by
Eq.

5.1.2
.

For
i\notin\left\{1,i_{0}+1,t\right\}
, since
r_{i}
is even, by
(iv)
, we have the following recurrence relations:

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i-1}\right)+1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i-1}\right).

On the other hand, when
r_{i}
is odd, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i-1}\right)+1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i-1}\right).

If
i_{0}=t-1
, then
r_{t}=r_{i_{0}+1}=s_{t}
is also even, and we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)+1=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{2}\right)+t-2=t-2,

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{2}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{2}\right)=-\tfrac{1}{2},

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{t-2}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{2}\right)=\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{t-1}.

Otherwise, both
r_{t}
and
r_{i_{0}+1}
are odd, and we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i_{0}+1}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)+1=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{2}\right)+i_{0}-2+1=i_{0}-1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)+1=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}+1}\right)+t-i_{0}-2+1

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)+t-i_{0}-1=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{2}\right)+t-i_{0}-1=t-i_{0}-1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=-\tfrac{1}{2}\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{t-i_{0}-2}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i_{0}+1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{t-i_{0}-1}\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\cdots=\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{t-i_{0}-1}\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{2}\right)=\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{t-i_{0}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}+1}\right)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\cdots=-\tfrac{1}{2}\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{i_{0}-2}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{2}\right)=\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{i_{0}}.

Then the lemma follows.
∎

Next, we consider the following situation.

5.5.7
.

Let
\operatorname{S}
be the
q
-function defined by the following multi-summation

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\colon\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}=z}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{c}}q^{\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{c}},

where
\mathbf{s}
is a sequence of integers and
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is a sequence of non-negative rational numbers.
Define the following notations:

•

\mu_{\max}
is the maximum of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
;

•

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}
is the set of indices
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
such that
\mu_{i}=\mu_{\max}
;

•

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}_{\max}
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}_{\max}
) is the set of indices
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}
such that
s_{i}
is even (resp. odd);

•

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}
(resp.
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}
) is the set of indices
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}
such that
s_{i}
is even (resp. odd).

Then
\operatorname{S}
can be defined by a super
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\left(f_{\mathbf{s},\boldsymbol{\mu},0}(z)+f_{\mathbf{s},\boldsymbol{\mu},1}(z)(-1)^{z}\right)q^{\mu_{\max}z},

where
f_{\mathbf{s},\boldsymbol{\mu},\square}(z)
(
\square=0,1
) is the following
q
-polynomial:

f_{\mathbf{s},\boldsymbol{\mu},\square}(z):=\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left((-1)^{\square+s_{i}}q^{\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1-\square}_{\max}\right\rvert}\binom{z}{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\square}_{\max}\right\rvert-1}.

Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{\mu}
takes integral values, then the super
q
-exponential polynomial is primary.

.

Note that the leading coefficient may be negative.
However, if
\mathbf{s}
contains no even numbers (resp. odd numbers), then the even (resp. odd) leading coefficient is zero and the odd (resp. even) leading coefficient is positive.

Proof.

First note that the condition on the sequence
\mathbf{c}
of variables is stable under reindexing.
Hence, we may assume
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
,
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{1,\cdots,i_{0}\right\}
, and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}=\left\{i_{0}+1,\cdots,i_{1}\right\}
by reindexing the sequences
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
\mathbf{s}
if necessary.
We change the variables from
\mathbf{c}
to
\mathbf{b}
as follows:

\displaystyle b_{i}

\displaystyle:=c_{1}+\cdots+c_{i}.

\displaystyle\mathllap{(i_{0}\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Then we can write
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)
as follows:

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=(-1)^{r_{t}z}q^{\nu_{t}z}\sum_{b_{t-1}=t-1}^{z-1}(-1)^{r_{t-1}b_{t-1}}q^{\nu_{t-1}b_{t-1}}\cdots\sum_{b_{i_{0}}=i_{0}}^{b_{i_{0}+1}-1}(-1)^{-s_{i_{0}+1}b_{i_{0}}}q^{\nu_{i_{0}}b_{i_{0}}}\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\left(b_{i_{0}}\right),

where

•

r_{i}=s_{i}-s_{i+1}
for
i_{0}+1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1
and
r_{t}=s_{t}
;

•

\nu_{i}=\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}
for
i_{0}\leqslant i\leqslant t-1
and
\nu_{t}=\mu_{t}
;

•

\mathbf{s}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}
is the subsequence of
\mathbf{s}
indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}
and the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}
is defined as follows:

\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\left(z\right):=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\colon\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}=z}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\cdot\mathbf{c}}.

By
Lemma

5.5.6
, the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-polynomial
f
, for which we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}_{\max}\right\rvert-1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}_{\max}\right\rvert-1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}_{\max}\right\rvert},

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}_{\max}\right\rvert}.

To analyze the growth of
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)
, we can define
f_{i_{0}},\cdots,f_{t}
inductively as follows:

\displaystyle f_{i_{0}}(z)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{-s_{i_{0}+1}z}q^{\nu_{i_{0}}z}f(z),

\displaystyle f_{i}(z)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r_{i}z}q^{\nu_{i}z}\operatorname{\Sigma}_{i-1}{f_{i-1}}(z).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(i_{0}<i\leqslant t)}

Then each
f_{i}
is a super
q
-exponential polynomial, and we can analyze them by induction.
In particular,
f_{t}
defines the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}
by
Eq.

5.1.2
.
Moreover, if
\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{t}
are integers, then every
f_{i}
is primary by
Lemma

5.4.18
.

For each
i_{0}<i\leqslant t
, repeatedly applying
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{ord}\left(f_{i}\right)=\nu_{i}+\operatorname{ord}\left(f_{i-1}\right)=\nu_{i}+\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}=\begin{cases}\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i+1}&if $i<t$,\\
\mu_{i_{0}}&if $i=t$.\end{cases}

When
i\notin\left\{i_{1},t\right\}
, since
r_{i}
is even, by
(iv)
, we have the following recurrence relations:

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i-1}\right).

On the other hand, when
r_{i}
is odd, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i-1}\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i-1}\right).

If
i_{1}=t
, namely
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}=\emptyset
, then both
s_{i_{0}+1}
and
r_{t}=s_{t}
are even, and we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}_{\max}\right\rvert-1=t-1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}_{\max}\right\rvert-1=-1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{t}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{t}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{t}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}_{\max}\right\rvert}.

If
i_{1}=i_{0}
, namely
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}=\emptyset
, then both
s_{i_{0}+1}
and
r_{t}=s_{t}
are odd, and we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}_{\max}\right\rvert-1=-1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}_{\max}\right\rvert-1=t-1,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{t}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{t}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{t}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}_{\max}\right\rvert}.

If
i_{0}<i_{1}<t
, then
s_{i_{0}+1}
is even while
r_{i_{1}}
and
r_{t}=s_{t}
are odd. We thus have

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{i_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i_{1}-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}_{\max}\right\rvert-1\geqslant 0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}_{1}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i_{1}-1}\right)=\cdots=\operatorname{deg}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)=\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}_{\max}\right\rvert-1\geqslant 0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{t}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\prod_{i=i_{1}+1}^{t}\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=i_{1}+1}^{t}\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i_{1}}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i_{1}-1}\right)=\cdots

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=i_{1}+1}^{t}\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{i_{1}}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=i_{1}+1}^{t}\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{i_{1}}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}_{\max}\right\rvert},

\displaystyle\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{t}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{t-1}\right)=\cdots=\prod_{i=i_{1}+1}^{t}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{0}\left(f_{i_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=i_{1}+1}^{t}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i_{1}}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i_{1}-1}\right)=\cdots

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=i_{1}+1}^{t}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{i_{1}}\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{lead}_{1}\left(f_{i_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=i_{1}+1}^{t}\left(q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\prod_{i=i_{0}+1}^{i_{1}}\left(-q^{\mu_{i_{0}}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}_{\max}\right\rvert}.

Then the lemma follows.
∎

To deduce
Lemma

5.5.5
from
Lemma

5.5.7
, we need the following notions.

5.5.8
.

Let
e
be a multivariable parity function indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
.
Then its
Fourier transform

\widehat{e}
is the following:

\widehat{e}(-):=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}}e(\mathbf{s})(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot-}.

5.5.9
.

It is often the case that the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
admits a partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
.
For any sequence
\mathbf{c}
indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
, we will use
\mathbf{c}_{1}
and
\mathbf{c}_{2}
to denote the subsequences of
\mathbf{c}
indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}
and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
respectively.
Conversely, if
\mathbf{c}_{1}
and
\mathbf{c}_{2}
are two sequences indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}
and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
respectively, then we will use
\mathbf{c}_{1}\sqcup\mathbf{c}_{2}
to denote the sequence indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
obtained from them.

We have the following multivariable version of
Lemma

5.4.5
.

5.5.10
.

Let
e
be a multivariable parity function indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
.
Then we have

e(\mathbf{c})=\frac{1}{2^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\right\rvert}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}}\widehat{e}(\mathbf{s})(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{c}}.

Moreover, if
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
admits a partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
, then we have

\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}}e(\mathbf{s}_{1}\sqcup\mathbf{c}_{2})(-1)^{\mathbf{s}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}_{1}}=\frac{1}{2^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}\right\rvert}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}}\widehat{e}(\mathbf{c}_{1}\sqcup\mathbf{s}_{2})(-1)^{\mathbf{s}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{c}_{2}}.

Proof.

This follows from the general theory of Fourier transforms on finite Abelian groups (see e.g.
[
10
]
*chap. XVIII, §5 and §6).
To verify the lemma directly, note that for any index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
and any sequence
\mathbf{c}
indexed by
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
, we have

\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{c}}=\begin{cases}2^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\right\rvert}&if $\mathbf{\overline{c}}=0$,\\
0&otherwise.\end{cases}

Then the statement follows by straightforward computations.
∎

We are now able to prove
Lemma

5.5.5
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.5.5
.

By
Lemma

5.5.10
, we can write
\operatorname{S}
as follows:

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\frac{1}{2^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\right\rvert}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}}\widehat{q^{e(-)}}(\mathbf{s})\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}}\left(z\right),

where each
\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}}
is defined as follows:

\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\colon\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}=z}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{c}}q^{\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{c}}.

By
Lemma

5.5.7
, each
\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}}
can be defined by a super
q
-exponential polynomial of order
\mu_{\max}
, even degree
\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{0}_{\max}\right\rvert-1
, and odd degree
\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{1}_{\max}\right\rvert-1
(see there for the notations). Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{\mu}
takes integral values, then these super
q
-exponential polynomials are primary.

Note that the degree of
\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}}
achieves its maximum
\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\right\rvert-1
if and only if
\mathbf{s}
contains no odd numbers or no even numbers.
Furthermore, in that case, both the even and odd leading coefficients of
\operatorname{S}_{\mathbf{s}}
are non-negative (indeed, one is zero and another is positive).

Therefore,
\operatorname{S}
can be defined by a super
q
-exponential polynomial of order
\mu_{\max}
and degree
\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\right\rvert-1
, and if
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and
e
take integral values, then this super
q
-exponential polynomial is primary.
Moreover, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:lem:MultiSumSEP:ast}\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{1}{2^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\right\rvert}}\operatorname{E}\left(z\right)\binom{z}{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\right\rvert-1}q^{\mu_{\max}z},

(
\ast
)

where the parity
q
-function
\operatorname{E}
is given as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\widehat{q^{e(-)}}(\mathbf{0}\sqcup\mathbf{s})\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left((-1)^{s_{i}}q^{\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\widehat{q^{e(-)}}(\mathbf{1}\sqcup\mathbf{s})\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left((-1)^{1+s_{i}}q^{\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot(-1)^{z}.

To deduce the formula in
Lemma

5.5.5
from above one, note that

\displaystyle\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left(q^{2(\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i})}-1\right)\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left((-1)^{s_{i}}q^{\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left((-1)^{s_{i}}q^{\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i}}+1\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}q^{(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}},

and similarly

\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left(q^{2(\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i})}-1\right)\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left((-1)^{1+s_{i}}q^{\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}=\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}(-1)^{(\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{s})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}q^{(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left(q^{2(\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i})}-1\right)\cdot\operatorname{E}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\widehat{q^{e(-)}}(\mathbf{0}\sqcup\mathbf{s})\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}q^{(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}
