\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\widehat{q^{e(-)}}(\mathbf{1}\sqcup\mathbf{s})\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}(-1)^{(\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{s})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}q^{(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\cdot(-1)^{z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\widehat{q^{e(-)}}(\mathbf{0}\sqcup\mathbf{s})(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\right)q^{(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\widehat{q^{e(-)}}(\mathbf{1}\sqcup\mathbf{s})(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\right)q^{(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\cdot(-1)^{z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}.

By
Lemma

5.5.10
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{\left\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\right\rvert}}\cdot\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}\left(q^{2(\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i})}-1\right)\cdot\operatorname{E}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}q^{e(\mathbf{s}\sqcup\mathbf{s}^{\prime})}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{0}}\right)q^{(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}}}q^{e(\mathbf{s}\sqcup\mathbf{s}^{\prime})}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}}\right)q^{(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\cdot(-1)^{z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}}q^{e(\mathbf{s})+(\mu_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\cdot\mathbf{s}}\left(1+(-1)^{z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\right).

Apply this to
Eq.

∗

, then
Lemma

5.5.5
follows.
∎

5.6
Asymptotic growth of non-balanced multi-summations

This subsection aims to apply the results in
Section

5.5
to get asymptotic growth of multi-summations which are
non-balanced
in the sense that the summation condition of variables is no longer
\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}=z
.
In our applications, the coefficients in the summation condition can only be either
1
or
2
, see
(vii)
,
(vii)
,
(vii)
, and
(vii)
.
Hence, we will assume that the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
admits a partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
and then follow
Convention

5.5.9
.

5.6.1
.

Let
\operatorname{S}
be the
q
-function defined by the following multi-summation

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\colon\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}_{1}+2(\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}_{2})=z}q^{\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{c}},

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is a sequence of non-negative rational numbers.
For
\square=1,2
,
define the following notations:

•

\mu_{\square\max}
is the maximum of
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\square}
;

•

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\square\max}
is the set of indices
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\square}
such that
\mu_{i}=\mu_{\square\max}
.

Then
\operatorname{S}
can be defined by a super
q
-exponential polynomial.

(iv)

If
2\mu_{1\max}>\mu_{2\max}
, then we have

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}}q^{(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}})\cdot\mathbf{s}}\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}\rvert-1}q^{\mu_{1\max}z},

where
\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}
denotes the sequence
(\mu_{1\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}
and the constant
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is defined as follows:

C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}\left(q^{2\mu_{1\max}-2\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}\left(q^{2\mu_{1\max}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}.

(iv)

If
2\mu_{1\max}<\mu_{2\max}
, then we have

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\left(C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},0}+C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},1}(-1)^{z}\right)\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}\rvert-1}q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}z},

where the constants
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},0}
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},1}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},0}

\displaystyle=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}}q^{(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}},

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},1}

\displaystyle=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}q^{(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}},

where
\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
denotes the sequence
(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}
and the constant
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is defined as follows:

C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\frac{1}{2^{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}\rvert}}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}\left(q^{\mu_{2\max}-2\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}}\left(q^{\mu_{2\max}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}.

(iv)

If
2\mu_{1\max}=\mu_{2\max}
, then we have

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}}q^{(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}})\cdot\mathbf{s}}\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}\rvert+\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}\rvert-1}q^{\mu_{1\max}z},

where
\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}
denotes the sequence
(\mu_{1\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}
and the constant
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is defined as follows:

C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\frac{1}{2^{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}\rvert}}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}\left(q^{2\mu_{1\max}-2\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}}\left(q^{2\mu_{1\max}-\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}.

Moreover, if both
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
and
\tfrac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
take integral values, then the super
q
-exponential polynomial is primary.

.

Note that the even leading coefficient
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},0}
is positive and the odd leading coefficient
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},1}
satisfies
\left\lvert C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},1}\right\rvert<C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},0}
.
Hence, the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}
is eventually positive.
Note that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},1}
could be
0
, in which case the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)
along even integers and odd integers coincide.

In the proof of above lemma and its many applications, a fundamental trick is to extend the domain of a
q
-function to include non-integers.
If this
q
-function is defined by a (super)
q
-exponential polynomial, then it is clear how to do this: simply evaluate this (super)
q
-exponential polynomial.
On the other hand, when the
q
-function is given by a (multi-)summation, it is natural to define its value at non-integer points being zero.
However, keep these two conventions may cause confusions especially when an asymptotic equality connecting a (multi-)summation and a (super)
q
-exponential polynomial is provided.
Hence, we will abandon the second convention and use the following one instead.

5.6.2
.

Suppose
\operatorname{S}
is a
q
-function such that it can be defined by a (super)
q
-exponential polynomial
f
.
When we write
\operatorname{S}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}n\right)
, where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we actually mean the evaluation of
f(z)
at
z=\tfrac{1}{2}n
.
Note that this may cause
\operatorname{S}
having nonzero value at a half-integer even though
\operatorname{S}
may be given by a (multi-)summation.

Then by
Lemma

5.4.5
, the
q
-function that gives
\operatorname{S}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}z\right)
when
z
is even and
0
when
z
is odd is the following one:

\tfrac{1}{2}(1+(-1)^{z})\operatorname{S}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}z\right).

Proof of
Lemma

5.6.1
.

By introducing new variables
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}
and replacing
\mathbf{c}_{1}
by
2\mathbf{c}_{1}-\mathbf{s}
, we can write the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}
as follows:

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}},\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}\\
2(\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c})=z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}}q^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}+(2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{c}}.

Consider the following
q
-function:

\operatorname{S}^{\prime}\left(z\right):=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\colon\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}=z}q^{\boldsymbol{\mu^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{c}},

where
\boldsymbol{\mu^{\prime}}
is the sequence
2\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
.
Then we have

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}\colon z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}\in 2\mathbb{Z}}q^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\operatorname{S}^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s})\right).

Note that the summation only takes over those sequence
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}
satisfying
z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence, following
Convention

5.6.2
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SintoBanlace}\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}}\tfrac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\right)q^{-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\operatorname{S}^{\prime}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s})\right).

(
\ast
)

By
Lemma

5.5.2
, the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}^{\prime}
can be defined by a
q
-exponential polynomial
f
which is primary when
\boldsymbol{\mu^{\prime}}
only contains integers.
Moreover, we have

\operatorname{S}^{\prime}\left(z\right)\sim\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}}\left(q^{\mu^{\prime}_{\max}-\mu^{\prime}_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}\rvert-1}q^{\mu^{\prime}_{\max}z},

where
\mu^{\prime}_{\max}
is the maximum of the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu^{\prime}}
and
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}
is the set of indices
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
such that
\mu^{\prime}_{i}
achieves this maximum.
Applying this to
Eq.

∗

and noticing that

\binom{\tfrac{1}{2}(z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s})}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}\rvert-1}q^{\mu^{\prime}_{\max}\tfrac{1}{2}(z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s})}\sim\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}\rvert-1}q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime}_{\max}\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}\rvert-1}q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime}_{\max}z},

we obtain the following asymptotic equality:

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tfrac{1}{2^{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}\rvert}}\prod_{i\notin\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}}\left(q^{\mu^{\prime}_{\max}-\mu^{\prime}_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}}\left(1+(-1)^{z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\right)q^{(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime}_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}}\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}\rvert-1}q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime}_{\max}z}.

In the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu^{\prime}}
, the maximum
\mu^{\prime}_{\max}
is
\max\left\{2\mu_{1\max},\mu_{2\max}\right\}
, and we have

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}^{\prime}_{\max}=\begin{cases}\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}&if $2\mu_{1\max}>\mu_{2\max}$,\\
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}&if $2\mu_{1\max}<\mu_{2\max}$,\\
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}&if $2\mu_{1\max}=\mu_{2\max}$.\end{cases}

In the first and the third case, the sequence
\tfrac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime}_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
contains a zero.
Hence, we have

\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}}(-1)^{z+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}q^{(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime}_{\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}}=0.

Then the asymptotic relations in the lemma follows.
Note that the proof of
Lemma

5.5.2
also shows that
f(\tfrac{1}{2}z)
is a primary
q
-exponential polynomial if
\boldsymbol{\mu^{\prime}}
only contains even integers.
Then the last statement follows.
∎

The following lemma will not be used in this paper.
It can be deduced from
Lemma

5.5.5
similarly to
Lemma

5.6.1

5.6.3
.

Let
\operatorname{S}
be the
q
-function defined by the following multi-summation

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}}\colon\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}_{1}+2(\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{c}_{2})=z}q^{\boldsymbol{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{c}+e(\mathbf{c})},

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is a sequence of non-negative rational numbers and
e
is a multivariable parity function.
For
\square=1,2
,
define the following notations:

•

\mu_{\square\max}
is the maximum of
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\square}
;

•

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\square\max}
is the set of indices
i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\square}
such that
\mu_{i}=\mu_{\square\max}
.

Then
\operatorname{S}
can be defined by a super
q
-exponential polynomial.

(iv)

If
2\mu_{1\max}>\mu_{2\max}
, then we have

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\left(C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}+C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}(-1)^{z}\right)\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}\rvert-1}q^{\mu_{1\max}z},

where the constants
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}

\displaystyle=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}}q^{2(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}},\mathbf{s}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}}q^{e(\mathbf{s}_{0}\sqcup\mathbf{s}_{2})+(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}+(2\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{2}},

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}

\displaystyle=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}}q^{2(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}},\mathbf{s}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}}q^{e(\mathbf{s}_{0}\sqcup\mathbf{s}_{2})+(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}+(2\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{2}},

where
\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}
denotes the sequence
(\mu_{1\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}
,
\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
the sequence
(\mu_{1\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}
,
2\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
the sequence
(2\mu_{1\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}
, and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
the following constant:

C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\frac{1}{2^{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}\rvert}}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}\left(q^{4\mu_{1\max}-4\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}\left(q^{4\mu_{1\max}-2\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}.

(iv)

If
2\mu_{1\max}<\mu_{2\max}
, then we have

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\left(C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}+C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}(-1)^{z}\right)\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}\rvert-1}q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}z},

where the constants
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}

\displaystyle=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathbf{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}},\mathbf{s}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}}\left(1+(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{1}+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{2}}\right)\Big(

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad q^{e(\mathbf{s}_{0}\sqcup\mathbf{s}_{2})+(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}+2(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{1}+(\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{2}}\Big),

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}

\displaystyle=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathbf{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}},\mathbf{s}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}}\left((-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}}+(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{1}+\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{2}}\right)\Big(

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad q^{e(\mathbf{s}_{0}\sqcup\mathbf{s}_{2})+(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}+2(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{1}+(\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{2}}\Big),

where
\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
denotes the sequence
(\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{2\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}
,
\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
the sequence
(\mu_{2\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}
, and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
the following the constant:

C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\frac{1}{2^{2\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}\rvert}}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}\left(q^{2\mu_{2\max}-4\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}}\left(q^{2\mu_{2\max}-2\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}.

(iv)

If
2\mu_{1\max}=\mu_{2\max}
, then we have

\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)\sim\left(C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}+C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}(-1)^{z}\right)\cdot\binom{z}{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}\rvert+\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}\rvert-1}q^{\mu_{1\max}z},

where the constants
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}
and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}

\displaystyle=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}}q^{2(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}},\mathbf{s}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}}q^{e(\mathbf{s}_{0}\sqcup\mathbf{s}_{2})+(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}+(\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{2}},

\displaystyle C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}

\displaystyle=C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}}q^{2(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}},\mathbf{s}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}}q^{e(\mathbf{s}_{0}\sqcup\mathbf{s}_{2})+(\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{0}+(2\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{s}_{2}},

where
\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}|_{\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}
denotes the sequence
(\mu_{1\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}
,
\mu_{1\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
the sequence
(\mu_{1\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}}
,
\mu_{2\max}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
the sequence
(\mu_{2\max}-\mu_{i})_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}}
, and
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
the following constant:

C_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\frac{1}{2^{\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}\rvert+2\lvert\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}\rvert}}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}}\left(q^{4\mu_{1\max}-4\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}\setminus\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}}\left(q^{2\mu_{2\max}-2\mu_{i}}-1\right)^{-1}.

Moreover, if both
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}
and
\tfrac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
take integral values, then the super
q
-exponential polynomial is primary.

.

Note that the even leading coefficient
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}
is positive and the odd leading coefficient
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}
satisfies
\left\lvert C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}\right\rvert<C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,0}
.
Hence, the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}
is eventually positive.
Note that
C_{\boldsymbol{\mu},e,1}
could be
0
, in which case the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}\left(z\right)
along even integers and odd integers coincide.

§ 6  Simplicial volume in buildings of
A_{n}
type

In this section, we will prove the
A_{n}
part of
Theorems

1.1
and
1.4
.
More precisely, we will prove the following stronger theorem.

6.1
.

Let
{B}
be a Bruhat-Tits building of split classical type
A_{n}
over a local field
K
with residue cardinality
q
.
Then the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it can be defined by primary
q
-exponential polynomials whose leading terms are of the form:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}(n)\cdot r^{\varepsilon(n)}q^{\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim C(n)\cdot r^{\varepsilon(n)}q^{\pi(n)r},

where
\varepsilon(n)=0
and
\pi(n)=(\tfrac{n+1}{2})^{2}
if
n
is odd, while
\varepsilon(n)=1
and
\pi(n)=\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{2}+1)
if
n
is even.

Moreover, we will obtain explicit formulas for the constants
\tilde{C}(n)
and
C(n)
.

By the discussion at the beginning of
Section

5
,
this can be done as follows.
First, we will compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
for each type
I\subseteq\Delta
.
This allows us to find the dominant ones.
On the other hand, by
Eq.

3.4.6
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SSA=SumSI:An}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

(6.0.1)

Then we can obtain the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
.

6.1
Asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.
Since all vertices are special, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I,r\right)}q^{2\rho(x)}.

Then by
Eqs.

4.3.3
and
(vii)
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\ell_{i}(n+1-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to above summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(n+1-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary
q
-exponential polynomial.
Note that, by
Eq.

6.0.1
, this already implies that
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by primary
q
-exponential polynomials.

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that either
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}
is a singleton
\left\{i_{0}\right\}
or it consists of two consecutive indices
\left\{i_{0},i_{0}+1\right\}
.

If
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{i_{0}\right\}
.
Then
\mu_{\max}=\ell_{i_{0}}(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}})
,

\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i}=(\ell_{i_{0}}-\ell_{i})(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}}-\ell_{i})
,
and we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:AsymptoticOfSI:An:1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i\neq i_{0}}\left(q^{(\ell_{i_{0}}-\ell_{i})(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\ell_{i_{0}}(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}})r}.

(6.1.1)

In particular,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
has order
\ell_{i_{0}}(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}})
and degree
0
.

If
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{i_{0},i_{0}+1\right\}
.
Then
\mu_{\max}=\ell_{i_{0}}(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}})
,

\mu_{\max}-\mu_{i}=(\ell_{i_{0}}-\ell_{i})(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}}-\ell_{i})
,
and we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:AsymptoticOfSI:An:2}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i\neq i_{0},i_{0}+1}\left(q^{(\ell_{i_{0}}-\ell_{i})(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{\ell_{i_{0}}(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}})r}.

(6.1.2)

In particular,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
has order
\ell_{i_{0}}(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}})
and degree
1
.

6.2
Dominant types

Now, we are able to figure out for which type
I
, the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
is dominant among its siblings. We will say that such a type is
dominant
.

When
n
is odd, we have

\mu_{\max}=\ell_{i_{0}}(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}})\leqslant(\frac{n+1}{2})^{2}.

The equality achieves exactly when
\ell_{i_{0}}=\frac{n+1}{2}
.
Therefore,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
is dominant exactly when
\tfrac{n+1}{2}\notin{I}
.
In this case, we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{i_{0}\right\}
and
\ell_{i_{0}}=\frac{n+1}{2}
.

When
n
is even, we have

\mu_{\max}=\ell_{i_{0}}(n+1-\ell_{i_{0}})\leqslant\frac{n}{2}\left(\frac{n}{2}+1\right).

The equality achieves exactly when
\ell_{i_{0}}=\frac{n}{2}
or
\frac{n}{2}+1
.
Therefore,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
is dominant only if
\left\{\tfrac{n}{2},\tfrac{n}{2}+1\right\}\not\subseteq{I}
.
There are three cases:
if
\left\{\tfrac{n}{2},\tfrac{n}{2}+1\right\}\cap{I}=\left\{\tfrac{n}{2}+1\right\}
, we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{i_{0}\right\}
and
\ell_{i_{0}}=\frac{n}{2}
;
if
\left\{\tfrac{n}{2},\tfrac{n}{2}+1\right\}\cap{I}=\left\{\tfrac{n}{2}\right\}
, we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{i_{0}\right\}
and
\ell_{i_{0}}=\frac{n}{2}+1
;
if
\left\{\tfrac{n}{2},\tfrac{n}{2}+1\right\}\cap{I}=\emptyset
, we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{i_{0},i_{0}+1\right\}
,
\ell_{i_{0}}=\frac{n}{2}
, and
\ell_{i_{0}+1}=\frac{n}{2}+1
.
Among them, the last one gives the dominant type since
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
has degree
1
in that case while it has degree
0
in the first two cases.

6.3
Asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

We are now going to obtain the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
.
By
Eq.

6.0.1
, we have

\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\sim\sum_{I\text{ is dominant}}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

When
n
is odd, by
Section

6.2
and
Eq.

6.1.1
, we see that
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by a primary
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:An:odd:SSA}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\sim C(n)\cdot q^{(\frac{n+1}{2})^{2}r},

(6.3.1)

where the constant
C(n)
is defined as follows:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:An:odd:C}C(n):=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{I\subseteq\Delta\\
\tfrac{n+1}{2}\notin{I}}}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{\crampedsubstack{1\leqslant i\leqslant t_{I}\\
\ell_{i}(I)\neq\tfrac{n+1}{2}}}\left(q^{\left(\ell_{i}(I)-\tfrac{n+1}{2}\right)^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}.

(6.3.2)

As a consequence, we see that
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by a primary
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:An:odd:SV}\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{(\frac{n+1}{2})^{2}}}{q^{(\frac{n+1}{2})^{2}}-1}C(n)\cdot q^{(\frac{n+1}{2})^{2}r},

(6.3.3)

When
n
is even, by
Section

6.2
and
Eq.

6.1.2
, we see that
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by a primary
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:An:even:SSA}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\sim C(n)\cdot rq^{\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{2}+1)r},

(6.3.4)

where the constant
C(n)
is defined as follows:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:An:even:C}C(n):=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{I\subseteq\Delta\\
\tfrac{n}{2},\tfrac{n}{2}+1\notin{I}}}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{\crampedsubstack{1\leqslant i\leqslant t_{I}\\
\ell_{i}(I)\neq\tfrac{n}{2},\tfrac{n}{2}+1}}\left(q^{(\ell_{i}(I)-\frac{n}{2})(\ell_{i}(I)-\frac{n}{2}-1)}-1\right)^{-1}.

(6.3.5)

As a consequence, we see that
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by a primary
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:An:even:SV}\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{2}+1)}}{q^{\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{2}+1)}-1}C(n)\cdot q^{\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{2}+1)r},

(6.3.6)

Since all vertices are special, we have
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)=\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)=\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
, where
\dagger
denotes “being special”.

By
Eqs.

6.3.1
,
6.3.2
,
6.3.3
,
6.3.4
,
6.3.5
, and
6.3.6
,
we have proved
Theorem

6.1
.
Moreover, by
Eq.

A.1.4
, we have the following explicit formulas for the first factor of
C(n)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{n+1}{
\ell_{1}(I),\ell_{2}(I)-\ell_{1}(I),\cdots,\ell_{t}(I)-\ell_{t-1}(I),n+1-\ell_{t}(I)
}(q),

\displaystyle q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n};I}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{\binom{n+1}{2}}}{q^{\binom{\ell_{1}(I)}{2}}q^{\binom{\ell_{2}(I)-\ell_{1}(I)}{2}}\cdots q^{\binom{\ell_{t}(I)-\ell_{t-1}(I)}{2}}q^{\binom{n+1-\ell_{t}(I)}{2}}}.

See
Eq.

A.1.3
for the definition of the symbol
\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{\:\cdot\:}{\:\cdot\:,\cdots,\:\cdot\:}
.

§ 7  Simplicial volume in buildings of
C_{n}
type

In this section, we will prove the
C_{n}
part of
Theorems

1.1
and
1.4
.
More precisely, we will prove the following stronger theorem.

7.1
.

Let
{B}
be a Bruhat-Tits building of split classical type
C_{n}
over a local field
K
with residue cardinality
q
.
Then the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it can be defined by primary super
q
-exponential polynomials whose leading terms are of the form:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim C(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r},

where
\tilde{C}(n)
and
C(n)
are primary
q
-numbers, not just parity
q
-functions.

Moreover, we will obtain explicit formulas for the constants
\tilde{C}(n)
and
C(n)
.

By the discussion at the beginning of
Section

5
,
this can be done as follows.
First, we estimate the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
for each type
I\subseteq\Delta
using the auxiliary function
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
in
Section

7.1
.
This allows us to find the dominant ones in
Section

7.3
.
Then we can compute the leading coefficient of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
for dominant ones in
Section

7.5
.
Finally, by
Eq.

3.4.6
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SSA=SumSI:Cn}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\sum_{I\text{ is dominant}}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

(7.0.1)

Then we can obtain the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
.

Along the discussion, we will also consider the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
, where
\dagger
denotes “being special”. Namely, we will also prove the following theorem (in
Sections

7.2
and
7.4
).

7.2
.

Let
{B}
be a Bruhat-Tits building of split classical type
C_{n}
over a local field
K
with residue cardinality
q
.
Then the special simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the special simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it can be defined by primary super
q
-exponential polynomials whose leading terms are of the form:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim C_{\dagger}(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r},

where
\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)
and
C_{\dagger}(n)
are primary
q
-numbers, not just parity
q
-functions.

Moreover, we will obtain explicit formulas for the constants
\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)
and
C_{\dagger}(n)
.

7.1
Asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to estimate the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
up to the leading coefficient.

By
Eqs.

4.4.3
and
(vii)
, we have

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n+1-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to above summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n+1-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary
q
-exponential polynomial.
The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n+1-\ell_{t})
.
Then we have

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n+1-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n+1-\ell_{t})r}.

Since
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
, we see that
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
has order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n+1-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.

7.2
Asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Next, we are going to compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.

If
\ell_{t}<n
, then by
(vii)
,
4.4.2
, and
4.4.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
2c_{1}+\cdots+2c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n+1-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\emptyset\sqcup\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n+1-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are even integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t}(2n+1-\ell_{t})
.
Then by
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n+1-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n+1-\ell_{t})r}.

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n+1-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.

If
\ell_{t}=n
, then by
(vii)
,
4.4.2
, and
4.4.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
2c_{1}+\cdots+2c_{t-1}+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\ell_{i}(2n+1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}c_{t}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\left\{t\right\}\sqcup\left\{1,\cdots,t-1\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n+1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i<t)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}.

Since
\mu_{t}
is an integer and all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
are even integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t-1\right\}
,
\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t-1}(2n+1-\ell_{t-1})
, and
2\mu_{1\max}>\mu_{2\max}
.
Then by
(iv)
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:AsymptoticOfSdaggerI:Cn}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n+1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r}.

(7.2.1)

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}
and degree
0
.

7.3
Dominant types

Now, we are able to figure out which type is dominant.

We begin with
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
, since
\ell_{t}\leqslant n
, we have

\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n+1-\ell_{t})\leqslant\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}.

The equality achieves exactly when
\ell_{t}=n
.
Therefore,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
is dominant exactly when
n\notin{I}
. In this case, we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
and
\ell_{t}=n
.

Next, we consider
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
.
Then similar argument shows that it is dominant exactly when
n\notin{I}
.

7.4
Asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

We are now able to obtain the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
.

By
Eq.

7.0.1
,
Eq.

7.2.1
, and
Section

7.3
, we see that
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:Cn:SSAdagger}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\dagger}(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r},

(7.4.1)

where the constant
C_{\dagger}(n)
is defined as follows:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:Cn:Cdagger}C_{\dagger}(n):=\sum_{n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=1}^{t_{I}-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))(n+1-\ell_{i}(I))}-1\right)^{-1}.

(7.4.2)

As a consequence, we see that
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:Cn:SVdagger}\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}}}{q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}}-1}C_{\dagger}(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r}.

(7.4.3)

7.5
Asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
when
I
is dominant.

First, we need to write
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
into a multi-summation.
To do this, we pick an arbitrary
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)
and investigate the difference between
2\rho(x)
and the sum of
\lceil a(x)\rceil
for
a(x)>0
.
To better describe this sum, we introduce the following conventions.

7.5.1
.

For any
j\in\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\}
, we will use
\ell^{-1}(j)
to denote the index
i\in\left\{1,\cdots,t+1\right\}
such that
\ell_{i-1}<j\leqslant\ell_{i}
, where
\ell_{t+1}=n
.

7.5.2
.

We will use
\overline{z}
to denote
standard parity function
mapping even numbers to
0
and odd numbers to
1
. Note that
\left\lceil\tfrac{1}{2}z\right\rceil=\tfrac{1}{2}(z+\overline{z})
.

7.5.3
.

The summation
c_{i}+\cdots+c_{j}
is read to be
0
when
i>j
.

Now, suppose
x=o+c_{1}\omega_{\ell_{1}}^{\prime}+\cdots+c_{t}\omega_{\ell_{t}}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)
. By
Eq.

4.4.1
, we have

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}-\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right),

\displaystyle{(1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}+\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right)+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})}+\cdots+c_{t},

\displaystyle{(1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle 2\chi_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t}.

\displaystyle{(1\leqslant j\leqslant n)}

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n}\left(\lceil(\chi_{j}-\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)\rceil+\lceil(\chi_{j}+\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)\rceil\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lceil 2\chi_{j}(x)\rceil

\displaystyle=2\rho(x)+\sum_{1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n}\overline{c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}}.

From above analysis, we can define the parity function
e_{I}
as follows:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:DefineEpsilon:Cn}e_{I}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}):=\sum_{1\leqslant i<i^{\prime}\leqslant t+1}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})(\ell_{i^{\prime}}-\ell_{i^{\prime}-1})\overline{c_{i}+\cdots+c_{i^{\prime}-1}}.

(7.5.1)

Then we have

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil=2\rho(x)+e_{I}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.5
to the following summation.

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n+1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+e_{I}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})}.

Note that the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n+1-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers and
e_{I}
is valued in integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
Note that, by
Eq.

7.0.1
, this already implies that
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by primary super
q
-exponential polynomials.

Now, let
I
be a dominant type, namely
n\notin I
.
Then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
,
\ell_{t}=n
, and
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}
.
Therefore,

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\left(C_{I,0}+C_{I,1}(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r},

where the constants
C_{I,0}
and
C_{I,1}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{I,0}

\displaystyle:=C_{I}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{I}(\mathbf{s}),

\displaystyle C_{I,1}

\displaystyle:=C_{I}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\operatorname{E}_{I}(\mathbf{s}),

where the constant
C_{I}
and the function
\operatorname{E}_{I}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}\to\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{I}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n+1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}_{I}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=q^{e_{I}(\mathbf{s})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})(n+1-\ell_{i})s_{i}}.

(7.5.2)

From the definition
Eq.

7.5.1
of
e_{I}
, it is clear that

\operatorname{E}_{I}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{t-1},0)=\operatorname{E}_{I}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{t-1},1).

Therefore,
C_{I,1}=0
and we thus have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:AsymptoticOfSI:Cn}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{I}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t-1}}2\operatorname{E}_{I}\left(\mathbf{s}\sqcup 0\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r},

(7.5.3)

where
\mathbf{s}\sqcup 0
is the sequence
s_{1},\cdots,s_{t-1},0
.

7.6
Asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

We are now going to obtain the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
.

By
Eq.

7.0.1
,
Section

7.3
, and
Eqs.

7.5.1
,
7.5.2
, and
7.5.3
, we see that
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:Cn:SSA}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\sim C(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r},

(7.6.1)

where the constant
C(n)
is defined as follows:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:Cn:C}C(n):=\sum_{n\notin I}\left(\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=1}^{t_{I}-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))(n+1-\ell_{i}(I))}-1\right)^{-1}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t_{I}-1}}\operatorname{E}_{C_{n};I}\left(\mathbf{s}\right)\right),

(7.6.2)

where the function
\operatorname{E}_{C_{n};I}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t_{I}-1}\to\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}_{C_{n};I}\left(\mathbf{s}\right)

\displaystyle:=q^{\sum\limits_{1\leqslant i<i^{\prime}\leqslant t_{I}}(\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I))(\ell_{i^{\prime}}(I)-\ell_{i^{\prime}-1}(I))\overline{s_{i}+\cdots+s_{i^{\prime}-1}}}

(7.6.3)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t_{I}-1}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i}(I))(n+1-\ell_{i}(I))s_{i}}.

As a consequence, we see that
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial so that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Asymp:Cn:SV}\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}}}{q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}}-1}C(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2}r}.

(7.6.4)

By
Eqs.

7.6.1
,
7.6.2
,
7.6.3
, and
7.6.4
,
we have proved
Theorem

7.1
.
Moreover, by
Eq.

A.2.2
, we have the following explicit formulas for the first factor of
C(n)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{[2n]!!(q)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}[\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)]!(q)},

\displaystyle q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n};I}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{n^{2}}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}q^{\binom{\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)}{2}}}.

See
Eqs.

A.1.2
and
A.2.1
for the definitions of the symbols
[\>\cdot\>]!
and
[2\>\cdot\>]!!
.

§ 8  Simplicial volume in buildings of
B_{n}
type

In this section, we will prove the
B_{n}
part of
Theorems

1.1
and
1.4
.
More precisely, we will prove the following stronger theorem.

8.1
.

Let
{B}
be a Bruhat-Tits building of split classical type
B_{n}
over a local field
K
with residue cardinality
q
.
Then the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it can be defined by super
q
-exponential polynomials whose leading terms are of the form:
2
2

2

The leading terms may give an impression that these
q
-functions can be defined by primary
q
-exponential polynomials when
n=3
. However, we will see this is false in
Section

8.1
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}(n)\cdot q^{\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim C(n)\cdot q^{\pi(n)r},

where
\pi(n)=\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}
when
n\geqslant 4
and
\pi(3)=5
.
The leading coefficients
\tilde{C}(3)
and
C(3)
are primary
q
-numbers, not just parity
q
-functions.
Moreover, the four
q
-functions
\operatorname{SV}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
,
\operatorname{SV}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
,
\operatorname{SSA}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
, and
\operatorname{SSA}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
can be defined by primary
q
-exponential polynomials whose leading terms are of the form:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(2r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}_{0}(n)\cdot q^{2\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(2r\right)

\displaystyle\sim C_{0}(n)\cdot q^{2\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(2r+1\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}_{1}(n)\cdot q^{2\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(2r+1\right)

\displaystyle\sim C_{1}(n)\cdot q^{2\pi(n)r}.

We will obtain explicit formulas for the parity functions
\tilde{C}(n)
and
C(n)
, and the constants
\tilde{C}_{0}(n)
,
C_{0}(n)
,
\tilde{C}_{1}(n)
, and
C_{1}(n)
.

But before proving
Theorem

8.1
, we will first analyze the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
, where
\dagger
denotes “being special”. We will prove the following.

8.2
.

Let
{B}
be a Bruhat-Tits building of split classical type
B_{n}
over a local field
K
with residue cardinality
q
.
Then the special simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the special simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it can be defined by super
q
-exponential polynomials whose leading terms are of the form:
Footnote

2

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}_{{}_{\dagger}}(n)\cdot q^{\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim C_{{}_{\dagger}}(n)\cdot q^{\pi(n)r},

where
\pi(n)=\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}
when
n\geqslant 4
and
\pi(3)=5
.
The leading coefficients
\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(3)
and
C_{\dagger}(3)
are primary
q
-numbers, not just parity
q
-functions.
Moreover, the four
q
-functions
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
,
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
,
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
, and
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
can be defined by primary
q
-exponential polynomials whose leading terms are of the form:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(2r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}_{{\dagger}0}(n)\cdot q^{2\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(2r\right)

\displaystyle\sim C_{{\dagger}0}(n)\cdot q^{2\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(2r+1\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}_{{\dagger}1}(n)\cdot q^{2\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(2r+1\right)

\displaystyle\sim C_{{\dagger}1}(n)\cdot q^{2\pi(n)r}.

We will also give explicit formulas for the parity functions
\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)
and
C_{\dagger}(n)
, and the constants
\tilde{C}_{{\dagger}0}(n)
,
C_{{\dagger}0}(n)
,
\tilde{C}_{{\dagger}1}(n)
, and
C_{{\dagger}1}(n)
.
The proof of
Theorem

8.2
turns out to play an essential role in the study of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
.

This section is structured as follows.
In
Section

8.1
, we will compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
for each type
I\subseteq\Delta
. This allows use to find the dominant ones of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
, which will be done in
Section

8.2
.
Then in
Section

8.3
, we will obtain the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
.
In
Section

8.4
, we will analyze
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r+1\right)
. Combine
Sections

8.3
and
8.4
, we finish proving
Theorem

8.2
.
After that, in
Section

8.5
, we will estimate the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
using the auxiliary functions
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}
and
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}
.
Note that
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
is between
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}
.
Therefore, we can combine
Section

8.1
and
Section

8.5
to estimate the asymptotic growth of each
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and find the dominant ones, which will be done in
Section

8.6
.
Once we found the dominant types, we can proceed to compute the asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.
This will be done in three steps:
in
Section

8.7
, we will compute the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
; in
Section

8.8
, we will deduce the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
from that of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
; finally in
Section

8.9
, the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
will be deduced from them.
Then in
Section

8.10
, we will obtain the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
.
In
Section

8.11
, we will analyze
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(2r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(2r+1\right)
. Combine
Sections

8.10
and
8.11
, we finish proving
Theorem

8.1
.

Throughout this section, we will heavily use the various index sets
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}
, and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}
.
We refer to
Figs.

4.5.1
and
4.5.2
for the structure of them.

8.1
Asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

\ell_{1}>1
and
(ii)

\ell_{1}=1
.

(i)
.

If
\ell_{1}>1
, then by
(vii)
,
4.5.2
, and
4.5.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
2c_{1}+\cdots+2c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\emptyset\sqcup\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
.
Then by
(iv)
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:AsymptoticOfSdaggerI}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})r}.

(8.1.1)

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.

(ii)
.

If
\ell_{1}=1
, then by
(vii)
,
4.5.2
, and
4.5.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+2c_{2}+\cdots+2c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\left\{1\right\}\sqcup\left\{2,\cdots,t\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t\right\}
and
\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
.

Depending on
n
and
\ell_{t}
, there are two possibilities:
2(2n-1)>\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
and
2(2n-1)<\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
.
Note that
2(2n-1)=\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
is impossible since the left-hand side has remainder
2
modulo
4
while the right-hand side is either odd or a multiple of
4
.

If
2(2n-1)>\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
, then by
(iv)
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:AsymptoticOfSdaggerI:l}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t}\left(q^{2(2n-1)-\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{(2n-1)r}.

(8.1.2)

In particular, it has order
2n-1
and degree
0
.

If
2(2n-1)<\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
, then by
(iv)
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(q^{\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})-2(2n-1)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}

(8.1.3)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\left(1+q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})-(2n-1)}\right)+\left(1-q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})-(2n-1)}\right)(-1)^{r}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})r}.

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.

8.2
Dominant types for
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, we are able to figure out for which type
I
,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
is dominant.

When
n=3
, we have
\ell_{t}(I)\leqslant 3
for all
I
.
Therefore,
2(2n-1)>\ell_{t}(I)(2n-\ell_{t}(I))
.
Hence,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
is dominant exactly when
1\notin I
.
Note that, such a type
I
must be one of the following:
\left\{2,3\right\}
,
\left\{2\right\}
,
\left\{3\right\}
, and
\emptyset
.
Using
Eq.

8.1.2
, we can deduce the asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
as follows.

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(\left\{2,3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{(2n-1)r}=q^{5r},

(8.2.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(q^{2(2n-1)-\ell_{2}(2n-\ell_{2})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{(2n-1)r}

(8.2.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left(q-1\right)}\cdot q^{5r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(\left\{3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(q^{2(2n-1)-\ell_{2}(2n-\ell_{2})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{(2n-1)r}

(8.2.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left(q^{2}-1\right)}\cdot q^{5r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\prod_{i=2}^{3}\left(q^{2(2n-1)-\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{(2n-1)r}

(8.2.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left(q-1\right)\left(q^{2}-1\right)}\cdot q^{5r}.

If
n\geqslant 4
, then we have
2(2n-1)<\ell_{t}(I)(2n-\ell_{t}(I))
when
\ell_{t}(I)\geqslant 4
.
On the other hand, since
\ell_{t}(I)\leqslant n
, we have

\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t}(I)(2n-\ell_{t}(I))\leqslant n^{2}.

The equality achieves when
\ell_{t}(I)=n
.
Hence,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
is dominant exactly when
n\notin I
.
In that case, its asymptotic growth is given by
Eq.

8.1.3
.

8.3
Asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

We are now able to obtain the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
.
By
Eq.

3.5.2
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SSA=SumSI:BnSp}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\sum_{I\text{ is dominant}}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

(8.3.1)

Then by the discussion in
Section

8.2
, we see that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:Asymp:SSAdagger}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)\sim\begin{cases}C_{\dagger}(3)\cdot q^{5r}&if $n=3$,\\
C_{\dagger}(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r}&if $n\geqslant 4$.\end{cases}

(8.3.2)

When
n=3
, by
Eqs.

8.2.1
,
8.2.2
,
8.2.3
, and
8.2.4
, the constant
C_{\dagger}(3)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\dagger}(3)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{2,3\right\}}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{2,3\right\}}\right)}}+\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{2\right\}}\left(q\right)}{\left(q-1\right)q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{2\right\}}\right)}}+\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{3\right\}}\left(q\right)}{\left(q^{2}-1\right)q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{3\right\}}\right)}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\emptyset}\left(q\right)}{\left(q-1\right)\left(q^{2}-1\right)q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\emptyset}\right)}}.

Moreover, by
Eq.

A.2.2
, we have

\displaystyle C_{\dagger}(3)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)}{\left(q-1\right)q^{5}}+\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)}{\left(q^{2}-1\right)\left(q-1\right)^{2}q^{7}}+\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)}{\left(q-1\right)^{2}\left(q^{2}-1\right)q^{8}}

(8.3.3)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)\left(q^{2}-1\right)}{\left(q-1\right)^{4}\left(q^{2}-1\right)q^{9}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{2}+q+1\right)\left(q^{2}-q+1\right)(q+1)}{(q-1)^{2}q^{9}}\left(q^{6}-q^{5}+q^{4}+q^{3}+q^{2}+1\right).

As a consequence, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:B3:Asymp:SVdagger}\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{5}}{q^{5}-1}C_{\dagger}(3)\cdot q^{5r}.

(8.3.4)

When
n\geqslant 4
, by
Eqs.

8.1.1
and
8.1.3
,
C_{\dagger}(n)
is a parity
q
-function defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\dagger}(n)(\text{even})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1}{q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1}

(8.3.5)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{1\in I,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle C_{\dagger}(n)(\text{odd})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}}{q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1}.

(8.3.6)

As a consequence, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:Asymp:SVdagger}\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(z\right)\sim\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r},

(8.3.7)

where the parity
q
-function
\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)(\text{even})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{q^{n^{2}-(2n-1)}+q^{n^{2}}}{\left(q^{n^{2}}-1\right)\left(q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1\right)}

(8.3.8)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{1\in I,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{q^{n^{2}}}{q^{n^{2}}-1},

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)(\text{odd})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{q^{\tfrac{3n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}+q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}}}{\left(q^{n^{2}}-1\right)\left(q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1\right)}

(8.3.9)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{1\in I,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}}}{q^{n^{2}}-1}.

By
Eqs.

8.3.2
,
8.3.3
,
8.3.4
,
8.3.5
,
8.3.6
,
8.3.7
,
8.3.8
, and
8.3.9
, we have proved the asymptotic relations in
Theorem

8.2
, where

\displaystyle C_{{\dagger}0}(n)

\displaystyle=C_{\dagger}(n)(\text{even}),

\displaystyle C_{{\dagger}1}(n)

\displaystyle=C_{\dagger}(n)(\text{odd})\cdot q^{\pi(n)},

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{{\dagger}0}(n)

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)(\text{even}),

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{{\dagger}1}(n)

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)(\text{odd})\cdot q^{\pi(n)}.

Moreover, by
Eq.

A.2.2
, we have the following explicit formulas:

\displaystyle\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{[2n]!!(q)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}[\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)]!(q)},

\displaystyle q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{n^{2}}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}q^{\binom{\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)}{2}}}.

See
Eqs.

A.1.2
and
A.2.1
for the definitions of the symbols
[\>\cdot\>]!
and
[2\>\cdot\>]!!
.

8.4
Analysis of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r+1\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to show that
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
can be defined by primary
q
-exponential polynomials.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

\ell_{1}>1
and
(ii)

\ell_{1}=1
.

(i)
.

If
\ell_{1}>1
, then by
(vii)
,
4.5.2
, and
4.5.3
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}2c_{1}+\cdots+2c_{t}=2r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}}=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r+1\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}2c_{1}+\cdots+2c_{t}=2r+1}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}}=0.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r\right)
, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.

(ii)
.

If
\ell_{1}=1
, then by
(vii)
,
4.5.2
, and
4.5.3
, we have (noticing the involved change of variables)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}c_{1}+c_{2}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{2(2n-1)c_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r+1\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}c_{1}+c_{2}+\cdots+c_{t}=r+1}}q^{(2n-1)(2c_{1}-1)+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to these summations, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=2(2n-1),

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(2\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers, the
q
-functions
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
can be defined by primary super
q
-exponential polynomials.

By
Eq.

8.3.1
, the
q
-functions
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
,
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
,
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
, and
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
are
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;1\right)
-combinations of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
. We thus finish proving
Theorem

8.2
.

8.5
Asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to estimate the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
up to the leading coefficient.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

\ell_{1}>1
and
(ii)

\ell_{1}=1
.

(i)
.

If
\ell_{1}>1
, then by
Eqs.

4.5.3
and
4.5.7
, we have

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to above summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
.
Then we have

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})r}.

Since
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
, we see that
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}
has order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.

(ii)
.

If
\ell_{1}=1
, then by
Eqs.

4.5.3
,
4.5.7
, and
4.5.8
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{(2n-1)c_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-1)}\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to above summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=2n-1,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<i\leqslant t)}

Depending on
n
and
\ell_{t}
, there are two possibilities:
2n-1>\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
and
2n-1<\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
.
If
2n-1>\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{1\right\}
,
\mu_{\max}=2n-1
, and

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t}\left(q^{(2n-1)-\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{(2n-1)r}.

If
2n-1<\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
, then we have

\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
,
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
, and

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})-(2n-1)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})r}.

Since
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
, and
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=q^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-1)}\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
, we see that
\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1})(I)}
has order
\max\left\{2n-1,\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})\right\}
and degree
0
.

8.6
Dominant types for
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

We are going to estimate the asymptotic growth of each
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and figure out the
dominant
types, namely the types for which
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
is dominant.

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
If
\ell_{1}>1
,
then
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)
is between
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)
by
Fig.

4.5.1
.
Therefore,

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\gg\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\gg\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

From
Sections

8.1
and
(i)
, we see that both
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}
have order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.
Note that

\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})\leqslant\tfrac{n^{2}}{2},

where the equality holds exactly when
\ell_{t}=n
.
We thus see that
I
is dominant among those satisfying
\ell_{1}(I)>1
if and only if
