n\notin I
.
In that case,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
has order
\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}
and degree
0
.

If
\ell_{1}=1
,
then
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)
is between
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)
by
Fig.

4.5.2
.
Therefore,

\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1})(I)}\left(r\right)\gg\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\gg\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Depending on
n
and
\ell_{t}
, there are two there are two possibilities:
2n-1>\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
and
2n-1<\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
.
From
Sections

8.1
and
(ii)
, we see that
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}
, and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}
have the same order and degree.
Hence,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
has the same order and degree with them.
If
n=3
, then we must have
\ell_{t}\leqslant 3
and hence
2n-1=5>\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
.
Then
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
has order
5
and degree
0
.
In this case, all types
I
satisfying
\ell_{1}(I)=1
are dominant.
If
n\geqslant 4
, then we may have
2n-1<\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})
.
Note that

\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-\ell_{t})\leqslant\tfrac{n^{2}}{2},

where the equality holds exactly when
\ell_{t}=n
.
We thus see that
I
is dominant if and only if
n\notin I
.
In that case,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
has order
\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}
and degree
0
.

To summarize, when
n=3
, a type
I
is dominant if and only if
1\notin I
; when
n\geqslant 4
, a type
I
is dominant if and only if
n\notin I
.

8.7
Asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to compute the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
when
I
is dominant.

To do this, we pick an arbitrary
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)
(or
x\in(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1})(I)
if
\ell_{1}=1
) and investigate the difference between
2\rho(x)
and the sum of
\lceil a(x)\rceil
for
a(x)>0
.
To better describe these sums, we follow
Conventions

7.5.1
,
7.5.2
, and
7.5.3
.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

\ell_{1}>1
and
(ii)

\ell_{1}=1
.

(i)
.

We begin with the
\ell_{1}>1
case.
Suppose
x=o+c_{1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell_{1}}+\cdots+c_{t}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell_{t}}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)
. By
Eq.

4.5.1
, we have

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}-\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}+\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle\quad+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})}+\cdots+c_{t},

\displaystyle\chi_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t}\right).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant j\leqslant n)}

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n}\left(\lceil(\chi_{j}-\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)\rceil+\lceil(\chi_{j}+\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)\rceil\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lceil\chi_{j}(x)\rceil

\displaystyle=2\rho(x)+\sum_{1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n}\overline{c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t}}.

From above analysis, we can define the parity function
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}
as follows:

\displaystyle e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1\leqslant i<i^{\prime}\leqslant t+1}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})(\ell_{i^{\prime}}-\ell_{i^{\prime}-1})\overline{c_{i}+\cdots+c_{i^{\prime}-1}}

(8.7.1)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})\overline{c_{i}+\cdots+c_{t}}.

Then we have

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil=2\rho(x)+e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.5
to the following summation.

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}+e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})}.

Note that the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Now, let
I
be a dominant type, namely
n\notin I
.
Then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
,
\ell_{t}=n
, and
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}
.
Therefore,

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:AsymptoticOfSX0I:l1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)+\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r},

(8.7.2)

where the constant
C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}
and the function
\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}\to\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})^{2}}-1\right)^{-1},

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})^{2}s_{i}}.

(8.7.3)

(ii)
.

Now, we turn to
\ell_{1}=1
case.
Let
\square
be either
0
or
1
.
Suppose

x=o+(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)\cdot\omega_{1}+c_{2}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell_{2}}+\cdots+c_{t}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell_{t}}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right),

By
Eq.

4.5.1
, we have

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}-\chi_{j})(x)

\displaystyle=(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{2}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j)-1}\right),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}-\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}+\chi_{j})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle=(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{2}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j)-1}\right)+c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t},

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}+\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right)+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})}+\cdots+c_{t},

\displaystyle\chi_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{2}+\cdots+c_{t}\right),

\displaystyle\chi_{j}(x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t}\right).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j\leqslant n)}

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil

\displaystyle=2\rho(x)+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\overline{c_{2}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j)-1}-\square}+\sum_{2\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n}\overline{c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{c_{2}+\cdots+c_{t}-\square}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t}}.

From above analysis, we can define the parity function
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}
(
\square=0,1
) as follows:

\displaystyle e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{t}(\ell_{i+1}-\ell_{i})\overline{c_{2}+\cdots+c_{i}-\square}

(8.7.4)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{2\leqslant i<i^{\prime}\leqslant t+1}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})(\ell_{i^{\prime}}-\ell_{i^{\prime}-1})\overline{c_{i}+\cdots+c_{i^{\prime}-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{c_{2}+\cdots+c_{t}-\square}+\sum_{i=2}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})\overline{c_{i}+\cdots+c_{t}}.

Then we have

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil=2\rho(x)+e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.5
to the following summation (
\square=0,1
).

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{(2n-1)(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i})c_{i}+e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})}.

Note that the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=2n-1,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<i\leqslant t)}

Now, let
I
be a dominant type.
Depending on
n
, there are two cases:

(ii-a)

n=3
and
(ii-b)

n\geqslant 4
.

(ii-a)
.

When
n=3
, this means
1\notin I
.
Then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{1\right\}
and
\mu_{\max}=5
.
Therefore, for
\square=0,1
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)+\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{5r},

where the constant
C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}
and the function
\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}\to\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}q^{-\tfrac{5}{2}\cdot\square}\prod_{i=2}^{t}\left(q^{\left(10-\ell_{i}(6-\ell_{i})\right)}-1\right)^{-1},

(8.7.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t}\left(5-\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(6-\ell_{i})\right)s_{i}}.

(8.7.6)

From the definition
Eq.

8.7.4
of
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}
, we have

\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(0,s_{2},\cdots,s_{t})=\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(1,s_{2},\cdots,s_{t}).

Therefore, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:B3:AsymptoticOfSXI:e1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)\cdot q^{5r},

(8.7.7)

(ii-b)
.

When
n\geqslant 4
,
I
is dominant means
n\notin I
.
Then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
,
\ell_{t}=n
, and
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}
.
Therefore, for
\square=0,1
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:AsymptoticOfSXI:e1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)+\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r},

(8.7.8)

where the constant
C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}
and the function
\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}\to\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}q^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-1)\cdot\square}\left(q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})^{2}}-1\right)^{-1},

(8.7.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})+\left(\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)\right)s_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t-1}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})^{2}s_{i}}.

(8.7.10)

8.8
Asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to analyze
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.

Suppose
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right)
, where
I\cap J=\emptyset
.
Since
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}=\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}
, by
Lemma

4.5.5
, we can write
x
as

x_{0}-\sum\limits_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}
,
where
x_{0}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I,r+\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)\right)
.
Then we have

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil=2\rho(x_{0})+\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-\sum\limits_{j\in J}a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j})\right\rceil.

Note that the last summation gives an integral constant.
Then we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:SXJIfromSVdaggerI}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=q^{\sum\limits_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-\sum\limits_{j\in J}a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j})\right\rceil}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r+\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)\right).

(8.8.1)

Now, we assume
I
is dominant.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

n=3
and
(ii)

n\geqslant 4
.

(i)
.

When
n=3
, this means
\ell_{1}=1
.
Then the following
J
appears in
Fig.

4.5.2
:
\left\{1\right\},\left\{1,2\right\},\left\{2,3\right\}
.
In those cases, by
Eq.

4.5.1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{1\right\}\right\rvert-\delta(\left\{1\right\})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1})\right\rceil

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{1,2\right\}\right\rvert-\delta(\left\{1,2\right\})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{2})\right\rceil

\displaystyle=-4,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{2,3\right\}\right\rvert-\delta(\left\{2,3\right\})

\displaystyle=2,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{3})\right\rceil

\displaystyle=-6.

Then by
Eqs.

8.1.2
and
8.8.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t}\left(q^{10-\ell_{i}(6-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{5r},

(8.8.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1,2\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim q\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{t}\left(q^{10-\ell_{i}(6-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{5r},

(8.8.3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{2,3\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim q^{4}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{t}\left(q^{10-\ell_{i}(6-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{5r}.

(8.8.4)

(ii)
.

Now, we assume
n\geqslant 4
.
Then
I
is dominant means
\ell_{t}=n
.
Depending on
\ell_{1}
, there are two cases:

(ii-a)

\ell_{1}>1
and
(ii-b)

\ell_{1}=1
.

(ii-a)
.

If
\ell_{1}>1
, then the following
J
appears in
Fig.

4.5.1
:
\left\{2,3\right\},\cdots,\left\{n-1,n\right\}
.
In those cases, we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=2
and by
Eq.

4.5.1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j+1})\right\rceil

\displaystyle=-j(2n-1-j).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j<n)}

(8.8.5)

Then by
Eqs.

8.1.1
and
8.8.1
, we have (
1<j<n
)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:AsymptoticOfSXJI:l1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{j,j+1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim q^{n^{2}-j(2n-1-j)}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r}.

(8.8.6)

(ii-b)
.

If
\ell_{1}=1
, then the following
J
appears in
Fig.

4.5.2
:
\left\{1\right\}
,
\left\{1,2\right\}
,
\cdots
,
\left\{n-1,n\right\}
.
When
J=\left\{1\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert\left\{1\right\}\right\rvert-\delta(\left\{1\right\})=0
and

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1})\right\rceil=0.

Then by
Eqs.

8.1.3
and
8.8.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}

(8.8.7)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\left(1+q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}\right)+\left(1-q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}\right)(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r}.

When
J=\left\{1,2\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert\left\{1,2\right\}\right\rvert-\delta(\left\{1,2\right\})=1
and

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{2})\right\rceil=-(2n-2).

Then by
Eqs.

8.1.3
and
8.8.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1,2\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-2)}\cdot\left(q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}

(8.8.8)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\left(1+q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}\right)+\left(1-q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}\right)(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r}.

When
J=\left\{j,j+1\right\}
(
1<j<n
), we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=2
and
Eq.

8.8.5
.
Then by
Eqs.

8.1.3
and
8.8.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{j,j+1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim q^{n^{2}-j(2n-1-j)}\cdot\left(q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}

(8.8.9)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\left(1+q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}\right)+\left(1-q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}\right)(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r}.

8.9
Asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

We are now able to compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
when
I
is dominant.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

n=3
and
(ii)

n\geqslant 4
.

(i)
.

When
n=3
, the dominant types are
\left\{2,3\right\}
,
\left\{2\right\}
,
\left\{3\right\}
, and
\emptyset
.
By
Fig.

4.5.2
, we have (where zero summations are omitted)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\left\{2,3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(\left\{2,3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(\left\{2,3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(\left\{2,3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\left\{3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(\left\{3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(\left\{3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(\left\{3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1,2\right\}}\left(\left\{3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1,2\right\}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{2,3\right\}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right).

Therefore, by
Eqs.

8.7.7
,
8.8.2
,
8.8.3
, and
8.8.4
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\left\{2,3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(1+1-1\right)q^{5r}=q^{5r},

(8.9.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{(q^{2}+1)+(q+1)-1}{q-1}q^{5r}=\frac{q^{2}+q+1}{q-1}q^{5r},

(8.9.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\left\{3\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{(q^{4}+1)+(q+1)-1-q}{q^{2}-1}q^{5r}=\frac{q^{4}+1}{q^{2}-1}q^{5r},

(8.9.3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{(2q^{4}+q^{2}+1)+(q^{2}+2q+1)-1-q-q^{4}}{(q-1)(q^{2}-1)}q^{5r}

(8.9.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{4}+2q^{2}+q+1}{(q-1)(q^{2}-1)}q^{5r}.

(ii)
.

Now, we assume
n\geqslant 4
. Then
I
is dominant exactly when
n\notin I
.
Depending on
\ell_{1}
, there are two cases:

(ii-a)

\ell_{1}>1
and
(ii-b)

\ell_{1}=1
.

(ii-a)
.

If
\ell_{1}>1
, then by
Fig.

4.5.1
, we have (including the zero summations)

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)-\sum_{j=2}^{n-1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{j,j+1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Therefore, by
Eqs.

8.7.2
,
8.8.6
, and
8.7.3
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:AsymptoticOfSI:l1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\frac{1}{2}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(C_{I,0}+C_{I,1}(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r},

(8.9.5)

where the constants
C_{I,0}
and
C_{I,1}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{I,0}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})^{2}s_{i}}-\sum_{\crampedsubstack{1<j<n{\\
}\left\{j,j+1\right\}\cap I=\emptyset}}q^{n^{2}-j(2n-1-j)},

(8.9.6)

\displaystyle C_{I,1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})^{2}s_{i}}-\sum_{\crampedsubstack{1<j<n{\\
}\left\{j,j+1\right\}\cap I=\emptyset}}q^{n^{2}-j(2n-1-j)}.

(8.9.7)

Note that the multivariable parity function
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}
is defined in
Eq.

8.7.1
.

(ii-b)
.

If
\ell_{1}=1
, then by
Fig.

4.5.2
, we have (including the zero summations)

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{j,j+1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Therefore, by
Eqs.

8.7.8
,
8.7.9
,
8.7.10
,
8.8.7
,
8.8.8
, and
8.8.9
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:AsymptoticOfSI:e1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\frac{1}{2}\left(q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\left(C_{I,0}+C_{I,1}(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r},

(8.9.8)

where the constants
C_{I,0}
and
C_{I,1}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{I,0}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\square=0,1}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-1)\cdot\square+\left(\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)\right)s_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t-1}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})^{2}s_{i}}

(8.9.9)

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(1+\delta_{I}(2)q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-2)}+\sum_{\crampedsubstack{1<j<n{\\
}\left\{j,j+1\right\}\cap I=\emptyset}}q^{n^{2}-j(2n-1-j)}\right)\left(1+q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}\right),

\displaystyle C_{I,1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\square=0,1}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-1)\cdot\square+\left(\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)\right)s_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t-1}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})^{2}s_{i}}

(8.9.10)

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(1+\delta_{I}(2)q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-2)}+\sum_{\crampedsubstack{1<j<n{\\
}\left\{j,j+1\right\}\cap I=\emptyset}}q^{n^{2}-j(2n-1-j)}\right)\left(1-q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}-(2n-1)}\right),

where
\delta_{I}(i)=0
if
1\in I
and
1
if not.
Note that the multivariable parity functions
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square}\left(I\right)}
(
\square=0,1
) are defined in
Eq.

8.7.4
.

8.10
Asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

We are now able to obtain the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
.
By
Eq.

3.4.6
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SSA=SumSI:Bn}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\sum_{I\text{ is dominant}}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

(8.10.1)

What remains is to plug in the asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

n=3
and
(ii)

n\geqslant 4
.

(i)
.

When
n=3
, the dominant types are
\left\{2,3\right\}
,
\left\{2\right\}
,
\left\{3\right\}
, and
\emptyset
.
By
Eqs.

8.9.1
,
8.9.2
,
8.9.3
,
8.9.4
, and
A.2.2
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:B3:Asymp:SSA}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\sim C(3)\cdot q^{5r},

(8.10.2)

where the constant
C(3)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle C(3)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{2,3\right\}}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{2,3\right\}}\right)}}+\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{2\right\}}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{2\right\}}\right)}}\frac{q^{2}+q+1}{q-1}+\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{3\right\}}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\left\{3\right\}}\right)}}\frac{q^{4}+1}{q^{2}-1}

(8.10.3)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\emptyset}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{3};\emptyset}\right)}}\frac{q^{4}+2q^{2}+q+1}{(q-1)(q^{2}-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)}{\left(q-1\right)q^{5}}+\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)\left(q^{2}+q+1\right)}{\left(q^{2}-1\right)\left(q-1\right)^{2}q^{7}}+\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)\left(q^{4}+1\right)}{\left(q-1\right)^{2}\left(q^{2}-1\right)q^{8}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)\left(q^{2}-1\right)\left(q^{4}+2q^{2}+q+1\right)}{\left(q-1\right)^{4}\left(q^{2}-1\right)q^{9}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{2}+q+1\right)\left(q^{2}-q+1\right)(q+1)}{(q-1)^{2}q^{9}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\left(q^{8}+q^{7}+3q^{6}+q^{5}+5q^{4}+3q^{3}+4q^{2}+q+1\right).

As a consequence, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:B3:Asymp:SV}\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{5}}{q^{5}-1}C(3)\cdot q^{5r}.

(8.10.4)

(ii)
.

Now, we assume
n\geqslant 4
. Then
I
is dominant exactly when
n\notin I
.
By
Eqs.

8.9.5
and
8.9.8
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:Asymp:SSA}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\sim C(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r},

(8.10.5)

where the parity
q
-function
C(n)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle C(n)(r)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{\tfrac{1}{2}\left(C_{I,0}+C_{I,1}(-1)^{r}\right)}{q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1}

(8.10.6)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{1\in I,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(C_{I,0}+C_{I,1}(-1)^{r}\right).

As a consequence, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Bn:Asymp:SV}\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}\left(z\right)\sim\tilde{C}(n)q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}r},

(8.10.7)

where the parity
q
-function
\tilde{C}(n)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{C}(n)(r)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}

(8.10.8)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\cdot\frac{\tfrac{1}{2}\left((1+q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}})C_{I,0}+(1-q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}})C_{I,1}(-1)^{r}\right)}{\left(q^{n^{2}}-1\right)\left(q^{n^{2}-2(2n-1)}-1\right)}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{1\in I,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))^{2}}-1\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\frac{\tfrac{1}{2}\left((1+q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}})C_{I,0}+(1-q^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}})C_{I,1}(-1)^{r}\right)}{q^{n^{2}}-1}.

.

Note that the constants
C_{I,\square}
(
\square=0,1
) depends on
I
.
When
1\in I
and
n\notin I
, they are defined in
Eqs.

8.9.6
and
8.9.7
.
When
1,n\notin I
, they are defined in
Eqs.

8.9.9
and
8.9.10
.

By
Eqs.

8.10.2
,
8.10.3
,
8.10.4
,
8.10.5
,
8.10.6
,
8.10.7
, and
8.10.8

we have proved the asymptotic relations in
Theorem

8.1
, where

\displaystyle C_{0}(n)

\displaystyle=C(n)(\text{even}),

\displaystyle C_{1}(n)

\displaystyle=C(n)(\text{odd})\cdot q^{\pi(n)},

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{0}(n)

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}(n)(\text{even}),

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{1}(n)

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}(n)(\text{odd})\cdot q^{\pi(n)}.

One can see they are primary
q
-numbers by either
Section

8.11
or direct verification using
Eqs.

8.10.6
and
8.10.8
.
Moreover, by
Eq.

A.2.2
, we have the following explicit formulas:

\displaystyle\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{[2n]!!(q)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}[\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)]!(q)},

\displaystyle q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{B_{n};I}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{n^{2}}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}q^{\binom{\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)}{2}}}.

(8.10.9)

See
Eqs.

A.1.2
and
A.2.1
for the definitions of the symbols
[\>\cdot\>]!
and
[2\>\cdot\>]!!
.

8.11
Analysis of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(2r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(2r+1\right)

Now, let
I
be a general type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to show that
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(2r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(2r+1\right)
can be defined by primary
q
-exponential polynomials.

Suppose
I\cap J=\emptyset
.
By
Eq.

8.8.1
, we have (
\square=0,1
)

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(2r+\square\right)=q^{\sum\limits_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-\sum\limits_{j\in J}a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j})\right\rceil}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2r+\square+\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)\right).

We have seen that the
q
-functions
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
can be defined by primary super
q
-exponential polynomials in
Section

8.4
.
The exponent
\sum\limits_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-\sum\limits_{j\in J}a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j})\right\rceil
is an integer.
Therefore,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>+\square\right)
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.

Note that the proof of
Lemma

4.5.1
implies

\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I,r\right)=\bigcup_{J\neq\left\{1\right\},\left\{1,2\right\},\cdots,\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right).

Hence, the
q
-function
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>+\square\right)
(
\square=0,1
) is clearly a
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;1\right)
-combination of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>+\square\right)
.
On the other hand, by
Eq.

8.10.1
, the
q
-functions
\operatorname{SV}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
,
\operatorname{SV}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
,
\operatorname{SSA}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
, and
\operatorname{SSA}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
are
\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;1\right)
-combinations of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(2\>\cdot\>+1\right)
.
We thus finish proving
Theorem

8.1
.

§ 9  Simplicial volume in buildings of
D_{n}
type

In this section, we will prove the
D_{n}
part of
Theorems

1.1
and
1.4
.
More precisely, we will prove the following stronger theorem.

9.1
.

Let
{B}
be a Bruhat-Tits building of split classical type
D_{n}
over a local field
K
with residue cardinality
q
.
Then the simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it can be defined by primary super
q
-exponential polynomials whose leading terms are of the form:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}(n)\cdot\binom{r}{\varepsilon(n)}q^{\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim C(n)\cdot\binom{r}{\varepsilon(n)}q^{\pi(n)r},

where
\varepsilon(n)=1
and
\pi(n)=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
when
n\geqslant 5
, while
\varepsilon(4)=2
and
\pi(4)=6
.
The leading coefficients
\tilde{C}(n)
and
C(n)
are primary
q
-numbers, not just parity
q
-functions.

We will obtain explicit formulas for the parity functions
\tilde{C}(n)
and
C(n)
.

But before proving
Theorem

9.1
, we will first analyze the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
, where
\dagger
denotes “being special”. We will prove the following.

9.2
.

Let
{B}
be a Bruhat-Tits building of split classical type
D_{n}
over a local field
K
with residue cardinality
q
.
Then the special simplicial volume
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and the special simplicial surface area
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
in it can be defined by primary super
q
-exponential polynomials whose leading terms are of the form:

\displaystyle\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\tilde{C}_{{}_{\dagger}}(n)\cdot\binom{r}{\varepsilon(n)}q^{\pi(n)r},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim C_{{}_{\dagger}}(n)\cdot\binom{r}{\varepsilon(n)}q^{\pi(n)r},

where
\varepsilon(n)=1
and
\pi(n)=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
when
n\geqslant 5
, while
\varepsilon(4)=2
and
\pi(4)=6
.
The leading coefficients
\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)
and
C_{\dagger}(n)
are primary
q
-numbers, not just parity
q
-functions.

We will also give explicit formulas for the constants
\tilde{C}_{\dagger}(n)
and
C_{\dagger}(n)
.
The proof of
Theorem

9.2
will play an essential role in the study of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
.

This section is structured as follows.
In
Section

9.1
, we will compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
for each type
I\subseteq\Delta
. This allows use to find the dominant ones of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
, which will be done in
Section

9.2
.
Then in
Section

9.3
, we will obtain the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
.
After that, in
Section

9.4
, we will estimate the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
(
\square,\heartsuit
being
0
or
1
) using the auxiliary function
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
.
Note that
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
is between
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}
.
Therefore, we can combine
Section

9.1
and
Section

9.4
to estimate the asymptotic growth of each
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and find the dominant ones, which will be done in
Section

9.5
.
Once we found the dominant types, we can proceed to compute the asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.
This will be done in three steps:
in
Section

9.6
, we will compute the asymptotic growth of each
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
; in
Section

9.7
, we will deduce the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
from that of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
; then in
Section

9.8
, the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
will be deduced from them.
Finally, in
Section

9.9
, we will obtain the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)
.

Throughout this section, we will heavily use the various index sets
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}
, and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}
.
We refer to
Figs.

4.6.1
,
4.6.2
,
4.6.3
, and
4.6.4
for the structure of them.

9.1
Asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.
We will separate the discussion into the following six cases:

\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq I

\left\lvert\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I\right\rvert=1

\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset

1\in I

(i)

(ii)

(iii)

1\notin I

(iv)

(v)

(vi)

(i)
.

Suppose
1\in I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq I
.
By
(vii)
,
4.6.2
, and
4.6.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
2c_{1}+\cdots+2c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\emptyset\sqcup\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
.
Then by
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-1-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{r}\right)\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})r}.

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.

(ii)
.

Suppose
1\in I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I
is a singleton.
By
(vii)
,
4.6.2
, and
4.6.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
2c_{1}+\cdots+2c_{t-1}+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}c_{t}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\left\{t\right\}\sqcup\left\{1,\cdots,t-1\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t-1\right\}
,
\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t-1}(2n-1-\ell_{t-1})
, and
2\mu_{1\max}>\mu_{2\max}
.
Then by
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
0
.

(iii)
.

Suppose
1\in I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
By
(vii)
,
4.6.2
, and
4.6.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
2c_{1}+\cdots+2c_{t-2}+c_{t-1}+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}(c_{t-1}+c_{t})}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\left\{t-1,t\right\}\sqcup\left\{1,\cdots,t-2\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t-2)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t-2\right\}
,
\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t-2}(2n-1-\ell_{t-2})
, and
2\mu_{1\max}>\mu_{2\max}
.
Then by
(iv)
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:AsymptoticOfSdaggerI:l1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.1.1)

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
1
.

(iv)
.

Suppose
1\notin I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq I
.
By
(vii)
,
4.6.2
, and
4.6.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+2c_{2}+\cdots+2c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\left\{1\right\}\sqcup\left\{2,\cdots,t\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t\right\}
and
\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
.
On the other side
\mu_{1\max}=(2n-2)
.

Depending on
n
and
\ell_{t}
, there are three possibilities.

If
2\mu_{1\max}>\mu_{2\max}
, then by
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t}\left(q^{2(2n-2)-\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{(2n-2)r}.

In particular, it has order
2n-2
and degree
0
.

If
2\mu_{1\max}<\mu_{2\max}
, then by
(iv)
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(q^{\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})-2(2n-2)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-1-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\left(1+q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})-(2n-2)}\right)+\left(1-q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})-(2n-2)}\right)(-1)^{r}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})r}.

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.

If
2\mu_{1\max}=\mu_{2\max}
, then by
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\tfrac{1}{2}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-1-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{(2n-2)r}.

In particular, it has order
2n-2
and degree
1
.

(v)
.

Suppose
1\notin I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I
is a singleton.
By
(vii)
,
4.6.2
, and
4.6.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+2c_{2}+\cdots+2c_{t-1}+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}c_{t}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\left\{1,t\right\}\sqcup\left\{2,\cdots,t-1\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t-1\right\}
with
\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t-1}(2n-1-\ell_{t-1})
and that
t\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}
with
2\mu_{1\max}=n(n-1)>\mu_{2\max}
.

Depending on
n
, there are two possibilities.

If
n=4
, then
\mu_{1\max}=(2n-2)
and hence
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}=\left\{1,t\right\}
.
By
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{6r}.

In particular, it has order
6
and degree
1
.

If
n\geqslant 5
, then
\mu_{1\max}>(2n-2)
and hence
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}=\left\{t\right\}
.
By
(iv)
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1+q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}}{q^{n(n-1)-2(2n-2)}-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
0
.

(vi)
.

Suppose
1\notin I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
By
(vii)
,
4.6.2
, and
4.6.3
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+2c_{2}+\cdots+2c_{t-2}+c_{t-1}+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}(c_{t-1}+c_{t})}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.6.1
to this summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
, the partition
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}=\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1}\sqcup\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}=\left\{1,t-1,t\right\}\sqcup\left\{2,\cdots,t-2\right\}
, and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t-2)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{2\max}=\left\{t-2\right\}
with
\mu_{2\max}=\ell_{t-2}(2n-1-\ell_{t-2})
and that
\left\{t-1,t\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}
with
2\mu_{1\max}=n(n-1)>\mu_{2\max}
.

Depending on
n
, there are two possibilities.

If
n=4
, then
\mu_{1\max}=(2n-2)
and hence
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}=\left\{1,t-1,t\right\}
.
By
(iv)
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:D4:AsymptoticOfSdaggerI:e1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}.

(9.1.2)

In particular, it has order
6
and degree
2
.

If
n\geqslant 5
, then
\mu_{1\max}>(2n-2)
and hence
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{1\max}=\left\{t-1,t\right\}
.
By
(iv)
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:AsymptoticOfSdaggerI:e1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1+q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}}{q^{n(n-1)-2(2n-2)}-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.1.3)

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
1
.

Note that, in all cases,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
Then by
Eqs.

3.5.1
and
3.5.2
, we see that
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by primary super
q
-exponential polynomials.

9.2
Dominant types for
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, we are able to figure out for which type
I
,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
is dominant.
First, we summarize the asymptotic results in
Section

9.1
as follows.

\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq I

\left\lvert\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I\right\rvert=1

\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset

1\in I

\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t}),0\right)

\left(\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},0\right)

\left(\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},1\right)

1\notin I

\left(2n-2,0\right)

\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t}),0\right)

\left(2n-2,1\right)

\left(6,1\right)

\left(\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},0\right)

\left(6,2\right)

\left(\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},1\right)

In the table, the pair in each cell tells us the possible order and degree of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}
.

When
n=4
, we have
\ell_{t}(I)\leqslant 4
for all
I
.
Therefore,

(2n-2)\geqslant\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(I)(2n-1-\ell_{t}(I)).

Hence,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
is dominant exactly when
1\notin I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
Note that, such a type
I
must be either
\left\{2\right\}
or
\emptyset
.
By
Eq.

9.1.2
, the asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
are as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}

(9.2.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(q^{(4-\ell_{2})(3-\ell_{2})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}=\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}

(9.2.2)

If
n\geqslant 4
, then we have
(2n-2)
is no longer the highest order.
When
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq I
, we have
\ell_{t}(I)<n-1
and thus

\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(I)(2n-1-\ell_{t}(I))<\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

Therefore,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
is dominant exactly when
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
In that case, its asymptotic growth is given by
Eqs.

9.1.1
and
9.1.3
.

9.3
Asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)

We are now able to obtain the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)
.
By
Eq.

3.5.2
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SSA=SumSI:DnSp}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\sum_{I\text{ is dominant}}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

(9.3.1)

Then by the discussion in
Section

9.2
, we see that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:Asymp:SSAdagger}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)\sim\begin{cases}C_{\dagger}(4)\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}&if $n=4$,\\
C_{\dagger}(n)\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}&if $n\geqslant 5$.\end{cases}

(9.3.2)

When
n=4
, by
Eqs.

9.2.1
and
9.2.2
, the constant
C_{\dagger}(4)
is defined as follows:

C_{\dagger}(4)=\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{4};\left\{2\right\}}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{4};\left\{2\right\}}\right)}}+\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{4};\emptyset}\left(q\right)}{\left(q^{2}-1\right)q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{4};\emptyset}\right)}}.

Moreover, by
Eq.

A.3.2
, we have

\displaystyle C_{\dagger}(4)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)^{2}}{\left(q-1\right)^{3}q^{11}}+\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)^{2}}{\left(q-1\right)^{4}q^{12}}

(9.3.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{2}+q+1\right)\left(q^{2}-q+1\right)^{2}\left(q^{2}+1\right)^{2}(q+1)^{3}}{(q-1)q^{12}}.

As a consequence, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:D4:Asymp:SVdagger}\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{6}}{q^{6}-1}C_{\dagger}(4)\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}.

(9.3.4)

When
n\geqslant 5
, by
Eqs.

9.1.1
and
9.1.3
, the constant
C_{\dagger}(n)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\dagger}(n)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1,n-1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))(n-1-\ell_{i}(I))}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1+q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}}{q^{n(n-1)-2(2n-2)}-1}

(9.3.5)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{1\in I,n-1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=1}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))(n-1-\ell_{i}(I))}-1\right)^{-1}.

As a consequence, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:Asymp:SVdagger}\operatorname{SV}_{\dagger}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}_{\dagger}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}}}{q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}}-1}C_{\dagger}(n)\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.3.6)

By
Eqs.

9.3.2
,
9.3.3
,
9.3.4
,
9.3.5
, and
9.3.6
, we have proved
Theorem

9.2
.
Moreover, by
Eq.

A.3.3
, we have the following explicit formulas:

\displaystyle\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{[2(n-1)]!!(z)\cdot[n](z)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}[\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)]!(z)},

\displaystyle q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{n(n-1)}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}q^{\binom{\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)}{2}}}.

See
Lemmas

3.2.5
,
A.1.2
, and
A.2.1
for the definitions of the symbols
[\>\cdot\>]
,
[\>\cdot\>]!
, and
[2\>\cdot\>]!!
.

9.4
Asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let

We will separate the discussion into the following six cases:

\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq I

\left\lvert\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I\right\rvert=1

\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset

1\in I

(i)

(ii)

(iii)

1\notin I

(iv)

(v)

(vi)

(i)
.

Suppose
1\in I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq I
.
By
