4.6.1
, we only need to consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)
.
By
Eqs.

4.6.3
and
4.6.8
, we have

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to above summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
.
Then we have

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-1-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})r}.

Since
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
, it has order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.

(ii)
.

Suppose
1\in I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I
is a singleton.
By
Fig.

4.6.1
, we only need to consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)
.
By
Eqs.

4.6.3
and
4.6.8
, we have

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}c_{t}}.

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to above summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t-1)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
.
Then we have

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

Since
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
, it has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
0
.

(iii)
.

Suppose
1\in I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
By
Fig.

4.6.2
, we only need to consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)
.
By
Eqs.

4.6.3
,
4.6.8
, and
4.6.9
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-2}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}(c_{t-1}+c_{t})},

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}}\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to the first summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t-2)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t-1,t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
.
Then we have

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-2}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

In particular, it has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
1
.
We then know that
\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
also has the same order and degree.
Since
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
,
we see that
\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01})(I)}\left(r\right)
has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
1
.

(iv)
.

Suppose
1\notin I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq I
.
By
Fig.

4.6.3
, we only need to consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)
.
By
Eqs.

4.6.3
,
4.6.8
, and
4.6.10
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{(2n-2)c_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-2)}\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to the first summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=2n-2,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}).

\displaystyle\mathllap{(2\leqslant i\leqslant t)}

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\subseteq\left\{1,t\right\}
with

\mu_{\max}=\max\left\{2n-2,\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})\right\}.

Depending on
n
and
\ell_{t}
, there are three possibilities.

If
2n-2>\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{1\right\}
,
\mu_{\max}=2n-2
, and

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t}\left(q^{(2n-2)-\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{(2n-2)r}.

Then we can deduce that
\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10})(I)}\left(r\right)
has order
2n-2
and degree
0
.

If
2n-2<\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
,
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
, and

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})-(2n-2)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-1-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})r}.

Then we can deduce that
\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10})(I)}\left(r\right)
has order
\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
and degree
0
.

If
2n-2=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t})
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{1,t\right\}
and

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})(2n-1-\ell_{t}-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{(2n-2)r}.

Then we can deduce that
\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10})(I)}\left(r\right)
has order
2n-2
and degree
1
.

(v)
.

Suppose
1\notin I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I
is a singleton.
By
Fig.

4.6.3
, we only need to consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)
.
By
Eqs.

4.6.3
,
4.6.8
, and
4.6.10
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{(2n-2)c_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t-1}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}c_{t}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-2)}\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to the first summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=2n-2,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(2\leqslant i\leqslant t-1)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
t\in\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\subseteq\left\{1,t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
.

Depending on
n
, there are two possibilities.

If
n=4
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{1,t\right\}
and

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{6r}.

Then we can deduce that
\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10})(I)}\left(r\right)
has order
6
and degree
1
.

If
n\geqslant 5
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t\right\}
and

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\left(q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

Then we can deduce that
\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10})(I)}\left(r\right)
has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
0
.

(vi)
.

Suppose
1\notin I
and
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
By
Fig.

4.6.4
, we have to consider all the sets
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)
, and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(I\right)
.
By
Eqs.

4.6.3
,
4.6.8
,
4.6.9
,
4.6.10
, and
4.6.11
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}{\\
}c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r}}q^{(2n-2)c_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t-2}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}(c_{t-1}+c_{t})},

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}}\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-2)}\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-2)-\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}}\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\asymp\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.2
to the first summation, where the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=2n-2,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(2\leqslant i\leqslant t-2)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\left\{t-1,t\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\subseteq\left\{1,t-1,t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
.

Depending on
n
, there are two possibilities.

If
n=4
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{1,t-1,t\right\}
and

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}.

Then we can deduce that
\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11})(I)}\left(r\right)
has order
6
and degree
2
.

If
n\geqslant 5
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t-1,t\right\}
and

\operatorname{S}^{\asymp}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\left(q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

Then we can deduce that
\operatorname{S}_{(\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11})(I)}\left(r\right)
has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
1
.

9.5
Dominant types for
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

We are going to estimate the asymptotic growth of each
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and figure out the
dominant
types, namely the types for which
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
is dominant.

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
Depending on
I
, the set
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)
is contained in various sets
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\cup}\left(I\right)
, where

\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\cup}\left(I\right)=\begin{cases}\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)&if $1\in I$ and $\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I\neq\emptyset$,\\
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)&if $1\in I$ and $\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset$,\\
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)&if $1\notin I$ and $\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I\neq\emptyset$,\\
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)\cup\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(I\right)&if $1\notin I$ and $\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset$.\end{cases}

Refer to
Figs.

4.6.1
,
4.6.2
,
4.6.3
, and
4.6.4
.
Then we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\cup}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\gg\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\gg\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

We summarize
Section

9.4
as follows.

\left\{n-1,n\right\}\subseteq I

\left\lvert\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I\right\rvert=1

\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset

1\in I

\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t}),0\right)

\left(\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},0\right)

\left(\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},1\right)

1\notin I

\left(2n-2,0\right)

\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{t}(2n-1-\ell_{t}),0\right)

\left(2n-2,1\right)

\left(6,1\right)

\left(\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},0\right)

\left(6,2\right)

\left(\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},1\right)

In the table, the pair in each cell tells us the possible order and degree of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\cup}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.

Comparing this table with the discussion in
Section

9.2
, we see the followings.

1.

When
n=4
, a type
I
is dominant if and only if
\left\{1,n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
. In that case,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
has order
6
and degree
2
.

2.

When
n\geqslant 5
, a type
I
is dominant if and only if
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
. In that case,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
has order
\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
and degree
1
.

9.6
Asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to compute the asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{1}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
when
I
is dominant.
To do this, we pick an arbitrary
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)
and investigate the difference between
2\rho(x)
and the sum of
\lceil a(x)\rceil
for
a(x)>0
.
To better describe these sums, we follow
Conventions

7.5.1
,
7.5.2
, and
7.5.3
.

By
Section

9.5
, a necessary condition for
I
being dominant is
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
We will assume that
I
satisfies this condition.
Depending on
1\in I
or not, we will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)
and
(ii)
.

(i)
.

Suppose
1\in I
.
By
Fig.

4.6.2
, we only need to consider
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)
and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)
.
Let
\heartsuit
be either
0
or
1
. Suppose

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=o+c_{1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell_{1}}+\cdots+c_{t-2}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell_{t-2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+(c_{t-1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit)\cdot\omega_{n-1}+(c_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit)\cdot\omega_{n}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right).

By
Eq.

4.6.1
, we have

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}-\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right),

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}+\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right)+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})}+\cdots+c_{t}-\heartsuit,

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}-\chi_{n})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant j\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t-2}\right)+c_{t-1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit,

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}+\chi_{n})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant j\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t-2}\right)+c_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n}\left(\lceil(\chi_{j}-\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)\rceil+\lceil(\chi_{j}+\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)\rceil\right)

\displaystyle=2\rho(x)+\sum_{1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n-1}\overline{c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\overline{c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t-2}-\heartsuit}.

From above analysis, we can define the parity functions
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
(
\heartsuit=0,1
) as follows:

\displaystyle e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1\leqslant i<i^{\prime}\leqslant t-1}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})(\ell_{i^{\prime}}-\ell_{i^{\prime}-1})\overline{c_{i}+\cdots+c_{i^{\prime}-1}}

(9.6.1)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})\overline{c_{i}+\cdots+c_{t-2}-\heartsuit}.

Then we have

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil=2\rho(x)+e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.5
to the following summation (
\heartsuit=0,1
).

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r+\heartsuit}}q^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-2}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}(c_{t-1}+c_{t}-\heartsuit)+e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})}.

Note that the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1\leqslant i\leqslant t-2)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial. The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t-1,t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
.
Therefore, for
\heartsuit=0,1
, we have

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)+\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)(-1)^{r+\heartsuit}\right)\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r},

where the constant
C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
and the function
\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}\to\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{4}\prod_{i=1}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1},

(9.6.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-2}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})s_{i}}.

(9.6.3)

From the definition
Eq.

9.6.1
of
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
, we see that
\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{t})
does not depend on
s_{t-1}
and
s_{t}
.
Therefore, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:AsymptoticOfSXI:l1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.6.4)

(ii)
.

Suppose
1\in I
.
By
Fig.

4.6.4
, we have to consider all the sets
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)
,
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)
, and
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(I\right)
.
Let
\square,\heartsuit
be either
0
or
1
. Suppose

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=o+(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)\cdot\omega_{1}+c_{2}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell_{2}}+\cdots+c_{t-2}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{\ell_{t-2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+(c_{t-1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit)\cdot\omega_{n-1}+(c_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit)\cdot\omega_{n}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right).

By
Eq.

4.6.1
, we have

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}-\chi_{n})(x)

\displaystyle=(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{2}+\cdots+c_{t-2}\right)+c_{t-1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit,

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}+\chi_{n})(x)

\displaystyle=(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{2}+\cdots+c_{t-2}\right)+c_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit,

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}-\chi_{j})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{2}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j)-1}\right),

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}+\chi_{j})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{2}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j)-1}\right)+c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t}-\heartsuit,

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}-\chi_{n})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t-2}\right)+c_{t-1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit,

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}+\chi_{n})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t-2}\right)+c_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\heartsuit,

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}-\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right),

\displaystyle(\chi_{j}+\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)

\displaystyle\mathllap{(1<j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n-1)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}\right)+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})}+\cdots+c_{t}-\heartsuit.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n}\left(\lceil(\chi_{j}-\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)\rceil+\lceil(\chi_{j}+\chi_{j^{\prime}})(x)\rceil\right)

\displaystyle=2\rho(x)+\overline{c_{2}+\cdots+c_{t-2}-\square-\heartsuit}+\sum_{j=2}^{n-1}\overline{c_{2}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j)-1}-\square}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=2}^{n-1}\overline{c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{t-2}-\heartsuit}+\sum_{1<j<j^{\prime}\leqslant n-1}\overline{c_{\ell^{-1}(j)}+\cdots+c_{\ell^{-1}(j^{\prime})-1}}.

From above analysis, we can define the parity functions
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
as follows:

\displaystyle e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})

\displaystyle:=\overline{c_{2}+\cdots+c_{t-2}-\square-\heartsuit}

(9.6.5)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=2}^{t-1}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})\overline{c_{2}+\cdots+c_{i-1}-\square}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=2}^{t-1}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})\overline{c_{i}+\cdots+c_{t-2}-\heartsuit}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{2\leqslant i<i^{\prime}\leqslant t-1}(\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1})(\ell_{i^{\prime}}-\ell_{i^{\prime}-1})\overline{c_{i}+\cdots+c_{i^{\prime}-1}}.

Then we have

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil=2\rho(x)+e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t}).

Now, we apply
Lemma

5.5.5
to the following summation (
\square,\heartsuit=0,1
).

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\sum_{\crampedsubstack{c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\\
c_{1}+\cdots+c_{t}=r+\heartsuit}}q^{(2n-2)(c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\square)\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t-2}\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i})c_{i}+\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}(c_{t-1}+c_{t}-\heartsuit)+e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(c_{1},\cdots,c_{t})}.

Note that the index set
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}
is
\left\{1,\cdots,t\right\}
and the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=2n-2,

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\ell_{i}(2n-1-\ell_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathllap{(2\leqslant i\leqslant t-2)}

\displaystyle\mu_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{t}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}.

Since all members of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are integers,
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial. The knowledge of quadratic function shows that
\left\{t-1,t\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}\subseteq\left\{1,t-1,t\right\}
with
\mu_{\max}=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}
.

Depending on
n
, there are two possibilities.

If
n=4
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{1,t-1,t\right\}
and (
\square,\heartsuit=0,1
)

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)+\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)(-1)^{r+\heartsuit}\right)\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r},

where the constant
C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
and the function
\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}\to\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{8}q^{-3\cdot\square}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1},

(9.6.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t-2}\tfrac{1}{2}(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})s_{i}}.

(9.6.7)

From the definition
Eq.

9.6.5
of
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
, we see that
\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{t})
does not depend on
s_{1}
,
s_{t-1}
, and
s_{t}
.
Therefore, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:D4:AsymptoticOfSXI:e1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}.

(9.6.8)

If
n\geqslant 5
, then we have
\operatorname{\mathfrak{i}}_{\max}=\left\{t-1,t\right\}
and (
\square,\heartsuit=0,1
)

\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)+\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}(-1)^{\mathbf{1}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)(-1)^{r+\heartsuit}\right)\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r},

where the constant
C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
and the function
\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}\to\operatorname{\mathbb{Q}}\left(q;-\right)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{4}\frac{q^{-\tfrac{1}{2}(2n-2)\cdot\square}}{q^{n(n-1)-2(2n-2)}-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1},

(9.6.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle:=q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})+\left(\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)\right)s_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t-2}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})s_{i}}.

(9.6.10)

From the definition
Eq.

9.6.5
of
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
, we see that
\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{t})
does not depend on
s_{t-1}
and
s_{t}
.
Therefore, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:AsymptoticOfSXI:e1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim C_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}\cdot\left(\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{t}}\operatorname{E}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})\right)\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.6.11)

9.7
Asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

Now, let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
We are going to analyze
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.

Suppose
x\in\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right)
, where
I\cap J=\emptyset
.
Since
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\emptyset}=\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}
, by
Lemma

4.6.11
, we can write
x
as

x_{0}-\sum\limits_{j\in J}\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}
,
where
x_{0}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I,r+\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)\right)
.
Then we have

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\lceil a(x)\rceil=2\rho(x_{0})+\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-\sum\limits_{j\in J}a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j})\right\rceil.

Note that the last summation gives an integral constant.
Then we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:SXJIfromSVdaggerI}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=q^{\sum\limits_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-\sum\limits_{j\in J}a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j})\right\rceil}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}_{\dagger}\left(I\right)}\left(r+\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)\right).

(9.7.1)

In particular, each
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
Since
\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I,r\right)
is a disjointed union of various
\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{J}\left(I,r\right)
, we see that
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}
can be defined by a primary super
q
-exponential polynomial.
Then by
Eqs.

3.4.5
and
3.4.6
, we see that
\operatorname{SV}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
and
\operatorname{SSA}\left(\>\cdot\>\right)
can be defined by primary super
q
-exponential polynomials.

Now we assume that
I
is dominant.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

n=4
and
(ii)

n\geqslant 5
.

(i)
.

Suppose
n=4
.
Then we have
\left\{1,3,4\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
The following
J
appears in
Fig.

4.6.4
:

\left\{1\right\}
,
\left\{1,2\right\}
,
\left\{3,4\right\}
,
\left\{2,3,4\right\}
.
In those cases, by
Eq.

4.6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{1\right\}\right\rvert-\delta(\left\{1\right\})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1})\right\rceil

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{1,2\right\}\right\rvert-\delta(\left\{1,2\right\})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{2})\right\rceil

\displaystyle=-5,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{3,4\right\}\right\rvert-\delta(\left\{3,4\right\})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{3}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{4})\right\rceil

\displaystyle=-3,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{2,3,4\right\}\right\rvert-\delta(\left\{2,3,4\right\})

\displaystyle=2,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{3}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{4})\right\rceil

\displaystyle=-8.

Then by
Eqs.

9.1.2
and
9.7.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r},

(9.7.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1,2\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{6-5}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r},

(9.7.3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{3,4\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{6-3}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r},

(9.7.4)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{2,3,4\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{12-8}\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(4-\ell_{i})(3-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}.

(9.7.5)

(ii)
.

Suppose
n\geqslant 5
. Then we have
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
Depending on
\ell_{1}
, there are two cases:
(ii-a)

\ell_{1}>1
and
(ii-b)

\ell_{1}=1
.

(ii-a)
.

If
\ell_{1}>1
, then we consider
Fig.

4.6.2
and the following
J
:
\left\{2,3\right\}
,
\cdots
,
\left\{n-3,n-2\right\}
,
\left\{n-1,n\right\}
, and
\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}
.
When
J=\left\{j,j+1\right\}
, where
2\leqslant j\leqslant n-3
, we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=2
and by
Eq.

4.6.1
,

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j+1})\right\rceil=-j(2n-j).

Then by
Eqs.

9.1.1
and
9.7.1
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:AsymptoticOfSXJI:l1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{j,j+1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=q^{n(n-1)-j(2n-j)}\prod_{i=1}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.7.6)

When
J=\left\{n-1,n\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=1
and by
Eq.

4.6.1
,

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n-1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n})\right\rceil=-\tfrac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}.

Then by
Eqs.

9.1.1
and
9.7.1
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:AsymptoticOfSXJI:l1:nn}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-\tfrac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.7.7)

When
J=\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=2
and by
Eq.

4.6.1
,

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n-2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n-1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n})\right\rceil=-(n-1)^{2}.

Then by
Eqs.

9.1.1
and
9.7.1
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:AsymptoticOfSXJI:l1:nnn}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=q^{n(n-1)-(n-1)^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.7.8)

(ii-b)
.

If
\ell_{1}=1
, then we consider
Fig.

4.6.4
and the following
J
:
\left\{1\right\}
,
\left\{1,2\right\}
,
\left\{2,3\right\}
,
\cdots
,
\left\{n-3,n-2\right\}
,
\left\{n-1,n\right\}
, and
\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}
.
When
J=\left\{1\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=0
and by
Eq.

4.6.1
,

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1})\right\rceil=0.

Then by
Eqs.

9.1.3
and
9.7.1
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:AsymptoticOfSXJI:e1:1}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)=\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{1+q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}}{q^{n(n-1)-2(2n-2)}-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.7.9)

When
J=\left\{1,2\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=1
and by
Eq.

4.6.1
,

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{2})\right\rceil=-(2n-3).

Then by
Eqs.

9.1.3
and
9.7.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-3)}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}

(9.7.10)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\frac{1+q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}}{q^{n(n-1)-2(2n-2)}-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

When
J=\left\{j,j+1\right\}
, where
2\leqslant j\leqslant n-3
, we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=2
and by
Eq.

4.6.1
,

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{j+1})\right\rceil=-j(2n-j).

Then by
Eqs.

9.1.3
and
9.7.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{j,j+1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{n(n-1)-j(2n-j)}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}

(9.7.11)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\frac{1+q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}}{q^{n(n-1)-2(2n-2)}-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

When
J=\left\{n-1,n\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=1
and by
Eq.

4.6.1
,

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n-1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n})\right\rceil=-\tfrac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}.

Then by
Eqs.

9.1.3
and
9.7.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-\tfrac{(n-1)(n-2)}{2}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}

(9.7.12)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\frac{1+q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}}{q^{n(n-1)-2(2n-2)}-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

When
J=\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert J\right\rvert-\delta(J)=2
and by
Eq.

4.6.1
,

\sum_{a\in\Phi^{+}}\left\lceil-a(\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n-2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n-1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{n})\right\rceil=-(n-1)^{2}.

Then by
Eqs.

9.1.3
and
9.7.1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=q^{n(n-1)-(n-1)^{2}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}

(9.7.13)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\frac{1+q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}}{q^{n(n-1)-2(2n-2)}-1}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

9.8
Asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

We are now able to compute the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
when
I
is dominant.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

n=4
and
(ii)

n\geqslant 5
.

(i)
.

Suppose
n=4
.
Then the dominant types are
\left\{2\right\}
and
\emptyset
.
By
Fig.

4.6.4
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{3,4\right\}}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right),

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1,2\right\}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{3,4\right\}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{2,3,4\right\}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right).

Therefore, by
Eqs.

9.6.5
,
9.6.6
,
9.6.7
,
9.6.8
,
9.7.2
,
9.7.3
,
9.7.4
, and
9.7.5
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\left\{2\right\}\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(1+q^{3}+1+q-1-q^{3}\right)\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}=\left(q+1\right)\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r},

(9.8.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(\emptyset\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left((1+q^{5})+(q^{3}+q^{4})+(1+q)+(1+q^{2})\right.

(9.8.2)

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-1-q-q^{3}-q^{4}\right)\left(q^{2}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{5}+q^{2}+q+1}{q^{2}-1}\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}.

(ii)
.

Now, we assume
n\geqslant 5
.
Then
I
is dominant exactly when
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
Depending on
\ell_{1}
, there are two cases:
(ii-a)

\ell_{1}>1
and
(ii-b)

\ell_{1}=1
.

(ii-a)
.

When
\ell_{1}>1
, by
Fig.

4.6.2
, we have (including the zero summations)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{j=2}^{n-3}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{j,j+1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Therefore, by
Eqs.

9.6.2
,
9.6.3
,
9.6.4
,
9.7.6
,
9.7.7
, and
9.7.8
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\prod_{i=1}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot C_{I}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r},

(9.8.3)

where the constant
C_{I}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\heartsuit=0,1}\sum_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{t-2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}}q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{t-2},0,0)+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-2}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})s_{i}}

(9.8.4)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{\crampedsubstack{2\leqslant j\leqslant n-3{\\
}\left\{j,j+1\right\}\cap I=\emptyset}}q^{(n-j)^{2}-n}-(1+\delta_{I}(n-2))q^{n-1},

where
\delta_{I}(i)=0
if
i\in I
and
1
is not.
Note that the definition of the multivariable parity function
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{0\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
is in
Eq.

9.6.1
.

(ii-b)
.

When
\ell_{1}=1
, by
Fig.

4.6.4
, we have (including the zero summations)

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{00}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{01}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{10}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)+\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{11}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{1,2\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)-\sum_{j=2}^{n-3}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{j,j+1\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-1,n\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)-\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}_{\left\{n-2,n-1,n\right\}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

Therefore, by
Eqs.

9.6.9
,
9.6.10
,
9.6.11
,
9.7.9
,
9.7.10
,
9.7.11
,
9.7.12
, and
9.7.13
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(q^{(n-4)(n-1)}-1\right)^{-1}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot C_{I}\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r},

(9.8.5)

where the constant
C_{I}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\square,\heartsuit=0,1}\sum_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{t-2}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}}q^{e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}(\mathbf{s})-(n-1)\cdot\square+\tfrac{(n-4)(n-1)}{2}s_{1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{t-2}\tfrac{1}{2}(n-\ell_{i})(n-1-\ell_{i})s_{i}}

(9.8.6)

\displaystyle\qquad-\Bigg(1+\delta_{I}(2)q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-3)}+\sum_{\crampedsubstack{2\leqslant j\leqslant n-3{\\
}\left\{j,j+1\right\}\cap I=\emptyset}}q^{(n-j)^{2}-n}

\displaystyle\qquad+(1+\delta_{I}(n-2))q^{n-1}\Bigg)\cdot\left(1+q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}-(2n-2)}\right),

Note that the definition of the multivariable parity function
e_{\operatorname{\mathcal{X}}^{\square\heartsuit}\left(I\right)}
is in
Eq.

9.6.5
.

9.9
Asymptotic growths of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
and
\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)

We are now able to obtain the asymptotic growth of
\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)
.
By
Eq.

3.4.6
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:SSA=SumSI:Dn}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)=\sum_{I\subseteq\Delta}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)\sim\sum_{I\text{ is dominant}}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}}\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right).

(9.9.1)

What remains is to plug in the asymptotic growth of dominant
\operatorname{S}_{\operatorname{\mathcal{V}}\left(I\right)}\left(r\right)
.
We will separate the discussion into two cases:
(i)

n=4
and
(ii)

n\geqslant 5
.

(i)
.

When
n=4
, the only dominant types are
\left\{2\right\}
and
\emptyset
.
Then by
Eqs.

9.8.1
,
9.8.2
, and
A.3.3
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:D4:Asymp:SSA}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\sim C(4)\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r},

(9.9.2)

where the constant
C(4)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle C(4)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{4};\left\{2\right\}}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{4};\left\{2\right\}}\right)}}(q+1)+\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{4};\emptyset}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{4};\emptyset}\right)}}\frac{q^{5}+q^{2}+q+1}{q^{2}-1}

(9.9.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)^{2}\left(q+1\right)}{\left(q-1\right)^{3}q^{11}}+\frac{\left(q^{6}-1\right)\left(q^{4}-1\right)^{2}\left(q^{5}+q^{2}+q+1\right)}{\left(q-1\right)^{4}q^{12}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(q^{2}+q+1\right)\left(q^{2}-q+1\right)^{2}\left(q^{2}+1\right)^{3}(q+1)^{4}}{(q-1)q^{12}}.

As a consequence, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:D4:Asymp:SV}\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{6}}{q^{6}-1}C(4)\cdot\binom{r}{2}q^{6r}.

(9.9.4)

(ii)
.

Now, we assume
n\geqslant 5
. Then
I
is dominant exactly when
\left\{n-1,n\right\}\cap I=\emptyset
.
By
Eqs.

9.8.3
and
9.8.5
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:Asymp:SSA}\operatorname{SSA}\left(r\right)\sim C(n)\cdot rq^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r},

(9.9.5)

where the constant
C(n)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle C(n)(r)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1,n-1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=2}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))(n-1-\ell_{i}(I))}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot\frac{C_{I}}{q^{(n-4)(n-1)}-1}

(9.9.6)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{1\in I,n-1,n\notin I}\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)}{q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}}\prod_{i=1}^{t-2}\left(q^{(n-\ell_{i}(I))(n-1-\ell_{i}(I))}-1\right)^{-1}\cdot C_{I}.

As a consequence, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Dn:Asymp:SV}\operatorname{SV}\left(r\right)=\sum_{z=0}^{r}\operatorname{SSA}\left(z\right)\sim\frac{q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}}}{q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}}-1}C(n)\cdot q^{\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}r}.

(9.9.7)

.

Note that the constant
C_{I}
depends on
I
.
When
1\in I
and
n-1,n\notin I
, it is defined in
Eq.

9.8.4
.
When
1,n-1,n\notin I
, it is defined in
Eq.

9.8.6
.

By
Eqs.

9.9.2
,
9.9.3
,
9.9.4
,
9.9.5
,
9.9.6
, and
9.9.7

we have proved
Theorem

9.1
.
Moreover, by
Eq.

A.3.3
, we have the following explicit formulas:

\displaystyle\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\left(q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{[2(n-1)]!!(z)\cdot[n](z)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}[\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)]!(z)},

\displaystyle q^{\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n};I}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{n(n-1)}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}q^{\binom{\ell_{i}(I)-\ell_{i-1}(I)}{2}}}.

(9.9.8)

See
Lemmas

3.2.5
,
A.1.2
, and
A.2.1
for the definitions of the symbols
[\>\cdot\>]
,
[\>\cdot\>]!
, and
[2\>\cdot\>]!!
.

Appendix A
Poincaré polynomials of irreducible root systems

In this appendix, we will work out a closed formula for the Poincaré polynomial
\operatorname{{P}}_{X_{n};I}
of each irreducible reduced root system
\Phi
of type
X_{n}
and each type
I
.

A.1
Poincaré polynomials of
A_{n}

First, it is clear that
\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{0}}\left(z\right)=\operatorname{{P}}_{\emptyset}\left(z\right)=1
.
We then assume that
n\geqslant 1
.

Let
\Phi
be a root system of type
A_{n}
.
Then the Dynkin diagram with the label of simple roots in
\Phi
is the following one:

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{2}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n}}

By
[
3
]
*chap.VI, §4, no.7,
the degrees of its Weyl group are
d_{i}=i+1
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

3.2.5
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:PoincareAn}\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n}}\left(z\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[i+1](z).

(A.1.1)

In particular,
\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n}}\right)=\binom{n+1}{2}
.
Note that
[1](z)
is the constant
1
.
Hence,
\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n}}\left(z\right)
equals to the following

z
-factorial
polynomial:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:quantum_{f}actorial}[n+1]!(z):=\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}[i](z).

(A.1.2)

We also need the following
z
-multinomial
polynomial:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:quantum_{m}ultinomial}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{n}{n_0,\cdots,n_k}(z):=\frac{[n]!(z)}{[n_{0}]!(z)\cdots[n_{k}]!(z)},

(A.1.3)

where
n_{0}+\cdots+n_{k}=n
is a partition of
n
into natural numbers.

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
Then the Dynkin diagram of the subsystem
\Phi_{I}
with labels is the following one:

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n}}

Hence,
\Phi_{I}
is of type

A_{\ell_{1}-\ell_{0}-1}\times\cdots\times A_{\ell_{t+1}-\ell_{t}-1}
, where
\ell_{t+1}
is defined to be
n+1
.
Note that
(\ell_{1}-\ell_{0})+\cdots+(\ell_{t+1}-\ell_{t})=n+1
.
Then we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:PoincareAnI}\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n},I}\left(z\right)=\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n}}\left(z\right)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t+1}\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}-1}}\left(z\right)}=\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{n+1}{\ell_{1}-\ell_{0},\cdots,\ell_{t+1}-\ell_{t}}(z).

(A.1.4)

In particular,
\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n},I}\right)=\binom{n+1}{2}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t+1}\binom{\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}}{2}
.

A.2
Poincaré polynomials of
B_{n}
and
C_{n}

First note that the two types of root systems share the same Weyl group, hence the same Poincaré polynomial.
It suffices to only consider one of them.
We will consider
C_{n}
.

Let
\Phi
be a root system of type
C_{n}
.
Then the Dynkin diagram with the label of simple roots in
\Phi
is the following one:

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{2}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-2}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n}}

When
n=0
or
1
, we can see that
C_{n}=A_{n}
.
We then assume that
n\geqslant 2
.
By
[
3
]
*chap.VI, §4, no.5 and no.6,
the degrees of its Weyl group are
d_{i}=2i
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

3.2.5
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:PoincareCn}\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n}}\left(z\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[2i](z).

(A.2.1)

In particular,
\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n}}\right)=n^{2}
.
We use
[2n]!!(z)
to denote the right-hand side and use the convention that
[0]!!=1
.

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
Then the Dynkin diagram of the subsystem
\Phi_{I}
with labels is one of the following three: (focusing on position of
\ell_{t}
)

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n}}

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-2}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n}}

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{i}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n}}

In either case,
\Phi_{I}
is of type

A_{\ell_{1}-\ell_{0}-1}\times\cdots\times A_{\ell_{t}-\ell_{t-1}-1}\times C_{n-\ell_{t}}

(notice that
C_{0}=A_{0}
and
C_{1}=A_{1}
).
Then we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n},I}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n}}\left(z\right)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}-1}}\left(z\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n-\ell_{t}}}\left(z\right)}

(A.2.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{[2n]!!(z)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}[\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}]!(z)\cdot[2(n-\ell_{t})]!!(z)}.

In particular,

\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{C_{n},I}\right)=n^{2}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\binom{\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}}{2}-(n-\ell_{t})^{2}
.

A.3
Poincaré polynomials of
D_{n}

Let
\Phi
be a root system of type
D_{n}
.
Then the Dynkin diagram with the label of simple roots in
\Phi
is the following one:

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{2}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-3}}
a_{n-2}
a_{n-1}
a_{n}

When
n=0
,
1
, or
3
, we can see that
D_{n}=A_{n}
.
When
n=2
, we have
D_{2}=A_{1}\times A_{1}
.
We then assume that
n\geqslant 4
.

[
3
]
*chap.VI, §4, no.8,
the degrees of
W
are
d_{i}=2i
for
i<n
and
d_{n}=n
.
Therefore, by
Lemma

3.2.5
, we have

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:PoincareDn}\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n}}\left(z\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}[2i](z)\cdot[n](z)=[2(n-1)]!!(z)\cdot[n](z).

(A.3.1)

In particular,
\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n}}\right)=n(n-1)
.

Let
I
be a type and follow
Convention

2.3.2
.
When
\ell_{t}<n-2
, the Dynkin diagram of the subsystem
\Phi_{I}
with labels is one of the followings: (focusing on position of
\ell_{t}
)

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t^{\prime}}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-3}}
a_{n-2}
a_{n-1}
a_{n}

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t^{\prime}}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-3}}
a_{n-2}
a_{n-1}
a_{n}

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t^{\prime}}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-3}}
a_{n-2}
a_{n-1}
a_{n}

a_{\mathrlap{1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{1}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t^{\prime}}-1}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t}}}
a_{\mathrlap{\ell_{t}+1}}
a_{\mathrlap{n-3}}
a_{n-2}
a_{n-1}
a_{n}

In the first two cases,
\Phi_{I}
is of the type

A_{\ell_{1}-\ell_{0}-1}\times\cdots\times A_{\ell_{t}-\ell_{t-1}-1}\times D_{n-\ell_{t}}
(Noticing that is
D_{n-\ell_{t}}
if
n-\ell_{t}<4
).
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n},I}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n}}\left(z\right)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}-1}}\left(z\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n-\ell_{t}}}\left(z\right)}

(A.3.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{[2(n-1)]!!(z)\cdot[n](z)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t}[\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}]!(z)\cdot[2(n-\ell_{t}-1)]!!(z)\cdot[n-\ell_{t}](z)}.

In particular,

\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n},I}\right)=n(n-1)-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}\binom{\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}}{2}-(n-\ell_{t})(n-\ell_{t}-1)
.

In the last two cases,
\Phi_{I}
is of the type

A_{\ell_{1}-\ell_{0}-1}\times\cdots\times A_{\ell_{t-1}-\ell_{t-2}-1}\times A_{n-\ell_{t-1}-1}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n},I}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n}}\left(z\right)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}-1}}\left(z\right)^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{{P}}_{A_{n-\ell_{t-1}-1}}\left(z\right)}

(A.3.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{[2(n-1)]!!(z)\cdot[n](z)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}[\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}]!(z)\cdot[n-\ell_{t-1}](z)}.

In particular,

\operatorname{deg}\left(\operatorname{{P}}_{D_{n},I}\right)=n(n-1)-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}\binom{\ell_{i}-\ell_{i-1}}{2}-\binom{n-\ell_{t-1}}{2}
.
